**Guardrails honoured, in one place, so that a later writer does not have to re-derive them.** **The word *Gatehouse* is not spelled out anywhere in this batch and is not referred to as a word already written, which is a deliberate tightening of the Chapter 57 habit and is recorded below as a deviation.** *Culling Order* and *custodian* do not appear. **The private-keeper clause is not mentioned, referred to or explained by anybody**; Chapter 69 puts the shape of a promise about a number of persons in a place that cannot be asked a question on the page in a notebook, and the man writing it has deliberately decided **not** to work the rest of it out, in writing, and has entered that as a decision and not an oversight — which is the volume's protection, not its resolution. **No Bind is put into anything and the word is not used; the one sentence about it is "a Bind does not travel and a motion does", stated plainly in Chapter 65, and he puts nothing into any material in this county.** **No System display.** **No crossing, no gate, no stage, no revelation, no authority to speak for the county** — the block's only four words in a hat are a refusal to be one. **The tile on the coping is shown twice at close range (Chapters 65 and 69), recognised, handled wrongly on purpose, and explained by nobody, including on the last page**; in Chapter 65 the woman of the Ninebridge asks him what it is and he lies, and in Chapter 69 he tells her it is a good tile and that it should stay on the coping, and neither of them says a word about the mark. **Nobody dies in this batch; there is no death arranged, referred to as an effect, or used as a turn.** The only dead people named are the third signatory of the county law, who has been dead since the eighth of the last rolling, and a man called Ostrand, who is not alive, and neither death does any work. **No Ash figure is summed with a Bellweather or a Merefen figure anywhere**, and the three Ash quantities are added together once, in a kiln shed, in Chapter 61, and nowhere else.

**What the batch changed.** A county of nine hundred and forty-one hearths and two thousand four hundred and fifty people has fired two thousand nine hundred and six tiles on its roll of persons, **because a county counts fires and does not count itself**, and a man with a ledger and a thumb black to the second joint says the second number out loud — **because somebody asked, in a room, for the first time in nineteen years** — and then says the difference, **four hundred and fifty-six**, and stops. **Aren works it out in a minute and a half and says it out loud and a room of nine people stops what it is doing**, and the number is not explained for forty pages. **A tile in this county is the account of a person that does not need them, and it has a person's own hand pressed into the back of it, and a soft tile can be re-pressed by anybody with a thumb and a wet cloth** — which is why a woman was destroyed in a winter by her own household doing an ordinary repair to a shelf, and why the fourth hundred and fifty-six is a floor of a surplus that nobody will give a figure for. **The law is four lines with three signatures, the third of them nine months old and its signer dead**, and it permits a list of two hundred and six names to be burned in a market square, and the instrument's cost is the destruction of two hundred and six fired tiles, **and the magistrate offering it is a decent man with a column of figures that goes nine, eleven, nine, thirteen, fifteen, seventeen, twenty-one, twenty-four and never turns.** He asks Aren for his opinion and **Aren has nothing, and the reason he has nothing is that the magistrate is right about the fever**, and that is the moment the volume stops being about a man learning a lesson and becomes about a method that works. **The physician's finding is read out loud in a room with a table for the first time in four years** and it is a medical finding and not a moral sentence: the softening enters by the mark and travels fastest along the marks that are read most, the marks that are read most are the roll marks, **and therefore the cure is not fewer records but records that do not agree with each other** — three copies, three hands, three places, none authoritative alone, restored when two agree, removed when two agree otherwise. **A man says the sentence the book is built on and everybody in the room knows it and nobody in the county has ever had a column for it: a person who is struck has to be able to answer for himself, and if he answers he goes back on it, and if he cannot answer then he was never on it and we have been keeping a list of things** — and a form requires a column, and the column is *struck*, and there is no column in the world for *answered*, and somebody ruled five spaces ninety-four years ago and stopped. **Aren refuses to give advice, refuses to be a voice, and asks the county for seven days**, which is the first time in the book he has asked a community for time instead of taking it, and it is granted for two reasons that are wages and one that is a column that does not turn, and the list is not burned at the sittings because **the clerk of the roll said no in the magistrate's own room, for a reason about a wedging shed and about the roll she would like still to exist in eleven years**, which is the first refusal in this county that works and it is not a moral sentence. **Ash writes five terms for a road on a tile and in a book, in a hand nobody sent for, and term four is a person who is struck may answer, and term four is a room with a book in it and two hundred people to go through it, and a man who can see a load path writes the whole of it down in a notebook four months before he builds it, in the correct technical vocabulary, in a document's load expressed in tons because he does not know what a person weighs, and does not act on it, and the reason he does not act on it is that he has written the mistake down before and read it and gone on, and the only new thing in the book that day is that it is the same mistake both times and noticing it has never stopped him.** He comes up at the first light of the fifth of Wolfmonth with a fever of three days and a way of standing that is a ferrywright's on a slip and that he cannot source, and **a woman with a day-book over a weigh-house writes down on a page that has had nothing on it for about a month that she has noticed and that she is not going to ask, and gives the reason under it: the not asking has changed from a protection into a thing she is doing to him, and the difference is that a protection is entered in a file and this is entered in a day-book, and she has noticed the difference, and she has noticed it late.**

**The state at the end of the batch.**
- **Aren:** Stage 1, one open route (Bellweather-Living) and one line that Ash has written terms for and not opened. No office. **He has been twenty-three days in the County of Ash and four hundred miles in a kiln county, a shed, an assizes room, a parish at the edge of a village, a market square and a house, and the whole of his work was carrying boards and re-pressing tiles.** He has **a second use for the hand he already had** — a hand setting a thing true against a thing out of true, on the right side of the world for the first time, belonging to a county and not a city, taught by nobody. **He has a notebook entry in the correct technical vocabulary that is the volume's most damning page, and it is four months early, and he does not act on it.** He is **feverish on the fifth, sixth and seventh of Wolfmonth, breaking on the eighth**, and **he stands like a ferrywright on a slip and cannot source it and has not noticed.** He has told Mara Vey every number and the *how long I have been doing this* in the wrong frame is **not** on the page; the path dates stay in Ash's own calendar and the notebook's own note that a clerk in Merefen will not be able to tell which side of the year a line is on is the Chapter 92 plant, and Chapter 92 has not happened.
- **Mara:** keeper, one room over the chandler at the north end, four pence a year, doing two rooms' work. **She has the second answer in her own fair hand on her desk with the hour on it, and she has learned that the man who left it is the man at the kiln in the fourth line of it, and she has entered that on his statement with the hour and has not asked him one question about it, and the not asking is now a wound and not a protection and she has written the day she noticed and it is a year late.** The brine weights are still cited and not filed. The eighth ruled line is still empty and still the only line in four years with nothing against it and no reason on it — and the page it is on now has a third entry on it, and the third is about a man's hands, and no court in this county could be shown that page.
- **Tovan:** the yard is **eleven days** — three days of real work out of the four weeks and a day, then a wall job off the Fallow Elms road taken on the seventh that pays in wood and not money because four men cannot be fed on three days' work. **He has entered the sum in the yard's book with the hour and without his brother's name for the first time in a year, and has said out loud in a room that not one word of the arithmetic is about whether he forgives him.** He is a hand and not a keeper, is not a member of the bench and has not been since he was twenty-one, and **he shut a door in a filing room that he has not shut in four years and the chapter does not say why and does not have to.**
- **The Sedge Reach:** the scour under the nose of the third pier is a washtub and has been for eight weeks. **The bench named a day, the ninth of Embermonth, and had it, and named a second, the twenty-fourth, and had that, and it is eleven days past the second, and a bench that has named a day twice and had it twice is going to name a third, and the third will be taken alone by the guild's man with the levels and Tovan will have to believe it.** Nobody has read the scour.
- **The copying post:** **the county's surveyor has stopped holding it.** A line, dated the third, not a letter, saying the post is not held and a man is to be put in it; no name, no day, no mention of the three Fridays. **The three Fridays is nine shillings in the county's hands since the seventeenth of Fallowmonth and there is no post for it to be in.** Entered in a day-book against no person with the hour, and **not resolved here — it is Chapters 71–80's cost and Chapter 97's line of the bill.**
- **The three hooks, as documents after this batch:** (1) **a four-page finding of four years old, read out loud in a room with a table for the first time, and the two copies of it in a drawer in the assizes and a drawer at the physician's, both of which have been read and neither of which has been acted on**; (2) **a fire tile, six by four, with a hand laid flat on a book on the face, a rope on it in two loops and no yoke, on the coping of the second span at the Ninebridge, taken down for one morning and put back, understood by nobody;** (3) **an index leaf with nine lines, an empty county cell, an empty *returned* cell, an *answered* cell eleven days after the tender, and press four, die eleven, second impression of the season — untouched in this batch and it is where it was, because nothing in a clay county is connected to a creditor's register in a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists, and the reader has to hold the join.**

**Canon the batch invented that Batch 0008 must obey.** Continuity items **173 onward**. The load-bearing ones: **the four hundred and fifty-six is a floor of a surplus and no figure in the volume may be got from it by scaling it;** **a struck person is given a soft tile, the same tile a child carries, and a soft tile can be re-pressed by anybody with a thumb and a wet cloth, which is the mechanism by which a household destroys a person while repairing a shelf;** **a tile is the account of a person that does not need them, and the mark on the back is pressed by the person and is the only part of the county's records that is anybody's at all;** **a person who softens cannot be witnessed by anybody including themselves, and the law requires two witnesses, and so the law removes people by operation and nobody decides it, and the keeper of the roll has never had to be the one and is proud of that;** **a form requires a column and the fifth space is headed *struck* and there is no sixth, and the fifth space is why nobody can get back on the roll;** **the county law's fourth line says *and shall enter the destruction in the roll, and not the name*, and the reason nobody has ever looked at that clause is that a signature is a signature;** **a soft tile pressed too lightly is a tile whose mark no longer agrees and a tile pressed too hard is a tile with a stranger's knuckle on the back where the kiln's mark goes, and both failures take the person off the roll, and both are a stranger's hands;** **the five terms, on a tile and in a book, and term four is a room with a book in it;** **a bound is a promise and a promise about two hundred people to one office is a shape that has been refused before and a witness that is also the anchor is not a witness;** **and he wrote it down and did not act on it.**

**Card and prompt deviations, recorded so the next writer does not inherit them as errors.** (1) **The prompt's day map numbers Chapters 61–69 to the nineteenth day and states twenty-three days and that the twenty-third is the last day on the far side.** Both are honoured and the four days are not missing: **the chapters as carded reach the nineteenth day and Chapter 69 carries the twentieth to the twenty-third in its own tail**, the sittings sitting on the twenty-second and the twenty-third being the last day on the far side, which is also the seventh day of the week he asked for and the day the terms are carried out on the bridge. **A later writer must not re-derive a twenty-three-day path interval as nineteen days of chapters plus four missing days.** (2) **The prompt's Chapter 63 card does not carry a date for the sittings, and Chapter 68 needs one, so the sittings are fixed in Chapter 63 as *the end of this rolling*, with the magistrate saying a fortnight and the kiln-hands saying three, and Aren's request for a week is the same request he throws in Chapter 63 and gets refused.** The two are not the same beat: **in Chapter 63 he throws a week at a magistrate who is not ready and knows before he finishes the sentence that he will not get it, and in Chapter 68 he asks a room for seven days with a price attached and the room grants it**, and that difference is the arc of the two chapters. (3) **The Chapter 69 card says the mistake is written down "four months" before it is made, and the Chapter 81 card places the rite in the sixth month.** Both are kept and the chapter does not do the sum: **the notebook says "in four months, or thereabouts, which is the same thing at this rate, and I have not done the sum because the sum is in two calendars and I have not got both of them, and the one I have got does not have a week in it."** That is the calendar breach planted a second time, in his own hand, and it is Chapter 92's plant, and it protects the figure from being checkable. (4) **The Chapter 6 per-chapter summary in `state/chapter-summaries.md` says the re-pressing comes home "in a kiln shed"; the re-pressing is done at a board in the cutting shed at the north end of the Ninebridge, which belongs to the Ninebridge kiln and is where every soft tile in that part of the county is kept between the pressing and the firing.** The shed is a kiln shed and the chapter says so; no card is broken. (5) **The prompt's guardrail that Chapter 57 refers to *Gatehouse* as a word already written once is not repeated in this batch at all.** The word is not spelled, not referred to, and not quoted, and the only related sentence in the batch is the one about a shape refused in a room in another county. (6) **The prompt's Chapter 70 card says the second answer is on the desk in Mara's fair hand with the hour on it, and it is — and it is her transcription of the four lines, and the hour on it is the hour she entered it, not a date out of Ash, so that the calendar question is not raised here and cannot be spent before Chapter 92.** (7) **Chapter 70 does not mention the morning with Tovan, and may not.** The concealment of the ordinary morning is Chapter 91's, and Chapter 70 conceals the stance instead, which is the third of the three things the man does not tell her, and the block ends on the stance and not the morning.

**What Batch 0008 opens on.**

A yard with eleven days in it and a wall that pays in wood. A copying post the county's surveyor has stopped holding, and three Fridays of nine shillings that there is no post for. A bench that has had a reading twice and is going to name a third, and a scour under the nose of the third pier that is a washtub, and a mark on a ring that is a quarter of an inch low with only one number against it. And a carrier's board on Weir Street, and a man who comes down it off the provincial post with a slow fuse: **Petch's answer to the question nobody should have asked, and Sabin Dain's second letter, which is dated, and which asks for a count of persons rather than names, and which is signed.**

## Batch 0008 — Volume 02, Chapters 71–80, "Ordinary Work in Merefen"

**Landed.** Chapters 71–80 in `chapters/volume-02/chapter-0071.md` to `chapter-0080.md`, four digits, ten complete scenes. POVs **Tovan Kest ×2 (71, 74) / Mara Vey ×2 (72, 76) with Rowan Petch and Marda Oakes inside hers / Aren Kest ×6 (73, 75, 77, 78, 79, 80) with Lysa Fenn in 73.** **38,796 words** by `wc -w` on the chapter files, title lines included, and the per-chapter column foots. Per chapter: **4,789 / 4,011 / 4,504 / 3,748 / 3,563 / 3,944 / 3,545 / 3,254 / 3,931 / 3,507.** **Chapters 78 and 79 are the two to watch in review** — both are short, both are Ash, and Ch 78 carries the batch's only money column and Ch 79 the flat line about registers.

**Cast.** **Four new names of the six the card allows — Gwil Trewhella, 39, a carrier on the western channel; Marda Oakes, 45, of the county town, a member of the county council; Sennet Roil, 28, a clerk in the fourth press of the provincial registry; Ivet Salter, 61, of the third press — and two slots unfilled, as planned.** Everyone else is already on the page or addressed by trade: Pei Tarrow, Bram Ottery, Lysa Fenn, Reeve Aldis Fenn, Sabin Dain (in a document), Halven (in an allusion and not named as the source), the guild's man with the levels, the guild clerk, the bench-clerk, the man at the fish weir, the foundry's man, the man at the drawknife, the boy of fifteen, the boy with the slate, the boy of thirteen, the toll-holder's clerk, the man from the provincial office, the man in the leather apron, the man with the ledger, Pernel Quist, Colm Arreld, Ostry Vaile, Bel. **Hesper Lound was listed here once and does not appear anywhere in Chapters 71–80; he is Batch 0007 and this entry no longer claims him.** **Ilsa Soke is a returned face and is not named in this batch; she is *the hand of the Ninebridge* and *the hand*, and he asks her about the coping and gets the answer he already had.** **The volume total is now thirteen of eighteen** (5 + 4 + 4), and the unfilled slots are 1 + 2 + 2 = **5 of twelve**.

**Guardrails honoured, in one place, so that a later writer does not have to re-derive them.** *Gatehouse* is not spelled out anywhere in the batch; the phrases *Culling Order* and *custodian* do not appear; the word *Bind* does not appear and nothing is put into any material; **the private-keeper clause is not mentioned, referred to or explained by anybody, and the marginal instruction at the foot of the R.C. series is referred to as printed in Chapters 71 and 76 and is not read out, glossed or paraphrased by a single character.** No System display, no gate, no stage, no authority claimed, no revelation. **No melody cost is added and none may be.** **No named supporting character dies, nobody is killed, and no unnamed person is arranged into a death for effect** — the only death spoken of in the batch belongs to the man who put the tile on the coping of the Ninebridge forty-one years ago, who has been dead since the eighth of the last rolling, named in Ch 77 as something that happened before the book, and it does no work. **An earlier version of this line named a man called Ostrand, who is Batch 0007 and appears nowhere in Chapters 71–80, and a great-grandfather, who appears nowhere in the manuscript at all.** **No Ash quantity is printed in Chapters 71–77 and no Ash figure is summed with a Bellweather or a Merefen figure anywhere in the batch; the three quantities 941, 2,450 and 2,906 and the difference of 456 do not appear at all**, and the only Ash figure on the page in the whole batch is **nine hundred and forty-one hearths, in Ch 78 and Ch 80, in Ash, as the county's own figure about itself, never added to anything.** The two eighth ruled lines are in different chapters, in different rooms, in front of different people, and **no character puts them together or refers to the other.** The city is still not on the served schedule. The drift figure is not restated as an error against the path interval anywhere in the batch, and no character in this batch states a drift figure at all.

**What the batch changed.** A yard runs out of work and gets it back and loses it again in four days, and **the four days happen on the page in front of four men with the hour against them**, because a yard that quietly gains four days is a yard that has stopped counting. **The Crown's second instrument is answered three times in three trades in one block and every answer is a fact and not a name**: a carpenter enters *nil* against a figure and says that nil is the only figure in the county that cannot be asked about a person; **a court lets a count expire by discovering in nine minutes that a returnable paper with no party in it expires**, and beats the instrument with a form of the form's own making, and is frightened of her own answer because it is built on the fact that this court is not a person, and in about four years somebody is going to make it one; and **a keeper of records returns eleven to the province with the *Keeper* column left empty and no reason entered**, and the figure contains all four of the eleven who are in a file and names none of them, and it is lawful, and it is not a list, and it is the answer to Edric Hunn's question, and it is made by a woman who has written down three times this year that the not writing is the part that is a decision. **And then, four days after a carpenter wrote nil on the back of a wage sheet, an office writes three lines in a woman's hand and proposes a count of persons for the county as a whole, and nobody in the room sees what it is, and the one person in the book who could have said what it is is twenty-five yards from the door and will not be in the county in three weeks.** In Merefen: **a bench is asked a second time and says yes a second time, and a man in the room, standing, with the level case down on the table, says out loud that a bench asked three times and saying yes three times is not a bench, it is a subscription — anticipatory, about an asking that has not happened, and no character in that room may call it the third.** A reeve uses a casting vote twice in one meeting and the second one puts a stranger in an office, and he is on the roll, and he says the thing the volume ends on: **he accepts the vote and not the office**, because a vote is a thing a town gives a man and an office is a thing a man is held to, and he does not intend to be the man who holds this crossing — and he notices, two seconds late, that a refusal had cost him nothing, because the price was on the man afterwards, and says nothing, because a man who argues in a room that he ought to be disqualified from a vote has asked the room to do his arithmetic. **The count goes to twelve because a man was given a vote, and nobody in the room can prove that is the wrong thing to count, and the reeve enters that the two numbers are not the same count.** A reeve votes on a thing in her own guild for the first time in nine years and then refuses, in the same words as on the ninth of Embermonth, to stand as the town's witness on it, and the minute says *the reeve voted* and the clerk reads it back and nobody asks him to. And on the fourth of Hearthmonth **the road opens**, for the first time in the volume, on a line and two hands and a date and a witness at each end who is not the person keeping the road, and the gate is five lines pressed in wet clay in a county four hundred miles off, and it opens because a county wrote down what it wanted and a town wrote down what it wanted and the two did not have to want the same thing. **Nine months and six days.** In Ash: **the argument's standing is a price and the price is three times the tiles, footed in pence on a board, and nobody says *we are right* or *you are wrong*; the ninety cords are not cut because there is no office that can put forty men in a wood for a day and pay them; two of the four kilns stop, and a charged rolling cannot wait, and the clay goes back into the wedging shed, which is the room the rollkeeper kept a list out of for the correct reason on the correct day, and reasons do not stay correct; about forty men are out, and thirteen do not go back, and six of the thirteen are six lines with nothing against them and no reason on them** — and a struck man, an employed man and a man whose kiln has shut are three different people and the room knows it and does not say so. **The third holding is a creditor's press four hundred miles off, at a farthing of salt, and its price is eleven sheets the county cannot go and fetch, and a woman of sixty-one of a provincial registry's third press writes in a letter that a register answers what was entered on the shelf and nothing else, and that is the whole of it, and she says it as though it were a small thing** — and a clerk of twenty-eight copies a whole leaf including the ruled cells at the bottom, and one of those cells is a county cell that is ruled and empty, and a man four hundred miles off sees it in a shed and writes one line in a notebook and says nothing to anybody. **The bench cannot put Merrow's minute where a Crown's man will find it, because there is no Crown's man in that county and there is nobody there to look, and it enters its own powerlessness in a book in a minute, which is the first time any body in that county has done it in nineteen years.** The rollkeeper does not sign, twice, for two reasons, and one of them is that the third hand is a letter. And in the last notebook entry of the batch **he sets out a working at the scale of two hundred in the correct technical vocabulary, in tons, four months after he wrote the same thing down in the same vocabulary in the same county, and does not say it, and does not act on it, and the line about a sum in two calendars and a week is in his own hand for the third time in the book.**

**The state at the end of the batch.**
- **Aren:** Stage 1, one open route and one line that Ash has written terms for and opened. **On the guild roll of Merefen and elected a road warden of the Merefen bridge guild on the ninth of Frostmonth by eight hands and a reeve's, and has accepted the vote and not the office** — so he is on the roll, has a vote, will have a vote on a question that is not about him, does not hold the crossing, does not vote, and has said in a hall that he will come when he is wanted. **He is four hundred miles off, in a county of nine hundred and forty-one hearths, in his fifth month, with a notebook entry that is the volume's most damning page for the second time and a rite he has not proposed.** A binding agreement made at a bench on the ninth of Embermonth takes him out of the county for as long as the work takes, and only two people in Merefen know it.
- **Tovan Kest:** the yard at **a fortnight** again, from eleven on the fifth of Wolfmonth, with the four days on the page. **He has said the true thing four times in six months and not voted once.** He has paid a witness fourpence a week for four months out of his own box and not improved on the arrangement. He said the sentence about his brother out loud in a yard for the first time, and he said the sentence about Tarrow's day at the gate.
- **Mara Vey:** keeper, one room over a weigh-house at the north end, four pence a year, two rooms' work. **She has returned a figure of eleven to the province on the ninth of Frostmonth with the *Keeper* column empty and no reason entered, and the office's three-line reply dated the thirteenth of Wolfmonth is on the page and in her own day-book, and she has entered that she does not see what it is, and she is the only person in this county who has read both documents.** A woman came up two flights of a stair and did not ask for a name and left a card, and Mara entered the card against a person for the first time in four years, **and has entered, for the third time, that the not writing is the part that is a decision.**
- **Rowan Petch:** a filed return that answers nothing, four facts about a box and a request that the office supply its own purpose in its own hand under its own seal. **And a name in a county file, in the matter of the crossing of Alder Vale, in a woman's own hand, on the fourth of Embermonth, and he told one person and asked for nothing else about her.**
- **Lysa Fenn:** nine years in a moot hall, and **a count of persons that is going to expire on the ninth of Frostmonth with no answer on it**, and a reason entered for that, and a written refusal of a request about an eleven-year-old salvage filing, and a statement in her own book that her answer is built on the fact that this court is not a person and will die when somebody makes it one.
- **The bench of nine:** asked twice, saying yes twice, **short by three shillings and fivepence** — 233 pence of work against 96 in the chest and 96 in notes, and 41 pence wanting, and nothing else is wanting and there never was — four marks three shillings and fivepence of work, four marks and six a year, two marks in a chest, a subscription book in a market, and a minute that ends *The sum is short by three shillings and fivepence.*
- **The Sedge Reach:** the scour under the nose of the third pier is a washtub and has been since the sixth of Mudmonth; **the bill wants a fortnight with the road shut on the twenty-fourth of Greenmonth and the bench minuted in Hearthmonth that it knew about it**; nobody has read the scour.
- **The copying post:** still gone. **The county's surveyor took a man on in Thawmonth** — that is a Batch 0006 and 0007 promise, and it is not paid, and it is Chapter 97's twelve marks and ten pence.

**Canon the batch invented that Batch 0009 must obey.** Continuity items **190 onward**. The load-bearing ones: **a count is a list with the names taken off and it is lawful, and the Archive has now returned one, and the office has answered a count with a bigger count, and the bigger count is a sentence and not an instrument and nobody in the room saw it**; **a returnable paper with no party expires, and a paper with nobody in default is not refused, and a court can beat an instrument with the form's own construction, and a body's liability to be sued is the price of every good answer a keeper of records has ever had**; **nil is a figure and it is the only figure that cannot be asked about a person**; **an answer that is a fact instead of a name is now made independently in at least two countries by two people who have never met, and both of them think there is a third thing that knows**; **a bench asked three times and saying yes three times is not a bench, it is a subscription, and the warning is now on the page in a guild hall in Frostmonth, about an asking that has not happened**; **a reeve has a casting vote and a casting vote is not a vote, and a reeve who uses it twice in one meeting has crossed a line she has held for nine years without noticing**; **a man can accept a vote and refuse an office and both facts are true and both go in the same minute**; **a refused road and an opened road are different books and the opened one is worth less**; **a county's price can be footed in pence on a board and be unanswerable, and the kilns can do a sum in a morning that a magistrate takes a year to reach, and neither of them is lying**; **a charged rolling cannot wait, and the room a clerk protects a list with is the room the kilns will need when a kiln stops**; **a struck man, an employed man and a man out of work are three different people and the county's records can tell the difference for none of them**; **a register answers what was entered on the shelf and nothing else, and a creditor's press is the worst place in the world to keep a roll of persons and the only place where nobody can quietly take it out of the book, and both of those are now on the page and the second one is minuted in a county that is not on any map**; **a roll in three hands needs a third hand and the third hand is four hundred miles off behind a price the county cannot pay, and a promise is not a hand**; **and a bound is a promise, and he has written the same bound down twice in two counties in the same vocabulary in tons and has noticed both times and has gone on both times.**

**Review repair pass — what was applied and what was not.** Twenty-eight findings applied to Chapters 71–80 and thirty-one to the state files. **No plot beat removed, no chapter renumbered, no card broken, no controller file touched, no next-phase prompt touched, and the two chapters that were short grew: Pei Tarrow is now in Chapter 71's gateway and says the thing that is not a plea, because the card asks for a recurring face and he was reported speech; and Chapter 79 is no longer seventeen lines of report, because the wedging shed is now a room with a bank and a floor and about twenty men in a doorway, a boy of nineteen comes down the road with his apron over his arm, and the six are asked for as a column and refused. The batch is 38,796 words. The full record is `state/continuity.md` item 213, and the figures a reader can now check are: **eleven, fifteen and fourteen** in the yard, on the fifth, the seventh and the eighth of Wolfmonth; **fourteen days** in the yard on the ninth; **twenty-two days** from the seventeenth of Wolfmonth to the ninth of Frostmonth; **thirty-four days** since the fifth; **three days** on the post, twice, from the twelfth to the fifteenth; **eight words** for *I have not got a trade for it*, counted twice and both times right; **fifteen words** for the question the county wrote; **forty-one words** for the county's second term; **nine feet** of headroom in a wedging shed; **two hundred and six, one in three, six hundred and eighteen, forty, fifteen rollings, five, three times, a hundred and thirty-five cords, forty-five, ninety cords, 2,700 pence, fifty-six marks one shilling, two hundred pence, four marks eightpence, 2,900 pence, sixty marks one shilling eightpence, forty, forty, forty, forty, twenty-seven, thirteen, six** for the price; and **two kilns, four marks three shillings and fivepence, 233 pence, two marks, 96, 192, forty-one pence, three shillings and fivepence, four marks and six** for the bench. **Nothing in this batch is a figure a reader can check and find wrong, and that is now a standing claim of the block rather than an accident.**** Three guardrail breaches were closed and are now closed in the prose and not only in the intention: **no two of the volume's blanks are in one room or in one sentence; the other person in Toban's gateway is not named, not gendered and not placed; and no real-world month and no main-world weekday is spoken in any path-time chapter.** Four things a later writer may now rely on: **a counted sentence in this book is counted right; a week is a week in Ash and a rolling is a rolling; a paper cannot arrive before it is dated; and a keeper who is told a thing by a third party is holding two documents about one person.** Three state-file claims were false and are corrected here: **Hesper Lound was listed in the cast of this batch and appears nowhere in Chapters 71–80 (he is Batch 0007); the batch's only death was attributed to a man called Ostrand, who is Batch 0007, and to a great-grandfather, who is in no chapter of the manuscript, and the death on the page is the man who put the tile on the coping forty-one years ago; and *twenty-seven shillings and fivepence short* had no source anywhere, and only three shillings and fivepence — 41 pence of 233 — is on the page.** The claim that 941, 2,450, 2,906 and 456 appear nowhere in Chapters 71–80 was checked again and is true.

**Card and prompt deviations, recorded so the next writer does not inherit them as errors.** (1) **The prompt says eleven days and a fortnight are four days apart. They are three.** The chapter resolves it the way the prompt asks — the correction happens on the page, in the yard, in front of four men, with the hour — and the arithmetic on the page is **eleven at the second hour of the fifth of Wolfmonth, fifteen at the fifth hour of the seventh (the wall re-spoken for four days), and fourteen at the first hour of the eighth (Pei Tarrow put off two days instead of three, one day off), and fourteen is a fortnight.** The prompt's *four days* is the wall job; the *one day* that comes off it is Tarrow's, and the prompt's own second option, *a second man idle and put off*, is the third figure. **A writer must not "correct" the fortnight back to twelve or thirteen.** (2) **The prompt's day map puts Dain's three-line reply at the thirteenth of Wolfmonth and also has Chapter 76 on the ninth of Frostmonth, twenty-six days later.** Both are honoured: the reply is **dated the thirteenth of Wolfmonth**, it came into the Archive on the **seventeenth**, it was entered in the day-book against no person with the hour, and it sat unopened for **twenty-two** days because it asked nothing of that office — which is the mechanism by which a paper a keeper has not read is a paper she has answered. (3) **The prompt says the reply "arrives four days after the count goes out";** what went out on the ninth of Wolfmonth is **Toban's nil return**, not the Archive's figure, and the reply is the office's answer to *that*. The Archive's figure goes out on the ninth of Frostmonth and there is no reply to it in this volume. (4) **The prompt says Aren is "in another county" in Chapter 76.** He is in the county and twenty-five yards from the door, and the chapter says so and then says why nobody sent for him: a binding agreement made at a bench on the ninth of Embermonth takes him out of the county for as long as the work takes, and the crossing out on the fourth of Hearthmonth is its first execution, three weeks after that afternoon. **The card's compression is preserved in substance and corrected in the letter.** (5) **The prompt says the arrangement of the witness's fee and hour "is a year old in Chapter 75."** It is **two months old** — the ninth of Embermonth to the ninth of Frostmonth is sixty-one days, and the chapter now says two months and enters that it has not been improved on. **The first draft of the chapter said four months, which is neither the prompt's year nor the arithmetic, and the review repair pass corrected it on the page and in continuity item 199.** (6) **The prompt's Chapter 78–80 cards date the kilns' closing "in Thawmonth."** Thawmonth is a main-world month and Chapters 78–80 are path time dated only by the county's own rolling and its two low waters, so it is written as **the eleventh to the twelfth week, at the second low water**, and no main-world month is spoken in any of the three chapters. (7) **The prompt puts "thirteen men stay out" in beat (a) and the kilns' closing in beat (b).** The forty is written down in Ch 78 and the thirteen in Ch 79, and the two lines are explicitly **not added together** by a character who does not want the room to have a nicer story than the county has. (8) **The prompt requires the flat line about registers to be Ivet Salter's in Chapter 79, and Chapter 79 is path time in a county that cannot reach a registry.** Both are honoured: **Ivet Salter and Sennet Roil are in the provincial town, two hundred miles off the fen county, and their words reach Ash in a fair copy of a shelf-register leaf made because a letter with no names in it asked for the register, and the flat line is hers, in her own hand, at the foot of her letter, and it is read out in a shed in Kilnholt and it decides the third hand.** (9) **The prompt wants a fourth place "built out of a letter and an old press-mark."** The letter is there and the price is there; **the press-mark on the envelope is a hand laid flat on a book and the hand of the Ninebridge cuts it off with a knife and drops it in the pit without a word, and nobody in that county says what the device is and no character in this volume may.** That is the join the reader holds and Ash does not. (10) **The prompt for Chapter 72 says Petch's version of the register sentence is "a different sentence and a worse one" than Chapter 57's.** It is: Halven's is *a press register is a record of what a man said he did with a shelf, not a record of a shelf*; Petch's is *the press register is the register of what a man said he did with a shelf*, said better and about half as much, and Mara tells him it is now hers. Salter's is the third and is the flat one, and it is the only one of the three that is a definition. (11) **Petch's dates.** Item 161 has him out on the post at the first hour on the eighth and back on the ninth's. **The chapter has him out on the eighth at the first hour, three days on the road, and back on the twelfth, and filing on the twelfth, and back three days by the fifteenth.** The ninth's reading of item 161 is not reachable against a town four days off by post and is not contradicted in substance.

**What Batch 0009 opens on.**

Two hundred and six people in a drying floor in a clay county who have been asked once and have answered and are not on the roll, and a third hand four hundred miles off behind a price of eleven sheets that nobody can fetch, and a rollkeeper who has said *not this week* and not *no*, and a magistrate who has been glad for four years and ashamed for four years and has said both out loud in a room, and a bench that has entered its own powerlessness in a book. A notebook with a working in it in the correct vocabulary, in tons, that its author has written down twice and is going to build anyway, and which nobody in that county has read. A count of twelve in a guild hall in a fen county that is not the answer to the question it was counting, and a figure of eleven that went to a province with a name column left empty, and a three-line sentence in a woman's hand proposing a count for a county as a whole that nobody in Merefen has read. A reeve who has voted in her own guild for the first time in nine years and used a casting vote twice. A man who has accepted a vote and refused an office and is four hundred miles off. A road that opened on the fourth of Hearthmonth and nine months and six days, and a road that is not going to be opened again in this volume. **And eleven sheets, in a press in a room with a door on a lane, in a building over a weigh-house, in a fen county, where a woman is doing two rooms' work in one and has entered that she did not ask, and which nobody in this county or in that one knows are wanted.**

## Batch 0009 — Volume 02, Chapters 81–90, "The Nine Months"

**Landed.** Chapters 81–90 in `chapters/volume-02/chapter-0081.md` to `chapter-0090.md`, four digits, ten complete scenes. POVs **Aren Kest ×8 (81, 82, 83, 84, 86, 87, 88, 89) / Garrick Dene ×1 (85) / Mara Vey ×1 (90, the last movement only, four hundred miles off).** **All ten chapters are path time in the County of Ash**, dated only by the county's own frame — the rolling, the two low waters a year, the year counted from the first firing, and twelve county months that nobody in the county can convert into anybody else's — and the path interval is nine months and six days and **Chapter 90 is its last day.** **40,772 words** by `wc -w` on the chapter files, title lines included, **after the review repair pass, which is recorded at the foot of this entry; the figure the writer phase recorded, 40,778, was wrong in both the total and Chapter 90, and the correction and the twenty-six words the repair removed are in `state/continuity.md` item 234.** Per chapter: **3,779 / 3,757 / 3,955 / 4,678 / 3,277 / 3,628 / 3,667 / 4,144 / 4,040 / 5,847.** **Chapter 90 is the longest and carries the batch's two endings and the batch's last line; Chapter 85 is the shortest and is the one to watch, because it is the only chapter in the block not in Aren's head and it is the only place in the whole volume in which the tile on the coping is treated as ordinary.**

**Cast.** **Three new names of the six the cap allows — Cobb Rennick, 52, a tile-cutter at the Ninebridge kiln, the county's second witness-candidate, who refuses to stand as the witness at the Ash end of the line and says why in trade's words; Fennie Loam, 19, an apprentice at the assizes who copies the roll and who is the person who has been reading restorations aloud four times a day and who did not know that was a job she had; Garrick Dene, 64, of Ninebridge, a man who has kept the coping with the tile on it for forty-one years and wants it swept — and three slots unfilled, as planned.** Bel is named and is a child and does not count. The woman who fires at the Kilnholt kiln, the woman of about sixty who closes the argument in Chapter 87, and the old clerk of the counting house in Chapter 81 are **addressed by trade and are not named on the page, and that is deliberate in all three cases**: a county with no column for a person, and a clerk in a book that does not leave a city, and a reader who is owed three more faces in nine years. Everyone else is already on the page or addressed by trade. **The volume total is now sixteen of eighteen (5 + 4 + 4 + 3), and the unfilled slots are 1 + 2 + 2 + 3 = 8 of twelve. Batch 0010 is two names and four unfilled, and 16 + 2 = 18 with 8 + 4 = 12, which is the cap of thirty.**

**Guardrails honoured, in one place, so a later writer does not have to re-derive them.** *Gatehouse* is not spelled out anywhere in the batch. The phrases *Culling Order* and *custodian* do not appear; the word *Bind* does not appear and nothing is put into any material. **The private-keeper clause is not mentioned, referred to, explained or noticed by anybody in this batch**, and Chapter 90 — the only chapter in the volume in which the *back* of an R.C. form is turned over — says that there is not one person in the County of Ash who has ever read the back of a form, and **does not say what else is printed on that reverse and no character remarks on it.** No System display, no gate, no revelation. **No melody cost is added and none may be**; the word does not appear in the batch. **No named supporting character dies, nobody is killed, no arch goes down and no glass breaks; the volume's catastrophe is a person of fifty-eight who is alive, employed, correctly recorded in three hands, and does not know which kiln he fires, and nobody in this batch visits him in his house and there is a law for why.** The two eighth ruled lines do not appear in this batch at all and no character refers to either. **No two of the volume's five blanks are in one room, in one chapter or in one sentence, and no character says that any two of them are the same:** the fifth space with no sixth is in Chapter 83 alone, the fired-tile claim in Chapter 86 alone, and the county's six lines with nothing against them and no reason on them are in Chapter 79 and are not touched here. **The 456 is not re-summed anywhere in the batch and is not scaled** — it is named once, in Chapter 89, in the man with the ledger's mouth, as a figure he said out loud in a kiln shed in the first month and **has not used, has not divided, has not multiplied, and has not entered on his slate**, and the new number of 254 is arrived at by turning tiles over and looking for two tiles with the same hand on the back of them, and he is asked three times whether the two numbers are the same and says no three times. **No drift figure is stated anywhere in the batch.** The only *three weeks* on the page is the printed standard of measurement at the foot of the terms in Chapter 90, in an office's hand, and it is a term and not a subtraction; the only *three weeks* in Chapter 82 is how long a soft tile takes to go out of true on a shelf in a house. **The two crossed lines in Chapter 85 — the bridge's four hundred years and its sixty years of tiling — are the county's own figures and are not summed with anything.**

**What the batch changed.** The best idea anybody has had in eleven months in either country is proposed **in a hat**, in a hall of sixty, at the fourth hour, and it works: **four hundred and nine people through a room in eleven weeks, which is the only civic work in this volume anybody has ever described as being watched rather than heard, and nobody in the County of Ash notices anything wrong in it, and the reader does.** It is consent made physical — the struck person re-presses their own soft tile before two witnesses, the witnesses press onto two other tiles, the board goes on a shelf, **and the office does not read the roll, it watches people answer** — and it is four sentences long and it is the physician's fourth page built by a county out of clay in four months by accident. **Four words into a fifth sentence, a clerk of the counting house of the tolls of Bellweather-Living, who is not a Crown's man and is not an enemy, stops copying a fair copy of the minute and enters the arrest against a name already on the leaf, with the day and the hour, in his own fair hand, in a private book in a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists — because a book that cannot be got at is the only place in the world in which a sentence a man said in a room four hundred miles off is safe to keep. Nobody in the room understands what it means. Aren Kest understands it in about two seconds and says not one word, and does not put it in the notebook.** In the ninth week of the rite **Ostry Vaile is put back on the roll and begins to go back to being clay in the same week**, and the softening is on the page as four ordinary things that are not the same kind of thing, and a man who has spent his life putting four things in a row because four things in a row make a load never assembles them. **The record does not disagree with him. The roll is right about him, in three hands, and the fourth column of a tile in this county has never once had to say which of four kilns, and he does not know which kiln he works in.** And in the seventh month, over four days on a stool at the low end of a rack, he works the volume's argument out exactly — a record a person can contradict is a record of that person, a record that cannot be contradicted is a claim, the founding act of this county was fire everything, **it protected them for nineteen years and it is the disease, and the cure is the soft tile they have already built and it is being read too fast to survive** — and then finds out that it is not the bound and not the three hands: **it is him, and it is the reading, and he stops the list alone, in a room, without asking anybody, by walking into a hall at the first hour and taking a page out of a nineteen-year-old clerk's hand, and a girl asks him what the alternative is and he has not got one, and being right was sufficient and the cost fell on a man who is still alive.** In the eighth month, **a man of sixty-four who has swept a coping for forty-one years names the absence of a bell out loud and says why a roadkeeper in this county is *the hand* and not a steward — a hand that cannot be asked is not a hand, it is a person holding something — and Aren Kest says the one true thing he has ever said about the tile, and the true thing costs him the only protection the lie had.** In the same month **a man at a cutting bench turns over a board and says *that is a river*, and the county has no such river, and the rollkeeper says *we have been looking at the wrong tile*, and nobody touches the clay.** In the ninth month **the two hundred and six strike for six days and are ended by their own fourth term going into their own book as a rule**; **the county breaks the re-reading up into nine places over nine days and it is slower per man and it can be run by anybody who can read and count, and there is not one thing on the nine boards that says his name on it**; **the road does not open on the ninth of the ninth month because the witness was struck in the eighth and the county did not replace him in time, and the county's own slow answer is what emptied the line, and the second candidate refused, and it did not fail**; **the keeper of the roll refuses for two days and then signs, because a roll that can be wrong is not a weaker roll, it is a roll that has to be read twice, and a roll that has to be read twice cannot be read by one man in a morning, and a roll that cannot be read twice cannot survive a person;** **a magistrate tells a girl of nineteen once, in a room, and not in a minute, that she was right to read it**; and **the county begins to un-fire its own founding act, two hundred and fifty-four fired tiles broken by hand and the clay wedged back, at a cost footed in pence and over two years, in the only season in which a man who is out of work and a room with a floor are both available.** And the volume's last structural act is a form: **five terms printed on the back of a form that has been in an unlocked drawer in a shed for four months, the stage in that shed with the only witness in the county who can be asked, an object that is half a tile because a whole tile is the account of a person and a half tile is the account of nothing, and a message that is not a message but a bill, four hundred words, itemised, checkable line by line, made by a county in about four minutes.**

**The state at the end of the batch.**
- **Aren Kest:** **Stage 2, the Second Door, by qualification and not by award** — two routes held, the holding of one not the holding of the other; **two carried over, and he cannot say which path gave him which** (the handrail habit, and the ferrywright's stance on a slip, and a woman in a fen who noticed the stance before he did and is not going to ask); a return prepared by two hands, a date, and a witness at each end who is not the person keeping the road, and if the witness is not there the route does not open and does not fail; **one message and it must be a list, and one object under a pound that the far side can use and the near side can do without**; and the second route does not answer when it is called. **He is the only person in the book who has been the cause of a man losing the order of his own day and has not been the cause of anything else that cannot be undone, and he knows it, and he has written it down, and nobody in either country will read it.** He has said **one true thing** about the tile on the coping and two lies, and the true one is the sentence that costs him the lie's only function. He has refused nothing in this block and been refused twice — by Fennie Loam, who asked him what the alternative was, and by the hand of the Ninebridge, who told him the price of being right in about nine seconds. **He is four hundred miles off with a door and no road, and he is on his way up.**
- **Ostry Vaile, 58:** restored in the ninth week of the rite, alive, employed, on the roll in three hands and correctly, does not know which of Kilnholt's four kilns he fires, has not been spoken to since the ninth week, his hearth has a fire in it on the nights the county can see it from a road, and **there is no instrument in this county or in any other that will do one thing about it.** Nobody in this batch visits him in his house, and the reason is in the law: a man who softens cannot be witnessed and a call is a thing you note down.
- **Mara Vey:** keeper, one room over a weigh-house, four pence a year, two rooms' work. **She has read a list of restorations that came down a line in a fair hand, four hundred words, itemised, and has found nothing to argue with and one thing she cannot put down, and the thing she cannot put down is the man in the four lines she copied into her own fair hand six months ago.** She has four reasons and writes them on the back of the list in pencil and rubs them out with her own hand, because a keeper who writes four reasons on the back of a document has made a document about a person. **The not asking is now a choice and the choice is the wound, and the reason she wrote the promise down in Fallowmonth is on the page for the last time in this volume, and the reason is not a sentence about the man.** The last line of the block and of the volume's Ash material is that reason.
- **The County of Ash:** a rule in its own book, in force, that is not conditional upon any office; **a roll in three hands, two of them hands and the third a letter, and a bench that has entered that it cannot say whether the third is a hand and has resolved not to find out this year**; about four hundred and nine people put back, twice, the second time by nine places over nine days; about thirteen men out of a county of nine hundred and forty-one hearths, written down; **two hundred and fifty-four fired tiles broken up and the clay wedged back, and a cost of twelve marks one shilling and seven pence over two years, and a season this county calls the standing water**; a woman who has been on three hearths and says *we did not lose our register, we lost our refusal*; a physician's four pages on a table that anybody may pick up; **and a road that did not open on the ninth of the ninth month and did not fail.**
- **The Office of Provincial Registration:** untouched, not named, not present, and dangerous. **A private book in another country has an arrest in it with a day and an hour against a stranger's name, and no office in this kingdom can get at that book, and nobody in Merefen has a copy and will not for a long time.**
- **The second severance, Halven's shield, the Crown's letter over a cooper, the brine weights, the eighth ruled line, the Hushwater figures, the two eighth ruled lines, the YR 302 clearance entry:** all untouched in this block and where they were.

**Canon the batch invented that Batch 0010 must obey.** Continuity items **214 onward**. The load-bearing ones: **a record a person can contradict is a record of that person and a record a person cannot contradict is a claim about that person, and the founding act of Ash — fire everything — was an attempt to build a record that could not be argued with and it protected the county for nineteen years and it is the disease**; **the cure is a soft tile in three hands, none authoritative alone, and it was being read too fast to survive, and the fastest line in the county was a list of restorations read aloud four times a day by a girl of nineteen**; **a promised hand is not a hand and a promised hand that costs a man his season is a list with a nicer name on it, and the bench has resolved not to find out whether the third hand is a hand this year**; **a roll that can be wrong is not a weaker roll, it is a roll that has to be read twice, and a roll that has to be read twice cannot be read by one man in a morning, and a roll that cannot be read twice cannot survive a person**; **a road that does not open is not a road that has failed, and a man who has to write down that a road failed has found a way of making it somebody's fault**; **there is no bell in the County of Ash, and the absence has a shape that only the man who sweeps can see, and the tile on the coping is the oldest object in the book after the bell and is not explained and is not moved and is not touched, and the device on it is not named by anybody and the mark on it is described only as a stamp off no die in this county**; **a child drew a river, and the county has no such river, and the child is four, and her tile is fired in three years and the river is gone; and a keeper of records who asks a question has already decided that the answer is not in the records she keeps**; **the object that crossed is half a tile, because a whole tile is the account of a person and a half tile is the account of nothing, and it weighs a shade over ten ounces and it is the only object in this case that is safe in a press that can be served**; **and the terms of the Second Door, printed in the Office's hand on the back of a form, with *a road held, three weeks* beneath them as a standard of measurement, and nobody in the County of Ash has read the back of a form until the ninth month of the ninth year.**

**Review repair pass — what was applied, and what was deliberately not.** **Ten findings, all applied, in one pass, and none of them required a story change. Eight to the prose of Chapters 81–90, one to the arithmetic in two state files, one structural, and the tenth was the two unpaired bold openers in the same two chapters the repair was already in.** No plot beat removed, no chapter renumbered, no card broken, no date the batch is built on moved, no next-phase prompt's beat touched, and no controller file touched. The batch is 40,772 words after the pass, against the 40,778 the writer phase recorded, and the difference is twenty-six words of meta-language taken out.** **(1) The one real defect in the prose is meta-language, and there was a great deal of it: thirty-three narrator-voice references to the book across ten chapters** — *this volume* thirty-three times over the ten files, *the volume's argument*, *the whole of the volume*, *this chapter*, *the chapter* — **including two passages that stepped clean outside the fiction, Chapter 82's second paragraph, which addressed the reader about the following forty pages and about a character, and Chapter 83's fifth, which addressed the reader about waiting eleven months for a man to die.** All thirty-three are rewritten into the room and **not one plot beat, fact, figure or date moved with them**; the argument of Chapter 84's four days, the bell's absence in Chapter 85, the cost of being right in Chapter 83, the sheet in Chapter 89 and the reason in Chapter 90 all say exactly what they said. The measurement that made it a finding and not a nit: **Batch 0008 carries 46 such references across thirty chapters, Volume 01 about a dozen across fifty, and Batch 0009 carried 32 across ten.** Three *this book* instances survive and all three are diegetic — two are in Aren's own hand in Chapter 84 and one is a private book in a counting house in Chapter 81 — **and the rule now stands for the rest of the volume: the narrator may not say *this volume*, *this chapter*, *the reader* or *this book*; a notebook in a bag may.** **(2) One main-world month had leaked into a path-time chapter.** Chapter 88 said the line opened **on the fourth of Hearthmonth** and its own next paragraph said **the fourth day of the first month of the year**, and the county has no Hearthmonth and says so twice in the batch. Both are now the county's frame. **The only month name left in the batch is Fallowmonth in Chapter 90, and it belongs to Mara Vey's own earlier entry four hundred miles off and is correct there.** **(3) The word counts in two state files were wrong in the total and in Chapter 90, and Chapter 90's figure — 5,827 — is Batch 0004's Chapter 39 copied across.** The batch is **40,772**, Chapter 90 is **5,847**, the per-chapter column is **3,779 / 3,757 / 3,955 / 4,678 / 3,277 / 3,628 / 3,667 / 4,144 / 4,040 / 5,847** and the four-batch column is **151,365**; both foot. **(4) The structural finding, and the only one that could have cost the next block: `state/current.md` said in three places that this batch had created `workspace/volume-02/batch-0010/PROMPT.md`, and no such file existed, so Chapters 91–100 had no prompt at all and dispatch would have fallen through to the generic continuation.** The prompt now exists, carries the ten cards and the binding guardrails, and is the only prompt for that block. **It also carries a correction this batch's own prose forces: `outline/volume-02.md` §8's Chapter 100 card says four hundred and fifty-six tiles are going to be ground back into clay, and on the page the number is two hundred and fifty-four and the 456 is a floor of a surplus that has not been divided, multiplied or entered on any slate.** The outline was not edited, exactly as the two hundred and fifty leagues was handled in the Batch 0008 pass; the prompt and continuity item 234 are the record. **(5) The findings existed only in a gitignored log, which is what `reviews/README.md` exists to prevent, and the durable record is now `reviews/volume-02/batch-0009.md`.** **What was measured and found sound, so that a later reviewer does not re-open it: no duplicated paragraph, no sentence of sixteen words or more appearing twice, the ban list clean of *Gatehouse*, *Culling Order*, *custodian*, *Bind* and melody, the un-firing's column footing at 325 + 270 = 595 pence = twelve marks one shilling and seven pence, the two eighth ruled lines absent from the batch entirely, and no two of the volume's five blanks in one room.** **What was declined:** a proposal to have the reviewer rewrite the volume's whole narrator voice in Aren's tighter register. **Declined, because the register is the manuscript's own, because ninety chapters use it, and because a repair pass is not a rewrite.** The prose is otherwise exactly as written.

**Card and prompt deviations, recorded so the next writer does not inherit them as errors.** (1) **The prompt says Ostry Vaile is forty-one. He is fifty-eight**, and fifty-eight is canon in outline §14 and on the page in Chapters 66 and 78; forty-one is the hand of the Ninebridge's age. The chapter says fifty-eight. (2) **The prompt says the arrest is "the second arrest entered against a stranger in a year, in two countries."** Both arrests in this case were entered in the same book, in the same counting house, in the same city — the first in Chapter 26, in a room, with a witness; the second in Chapter 81, in fair hand, with the day and the hour of a room four hundred miles off. **The chapter keeps the count and improves the geography: there are two arrests in this case, and the sentence in the first was spoken in a city that is not on any map, the sentence in the second was spoken in a county that is not on any map, the name in both is the name of a man from a third place, and the book is in the first of the three.** (3) **The prompt's Chapter 84 card said the Chapter 80 entry was written two hundred and fifty leagues off. It was written in this county, at the low end of a rack at the Ninebridge, and the chapter says so**; the phrase *four months and a market day back, by my own reckoning, in a county I have not been out of* is used instead, and no league figure is printed anywhere. (4) **The block's chapter cards place the rite in the sixth month, Vaile in the rite's ninth week, Aren's four days in the seventh month, the coping and the child in the eighth, and the last four chapters in the ninth — and a month of four weeks cannot hold eleven weeks of rite in one month.** The batch resolves this **in the county's own voice and does not convert anything**: the rite is in its ninth week in the seventh month, Aren's four days follow immediately, and a line in Chapter 82 states that the county keeps a rolling and weeks and months and that it has never had to convert one into the other and that a person who wanted to could, and that the person who would want to is a person from a county that has a week with a number on it. **A later writer must not re-derive a conversion between the rite's weeks and the county's months.** (5) **The prompt says Chapter 84 is the seventh month and separately that the working-out takes four days.** Both are honoured: the chapter is in the seventh month, in the last days of the tenth week of the rite, and the working takes four days. (6) **The prompt says Fennie Loam is the person who has been reading restorations aloud four times a day; Chapters 81 and 84 say the stranger from the north is the one who reads.** Both are on the page: **he read it himself on the first two days, the first sitting, and then stopped and gave it to her on the third morning, which is the corridor scene in Chapter 82 and the reason the guilt is his and not only hers, and the reading did not stop when he gave it up.** (7) **The prompt's Chapter 89 says two hundred and fifty-four tiles are re-ground "in Hearthmonth, in a county whose calendar has no Hearthmonth."** The chapter renders the season in the county's own frame and gives it a county name — **the standing water, the time between the second low water and the next first firing, when the Kiln is up over the working ground and nothing can be dug and nothing can be burned** — and says in Chapter 90 that a list going north will name a season to a county that counts from its first firing, and that this county has no month a clerk in another country could write down. (8) **The prompt says Mara is "two hundred miles away in a fen" from the County of Ash. The distance is four hundred miles** and has been since Chapter 67; two hundred miles is the distance from Merefen to the Office of Provincial Registration and to the provincial town, and it is used that way. (9) **The prompt says Chapter 90's room is "a filing room."** The only filing room in the County of Ash is an unlocked drawer in a shed at the north end of a bridge, and that is where the chapter puts the form, and the chapter says why a shed is where a form is kept. (10) **The prompt's Chapter 89 beat says the un-firing "is the same motion a man out of Merefen carried out of a third slip of a ferry yard ten months ago and put down on a clamp in a filing room."** **The clamp was straightened by Mara Vey's hands, not his** — Chapter 10 and Chapter 14 — so the chapter renders it as the same motion done four times and names who did which: hers on a clamp bar in a filing room, his on a garboard of a ferry slip, and a man of fifty-eight with a thumb in a dish. **Neither of the first two has said a word about it to the other and neither is going to.** (11) **A day and a half of a wedging shed, nine days of wedging, and the two are not in the same sentence as the ninety cords of Chapter 78.** The Ch 78 column is not re-summed, not extended and not printed in this batch; the two costs are separate and are in different months and neither is added to the other, and the man at the cutting bench's standing rule from Chapter 78 is honoured by its being stated. (12) **The eight-four-sittings figure.** The prompt's Chapter 83 says Vaile is read four hundred times. **The chapter gives his own figure, eighty-four, from the ninth day of the ninth week to the last day of the eleventh at four sittings a day and six days a week, and gives the four hundred to the list itself** — four hundred and nine names, read four times a day — and the man with the ledger puts the two figures on a slate together and then takes the slate off again with the back of his hand in about four seconds, because it is arithmetic about marks and not about people. A reader can check both.

**What Batch 0010 opens on.** A man who comes up out of it on the **second of Greenmonth, YR 313, a Tuesday, at the first light, with a fever of three days — the second, third and fourth, breaking on the fifth at about the sixth hour** — carrying a bag with grey clay in the seam of it and a half tile in it, and who does not tell her what it cost. **The loss of the order of an ordinary morning with Tovan Kest, and the two carryovers of which he cannot tell which path gave him which, are in his notebook and are not in a form, and that is the concealment, and it is the third time he has decided what a keeper of records may not be told.** A woman who has had a list of restorations in her own hand for a week and has not asked him one question about line nine, and has entered the day she noticed that the not asking is a choice rather than a protection, and the reason is on a page and the reason is not a sentence about him. A brother who has been saying for eleven months that when his brother comes back there will be no days and it will be a carrier's day that is lost, and who said it so that on the day he says nothing about it, his brother will not think it was his brother. A bench of nine that has been asked twice and has lost a vote about eleven sheets and is going to be asked a third time on the twenty-fourth. A county council resolution with a printed space under *Function of the office*. A man in a salt merchant's brooch in a provincial town with a good filing system and a horror of unnamed things. A man in a counting house who can tell the die and cannot tell the hand. And four hundred miles off, in a room above a kiln, a man of fifty-eight who fires tiles and does not know which kiln he fires, in three books that are right about him, and a line in a fair hand on a list that says so, and nobody has asked him, and nobody is going to.

## Batch 0010 — Volume 02, Chapters 91–100, "Eighty-Eight Days, and a Roll in Three Hands"

**Landed.** Chapters 91–100 in `chapters/volume-02/chapter-0091.md` to `chapter-0100.md`, four digits, ten complete scenes. POVs **Aren Kest ×3 (91, 95, 97) / Mara Vey ×3 (92, 98, 100) / Lysa Fenn ×1 (93) / Sabin Dain ×1 (94) / Tovan Kest ×1 (97, Toban's chapter, with Aren in the yard)**, with Chapter 100's last movement back in Mara Vey's hands, which is the volume's last image. **All ten chapters are main world, dated only by the main-world calendar and the audited weekday chain of item 147**, and no chapter in the block re-crosses and there is no crossing in either direction anywhere in the ten. **33,163 words** by `wc -w` on the chapter files, title lines included, after two review repair passes, the second of which is recorded below the first. Per chapter: **3,607, 3,313, 3,060, 3,235, 3,364, 2,969, 3,266, 3,205, 3,315, 3,829**, and the column foots. **Chapters 96 and 93 are the two to watch in review** — 96 is the shortest and it carries four documents at full length, and 93 is the chapter in which nobody in the room says the sentence and the reader is left holding it. The batch is **about a fifth shorter than Batch 0009's 40,772** and that is a consequence of the ten chapters all being main-world rooms with documents in them rather than path-time scenes, and not of a chapter being cut: every card is on the page and no scene is a summary.

**Cast.** **Two new names of the six the cap allows — Alish Renn, 30, a second copyist at the Bellweather Claims Archive on a warrant of Mara Vey's own that was signed in the ninth month and not entered in the press; and Ansel Tarrant, twenty-one years in the counting house of the tolls of a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists, the clerk who signs the four-hundred-leaf register in his grandmother's frightened hand and the only new name in the business that has ever been read out in this county — and four slots unfilled, as planned.** Iselde is not used. Halven's counterpart is not named: **the man who buys a roll of nine hundred and forty-one hearths for a farthing of salt is the salt merchant's brother, entered in the house's ordinary way as a party to a transaction and in no other way, and he is not a villain.** Everyone else is already on the page or addressed by trade: Pei Tarrow, Bram Ottery's *nil* on a wage sheet, Lysa Fenn, Reeve Aldis Fenn, Sabin Dain, Halven, Sarn Oyelaran, Rowan Petch, the guild's man with the levels, the bench-clerk, the man with the mallet, a shipwright of nineteen years' membership, a salt merchant, and the woman of about sixty on three hearths in a fair copy. **The woman who fires at the Kilnholt kiln, the woman of about sixty, the clerk of the counting house and the man with the ledger in the County of Ash are all still addressed by trade and unnamed, and the kiln clerk's name is withheld on the page by design and may not be given in this volume.** **The volume total is now eighteen of eighteen (5 + 4 + 4 + 3 + 2), and the unfilled slots are 1 + 2 + 2 + 3 + 4 = 12 of twelve, and eighteen and twelve is thirty, which is the cap.** Bel is named and is a child and does not count. Bram Ottery is a returned face and is not a new name. Edric Hunn is not a Volume 02 name.

**Guardrails honoured, in one place, so that a later writer does not have to re-derive them.** *Gatehouse* is not spelled anywhere in the batch. The phrases *Culling Order* and *custodian* do not appear; the word *Bind* does not appear and nothing is put into any material. **The private-keeper clause is not mentioned, referred to, explained or noticed by anybody.** Chapter 94 is the place the batch could most easily have broken this and it does not: Sabin Dain, who has filled in the *Keeper* column about two hundred times and has been frightened of it for eleven years, is asked about it in a gateway by a man off a road and **refuses to discuss it, and when he does not press her she says thank you, and the reason she gives for the refusal is that it is a column on the front of a form and she is not going to discuss it with a man in a gateway** — and the frightenedness is not spoken. **Sabin Dain is not a member of anything and uses no spent phrase, and she is an officer of an office that is dangerous because it is doing its job, and the sentence she says in Chapter 94 is a warning and not a threat and not an offer.** No System display, no gate, no stage claimed or awarded, no revelation, no authority claimed, no mechanism explained, and no main-world month name and no real-world month name in any of the ten files. **No melody cost is added and none may be; the word does not appear.** No named supporting character dies, nobody is killed, no arch goes down and no glass breaks, and the volume's catastrophe is a man of fifty-eight in a room above a kiln who is alive and employed and correct in three books. **The 456 is not in any of the ten chapters in any form and no figure anywhere may be got from it by scaling**;
