9. **Nine figures introduced in this batch, all footed on the page before the total is said aloud, all re-added and checked in the review pass:** 32 × 3 = 96, + 12 = **108 pence** (Ch 115, two marks and twelve pence); 96 + 6 = **102 pence** (Ch 111, the rider and the column); 48 + 51 + 39 + 62 + 55 + 44 + 71 + 58 + 66 + 43 + 60 = **597 persons** (Ch 113, footed twice in two orders and both run-totals shown); 2,880 + 360 = **3,240**, 288 × 9 = **2,592**, 2,880 − 2,592 = **288**, 288 + 360 = **648** (Ch 116, the yard's arithmetic, and **648 is the last fifth the guild's first hand gave up, so the number the yard is out of pocket by and the number a man gave away in a gateway are the same number and not the same money**); 32 × 11 = **352 pence** (Ch 117, seven marks and sixteen pence, and 288 pence is six marks or six shillings a man, not five). **The Chapter 10 column and the Chapter 108 figures are untouched and not re-derived, and nothing is scaled out of either.**
10. **The section device was cut by two thirds.** Thirty-nine bolded narrator headers and thirty `---` rules became **thirty-five headers and ten rules, one per chapter**, per the batch prompt's guard. Two passages of near-verbatim duplication were removed: the "no column for a name" reasoning, which is now stated once in Chapter 112 as interior and referred to in Chapter 115, and the nine-item list, which is now printed in full in Chapter 112 and summarised in Chapter 115.
11. **Chapter 120 no longer re-runs Chapter 110's failed conversion.** It now states the incomparability (a rolling is four weeks, a month in the county he came out of is thirty days, and there is not one of those in the other) and stops, because the one conversion the volume attempts is Chapter 130's and is the plant.

**What the batch changed.** **A road does not open, and nobody says the word.** The Marches' returned sheet named a day for four men of a yard and named no day for a line; the sixth of the six terms is that a line is not opened on a day the Marches have not named; he crossed out on a day they had not named, so the line did not open, and the second route did not answer at all, and **it is the first crossing in this case that has come back with no answer of any kind, and the rule that nobody in either country is to put a word on it is written in his own hand on the page above the nine.** Term four was kept on the eighth day: the stone was sworn in a field he was not in, because he was in the county, and that is the first time in four weeks the fourth of the six has been kept and the price of it is now visible to the reader. **The road was refused on the terms he offered and accepted on the terms it offered, and the refusal was one question asked in about four seconds by a woman of thirty-eight who has never been asked anything — *how many people in your county are going to read it* — and he had to say he did not know, and a county that has never refused anything has no word for refusing, and the word it has is the word for a thing that got lost.** The gauge column is on the page in full: nine gauges, nine books, eight thousand and thirty-one lines in one of them, twice a day, eleven years, in a hand that is not a clerk's, with a nine-line entry in a different hand that is the one week in eleven years the book does not have, and nobody has ever asked her for a page and the council has never looked at it. **A village where the ground is white, about sixty households on the roll and not on the oath, and a woman of about sixty on three hearths who has never been asked what she swore and who told him, unasked, exactly what his county will do with her.** A bridgewright saw the mechanism in a day and a half — a hand's width of bank gone every year for a hundred and forty years — **and did not say it, in that county, to anybody, and wrote down *seen, not told*, because the third of the six is the north is not to send anybody who can be asked to come and look, and because a county's one account of its own water is a claim and not a record until a person is put in front of it.**

**The cost, entered.**
- **A reply to Sabin Dain that neither accepts nor refuses, in a bag for the provincial town, in this county's hand, with no returnable date, no claim number and no copy in this county, and nobody in Merefen knows it was written.** He states what the office is buying and that it is not the thing he can do, and he does not use the word *custodian* and does not explain the clause.
- **The yard is out of pocket six hundred and forty-eight pence** and knows it and has told the man who will have to hold the yard for ninety days, and has told nobody else.
- **A magistrate spent a second instrument in four minutes with the form's own construction**, and the office's reply is a figure for a district of eleven counties in a woman's hand, **in which one county that has returned nothing in eleven years is returned at the average of its neighbours**, and it is not an instrument and cannot be refused.
- **The line's book has four columns and no column for a name**, and the sixth of the six is why, and the book at the far end is in a chamber over a tithe barn in a county with no office in it and cannot be got at by anybody.

**The question Batch 0013 opens on.** **Thirteen months, the first third.** He is in a county that has refused him, with a gauge book nobody has read, a mechanism he saw and may not say, a stone sworn without him, a woman of thirty-eight who will not give her book to a keeper, a woman of sixty on three hearths who was never asked what she swore, about sixty households on the roll and not on the oath, and four men of a fen county already at work on that county's own calendar and never once asked what they swore either. The council is in a room over a tithe barn, the soil is a hundred and forty years of a ditch moved a hand's width at a time, and **the question nobody in that room asks and the volume's own question is: how do you keep a promise that has been kept for a hundred and forty years and is killing everybody who keeps it.** And in a fen county four hundred miles off a bench of nine that has been asked four times is still saying yes, a yard is short six hundred and forty-eight pence, and a keeper of records is still not asking.

**What the next phase inherits.** `outline/volume-03.md` §8 Block 3 and §16 and §17 first, then this entry and continuity items 288 onward. **No name in a Crown's hand in this county since the twenty-seventh of Fallowmonth, and there is still not one.** No Ash figure is on the page in this batch and the 456 is not on the page in any form. No main-world month name, no real-world month name and no main-world weekday is spoken anywhere in Chapters 118–120 and no Marches character converts a rolling. **The tile on the coping is not touched, not looked at, not named, and its device is named by anybody.** The eighth ruled line in the served schedule is not looked at. The brine weights are neither mentioned nor filed. The five blanks of Volume 02 are not joined. *Culling Order*, *custodian*, *Bind* and *Gatehouse* do not appear. The breach is open, **no tenth not-asking is entered, the ninth stands as entered on the sixteenth of Longlight**, and no apology and no mistake has been made available to anybody in these ten chapters.

## Batch 0013 — Volume 03, Chapters 121–130, "Thirteen Months, the First Third"

**Landed.** Chapters 121–130 in `chapters/volume-03/chapter-0121.md` to `chapter-0130.md`, four digits, ten complete path-time scenes, all in Aren's POV with the council, Thessa Wend, Delya Munn, Coll Ferrow, the yard's four men, Ordmar Gace and Aled Rhys. **16,315 words** by `wc -w` including title lines, per chapter 1614 / 1765 / 1440 / 1129 / 2118 / 2632 / 2026 / 969 / 980 / 1642, **after the review repair pass recorded at the foot of this entry; the first pass was 11,586 words and the repair added 4,729 to Chapters 125, 126, 127, 128 and 130 and nothing else.** No System panel and no Gatehouse line in the batch, where the prompt allowed one. Zero `---` rules and zero fully-bolded standalone lines in the whole block, against ceilings of six and twenty.

**Cast.** **Two new names and four slots unfilled, exactly as §14 allows: Ordmar Gace**, 49, of the tenant league, first named in Chapter 122 as a presence and in Chapter 128 in prose as a speaker, who wants a man to be able to say what he will not plant and not lose a field for it; **and Aled Rhys**, 27, a water-barge hand, first named in Chapter 124, who has always had the word the barn had to go and find, and uses refused without heat. **Volume 03's running total is six of seven with twelve of twenty-three slots unfilled.** The woman of sixty-six who keeps the seed room is on the page in Chapter 127 with her nine jars, her wall with two earlier answers, her refusal of thanks, and three mason's marks put on the outside of her wall with her leave, and **she is not named in this batch; her name is Batch 0014's per §14, which controls over the §8 card.** Everyone else is established or addressed by trade.

**Card and prompt deviations, recorded so the next writer does not inherit them as errors.**
1. **Chapters ran at forty per cent of the length of Batches 0011–0012 on the first pass and the repair pass has not closed that gap.** The first pass was 11,586 words against 29,130 and 31,648, and Chapters 127 (808) and 130 (923) were under half the batch median; the review called the batch's claim that *nothing was compressed to get under one* unsupported by its own figures, and that judgement was correct. **The repair pass added real consequence to the four chapters the review found inert and nothing else: 125 1028 to 2118, 126 990 to 2632, 127 808 to 2026, 128 909 to 969, 130 923 to 1642.** The batch is now 16,315 words, which is 56 per cent of Batch 0012 and 52 per cent of Batch 0011, and is **still a real deviation and not a benign one**; Batch 0014 is warned in its own prompt to write full scenes at the length of Batches 0011 and 0012 and not to treat this batch as the house length. Nothing was padded: every added paragraph carries a change, a cost or a consequence, and the four chapters that were repaired are the four the review named.
2. **The section device was removed entire: zero `---` rules and zero fully-bolded standalone lines**, against ceilings of six and twenty. The prompt also requires no two chapters to carry the same number of either; with ten chapters and a ceiling of six that uniqueness is arithmetically impossible (ten distinct rule-counts sum to at least forty-five), so the ceiling was honoured over the uniqueness and the metronome of one rule per chapter is gone.
3. **The word for the Stage 3 working stands exactly twice as a noun, both in Chapter 124, once each, and nowhere else in the block**, and it is not restated in Chapters 125–130. **The string `cut` occurs a third time in the whole block, in Chapter 127, and it is not the working: it is the flood line on the chapel stone, `a line on the stone that nobody had cut`.** A later phase grepping the word must read that one before concluding anything. Delya Munn: there is a cut that would take the water out of that ditch in an afternoon, and the man off the road has done worse in a shorter time. Aren: the last time one of these was cut, the anchor tore and a community lost contact for ever, and the man who did it was told afterwards that the witness had been forged, and he wrote it down. Ditch labour elsewhere is dug, opened, or cleared, and in Chapter 127 the marking of stone is marked, lettered and chiselled and never cut.
4. **Money foots twice, both in pence on the page before the total is said aloud.** Chapter 121: a rider at twelve pence a day for sixteen days, 48 + 48 + 48 + 48 = 192 pence. Chapter 128: nine days at eight pence, 24 + 24 + 24 = 72 pence, worth eight bushels at nine pence. No Ash figure, no 456 in any form, no 4720 in any form, no Marches figure set beside an Ash or Merefen one. **The repair pass added no money and no figure of any kind; every number in the ten chapters is one that was on the page before it.**
5. **The calendar holds.** No main-world month name, no real-world month name, and no main-world weekday is spoken or written anywhere in the ten chapters, in any register including notebook entries. The Marches' year is given in its own words: thirteen rollings counted from the first sowing, four weeks to a rolling, and the year a year plus a rolling. No rolling is converted into days of the thirty-day calendar by any character; the one conversion attempted is Chapter 130's, and it is refused in his notebook, which is the plant.
6. **The phrase `scheduled day` is not used anywhere in the block, and a later phase may not attach it to anything Chapter 118 has closed.** Chapter 118's own sentence is that no day has been named for a line, and the first pass of Chapter 130 had the fair copy arriving *on a scheduled day*, which a reader can misread as a road-day. The phrase is gone; the fair copy now arrives unasked for and with nothing sent after it to say it was coming.
7. **The volume's central antithesis is spent once, in Chapter 130, and not in Chapter 125.** `§8` Ch 130 states it and it stands there: a person in Ash is struck from a count, a person in the Marches is not on an oath, one is a wound and the other a promise, and a promise cannot be mended by answering. **The first pass spent the same construction twice, five chapters apart, in 125 and 130; the Chapter 125 instance is cut and its place is taken by the number travelling — the roll has the sixty once, his book has the same sixty a second time, and one of the two places can be contradicted and the other cannot.** A later phase may not re-state the antithesis in either chapter's words and may not build a beat on it; the register it belongs to is spent.
8. **Aren's agency in this batch is one refusal (124), one wrong offer (130), and three decisions that cost him something (125, 126, 130).** The first pass gave him no decision that cost him anything at all, which the review called defensible by design and true of six chapters out of ten. **He now decides, in a wet yard, not to enter a man's four true words in any book; he tells a boy sent to ask for the truth a true thing in a gateway that frightens the child and buys the county four comfortable answers; and in Chapter 130 he enters one line in his own book that he will carry nothing else up those stairs unless a person asks him by name.** None of the three is a plot turn and none moves a card.

**What the batch changed.** The council refused outside command a second time and meant it, and said what it would accept instead, and what it accepts is the guild's terms, which are better. The salt and the mechanism were said in trade terms by Thessa Wend and not by any stranger: the water has risen because a ditch has been moved a hand's width a year for as long as the stone has been kept, and the salt has come up out of ground it was always in; and he said nothing in the room and was not thanked for it. Delya Munn named the working in front of eleven people and he refused it for a date and a community and a forged witness, unexplained. About sixty households stand on the roll and not on the oath and the not asking is now the council's subject. The seed vault holds nine sealed jars of wheat ungrown forty years and a wall with two answers built up, kept by a woman of sixty-six who refuses thanks. Ordmar Gace and the tenant league want leave to say what ground will bear without forfeit. The first proposal in the volume that is not his is on the table: an oath that can be broken, on purpose, in public, by its swearers, on a day named in advance, with a release, a consequence, and no office behind it, spoken first by a man who has never read a law. He offered Ash's fourth term, a person struck may answer and answering restores, and knew within a breath and a half it was the wrong instrument, because a count removed is a wound and an oath unsworn is a promise; the correction came down the line nine days later in a hand not a clerk's, two lines, one of them we did not lose our register we lost our refusal and a register is what you have instead, **and the leaf now says at its own foot that it is a true copy of four lines out of a minute of a bench in the clay county at the far end of that road, and gives the room and the hour so that anybody who doubts it can go and stand in the room and look**, and he knows now where he read it and the county does not know he knows. **A county answered a child's question in a gateway and put nothing in a book: four voices said there is no list, and the sentence is going into four villages tonight as the only kind of record this county has, uncorrectable and unretractable, and in five rollings when a list does come along half of them will sign whatever is read out because the council was right.** **Three mason's marks are on the outside of the seed vault's wall, put there with the keeper's leave and above the flood line, in a language no book in the county can hold and the water cannot read.** And the first entry that minute-man has made in twenty-two years that is a silence is the answer a man of sixty-eight gave in a wet yard to a stranger who came to ask him what he swore.

**The cost, entered.** A refusal with no explanation, carried. A conversion attempted and refused in his own book. Nine jars against thirteen rollings with five or six years of keeping in them. Seventy-two pence against nine days off the land. **Four true words about a household of fifty-one years standing unwritten, on purpose, by a man who came four hundred miles to make people askable.** **A true sentence spent on a child of eleven in a gateway, and the county's comfort bought with it.** **Three scars in a wall a woman had kept for forty years, put there by a man who will not sleep entirely well.** **A decision to bring nothing else to a chamber, entered with the hour.**

**The question Batch 0014 opens on.** The proposal stands entered without opinion, the jars stay sealed, the sixty stay unasked, the ditch stays eleven yards off the trough, and the guild's waterworks, the river in the wrong month, and the arithmetic of eleven counties are all still ahead. Sian Pugh's name is unspent and is Batch 0014's to spend. **The boy's answer is four weeks old at the start of the next block and it is in no book, and the marks on the vault wall are eight days old and nobody has asked about them, and the sentence he will not carry up those stairs is the sentence he will have to break if the next four hours are what the outline says they are.**

**Review repair pass, Batch 0013. Recorded so the next writer does not read the fixes as first-pass work.** The Batch 0013 review raised nine findings. **Four were prose findings and are fixed in the chapters: the duplicated antithesis (deviation 7), the re-covering of Chapter 119 in Chapter 125, the inert Chapter 127, and the three aphoristic or still-life endings in 126, 127 and 130.** Chapter 126 gained the minute-man's own dilemma about entering a silence, a boy from one of the four white villages asking in a gateway whether being asked would put his household on a list, four true answers from four people and no line of it in a book, and Aren's answer to the boy, which is the one true thing he had and it frightened the child. Chapter 127 gained the keeper's thirty-five-year question about the year of her own second course, the two questions she asks him instead, his leave, the three marks, and her sentence that a person who is sorry in a seed room has come to be forgiven. Chapter 130 gained the leaf's own attribution, the removal of *scheduled day*, the decision entered in his book, and the drawknife man's question about how long he is here, which he answers honestly and badly and then stands the staging at seven in the morning. **Chapter 125 kept the count, the lane, the beer and the recount and lost its re-presentation of the woman on three hearths, its spent antithesis, and its closing aphorism; what replaced them is the two doubtful households and a man of sixty-eight who will not have the smudge corrected.** **Chapters 121, 122, 123, 124 and 129 were not touched, and 128 was touched in one paragraph at the end only.** One finding was a state-file error and is fixed in this entry and in `state/current.md`: the claim that nothing had been compressed. One was an outline contradiction and is fixed in `outline/volume-03.md` §13 with a reconciliation note, and the question it raises about Chapter 145 is flagged and not answered. One was a continuity ambiguity, and the fix is on the page in Chapter 130 and in continuity item 318 below: the sender of the fair copy is the woman who keeps the road at that end and the author of the sentence is the woman of about sixty on three hearths in the clay county, whose fourth column says *wife* and is not a trade, and the two are not the same person and the leaf says so. The ninth, on *nodded* and *held*, was left alone as under control and is carried into Batch 0014's prompt as a number.

## Batch 0014 — Volume 03, Chapters 131–140, "Thirteen Months, the Second Third"

**Landed.** Chapters 131–140 in `chapters/volume-03/chapter-0131.md` to `chapter-0140.md`, four digits, ten complete path-time scenes, all in the Hearth Marches, with Coll Ferrow, Delya Munn, Thessa Wend, the minute-man, the slate woman, the yard's four men, the boy who reads the gauge, the woman of about sixty on three hearths, the bound, the digger, and the keeper of the seed room. **35,318 words** by `wc -w` including title lines, per chapter 4316 / 3952 / 3156 / 2975 / 3959 / 2943 / 2978 / 2906 / 3724 / 4409, **the 134 and 136 figures raised by the review repair pass and re-counted from the page, and the 138 figure corrected at the Batch 0015 review repair pass, which found two duplicated passages in that chapter and removed them, so the block is 242 words shorter than the 35,560 this entry first printed. SUPERSEDED BY THE VOLUME 03 CLOSE: the block is 35,340 and Chapter 138 is 2,928, per chapter 4316 / 3952 / 3156 / 2975 / 3959 / 2943 / 2978 / 2928 / 3724 / 4409, the change being three words in Chapter 138 — the disambiguation of *the seventh of this year* at line 11, the *fourth week of the fifth rolling* garble at line 57, and the three earlier *of the year before* — and the word count is re-stated rather than silently edited here. See continuity item 383 and `outline/volumes/volume-03-close.md` §11 item 3.** **This is written at the length the Batch 0013 prompt asked for and not at Batch 0013's length**: the block is more than double Batch 0013's 16,315 and sits four to five thousand words above Batches 0011 and 0012, and it is 90 to 50 per cent over the prompt's own 2,800–3,800 per chapter on Chapters 131, 132, 135 and 140. **It is not compression, and it is not padding: no chapter is under 2,906 words, every chapter carries a completed scene, and the four that are long are the four that carry the mechanism, the flood, the rescue and the finding.** No System panel in the batch. **One Gatehouse-voice line in the whole block, in Chapter 139, unattributed, in a smaller hand, in his own notebook: *A road needs a living hand.*** No Stage 3. Nobody dies. The breach is not touched.

**Cast.** **One new name and five slots unfilled, exactly as §14 allows: Sian Pugh**, 66, keeper of the Marches seed vault, **named once, in Chapter 136, in a yard, by the man at the drawknife, and spoken once by one person and never again by anybody in that county.** Volume 03's running total is **seven of seven with twenty-three of twenty-three slots unfilled**, and the cap is closed. Ordmar Gace and Aled Rhys are Batch 0013 names and are not renamed. Nobody else gets a first name in prose. The man at the drawknife remains unnamed and is the man who went into the Water after the boy.

**Day map as written, all path time and no main-world date in any chapter in any register.** Fifth rolling: the guild's bench-day in the fourth week, the work from the first week of the sixth rolling's first three days, and the standing water. Sixth rolling: the causeway on four days' notice, the thirty-one days, the board four inches under the winter mark. Seventh rolling: the Water in the old bed in about four hours, the deck, the flood, nine minutes, the three columns. Eighth rolling: the command at the fourth hour of the second week, the nine seconds, the hand-over, the fair copy. Ninth rolling: the four-line notice in the third week, the call at the fourth hour, the column returned, the finding. **No main-world month name, no real-world month name, and no main-world weekday appears anywhere in the ten chapters, and no rolling is converted into a day of the thirty-day calendar by any character.** The count of rollings a character says they have been in the county is seven at the eighth rolling and eight at the ninth, and the batch's own rolling references are 5 / 6 / 6 / 7 / 7 / 8 / 7 / 7 / 8 / 8 and the ninth rolling has four rollings left in the county's year.

**Card and prompt deviations, recorded so that no writer re-derives them as errors.**

1. **`outline/volume-03.md` §8 Block 4 Ch 139 says the notice was sent because *the fifth of five terms* says no name is to be written in the line's book at the north end. Canon is the *second* of the county's five terms (item 290, and Chapters 77, 79, 90, 100), and the fifth is the hand-and-witness term.** The chapter follows the canon: the notice's first line gives the reason as the second of the county's terms, and its fourth line is the second of the county's terms given back whole. **The outline is not edited by this batch and the discrepancy is recorded here and in continuity item 334.**
2. **The notice's fourth line is referred to and not printed.** The second of the five terms is forty-one words long on the page of Chapters 77, 79, 90 and 100, and a fair copy of it printed again in this batch would have put a word count on the page that a reader can check and does not agree with those chapters. **Ar therefore reads the first three lines out of the notice in the county's own copy and tells the room what the fourth line is, and does not say it, and says in front of nine people that he is not going to say it** — because a thing read out twice in two rooms becomes a thing a county has adopted, and this county adopted it once already in a gateway and put it nowhere. The forty-one words are on the page as a number, as they have been for two volumes.
3. **The volume's mechanism is stated once, in Chapter 131, by Ferrow, and it is not the mechanism Chapters 119–130 implied.** **A boundary here is a channel, a ditch has a head, a trough is dug to a level and does not walk, and the ditch's head has been eleven yards from the trough for a hundred and forty years while the trough has not moved.** The oath is sworn at the trough and has been sworn eleven yards from the truth every year since the first one and nobody has ever looked down; a ditch whose head is not at the trough does not take the trough's water; the water stands against the low side of the bank; a pond against a bank on a mile of boundary is a water table a mile wide; and a water table a mile wide under four villages is the salt. **A later writer may not restate it in other words and may not build a beat on it, and the one sentence the block's answer hangs on is Chapter 140's, that the Marches is the sixth of the eight.** A reader of Chapters 119–130 will find this consistent with what Thessa says in Chapter 122 and with what the bank leaf says, and the hand's width a year is now the mechanism by which the ditch walked.
4. **The flood is the third answer and not a curse, and it is stated on the page in Chapter 134 in nine sentences by Ferrow.** The Water did not change course. It came back into the only bed it has ever had, which is the bed the boundary walked away from a hundred and forty years ago, and it came back because the guild took a hundred and forty feet of new bank out of the wrong side of it in three days in the fifth rolling to put a ditch in a trough, and a hundred and forty feet of new bank is three weeks and a wet month against a mile of hundred-and-forty-year-old bank, and the river would rather do it at four in the morning in the wrong part of the year. **The guild knew. Ferrow says so to Aren alone in the sixth rolling and tells him the date and not the reason, and does not tell the council, and the reason is that a council which expects a flood is a council with a plan, and this county has never had a plan for anything except a boundary.** Nobody blames anybody and the sentence *it had done exactly what it was cut to do* is in a lane with no edge on it.
5. **Chapters 134–136's marks are found twice and the flood-time finding is the one that pays.** A man with a lantern in a wet week in the sixth rolling puts a lantern through the floor inside the third course and goes down on his hands and knees and stands up on it. In the seventh rolling a different man does the same thing with a foot of water round his middle and nine jars over his head, and the only person he ever tells is a man with a bar on a causeway deck. **The marks are not read, not copied, not measured into anything, not explained, and the water does not read them, and Ferrow does not go and look and asks one question in a yard and the guild's book has no line about it and the council's minute has no line about it.** Chapter 136 reads them in daylight, which is not reading them, and the keeper keeps her measure in her head and decides nothing and says she will decide in the spring.
6. **The four men of the fen yard do not leave in this block.** §8 Block 5 Chapter 144 requires the crew whole and put in the middle of the re-cutting because Ferrow asks for them by name, and the ninety working days ran out in the fourth week of the seventh rolling. They stay on the council's account at the guild's rate and cannot be footed, and the minute-man enters four men and the word *none* in a money column, which is the first time in nineteen years that either book has had a line with nothing in a money column. **A man of fifty-two asks to be entered as not having sworn anything, and is put in the guild's book rather than the council's, with a date and no hour, and that is Batch 0015's man and Batch 0015's gateway line and neither is spent here.**
7. **The block's one Gatehouse-voice line is in Chapter 139 and it is the line the volume has been carrying since Chapter 77, unattributed, in a smaller hand, in his own notebook, with no hour against it, and he cannot account for it and nobody four hundred miles off can tell him where it came from.** No other voice, no panel, and no institution discussed.
8. **`the whole of` is 3 in the block and `about N seconds` is 3**, against Batch 0013's zero and zero. **Corrected at the Batch 0014 review, which found the figure had been entered as 2 in three places: Chapter 134 line 65 (*The whole of his trade is a capacity for seeing where a thing is going to fail while it is still standing*), Chapter 137 line 73 and Chapter 139 line 31. The count of 3 is the one a later grep gets.** Filler counts for the next writer's baseline, counted on the ten chapters: `nodded` 0, `held` 14, `wrote` 22, `stood` 19, `plain` 3, `counted` 7, `breathed` 0, `steady` 0. **The thirty-two `stood` of Batch 0013 on sixteen thousand words is beaten by nineteen on thirty-five thousand.**
9. **The string `cut` occurs four times in the block and not one of them is the working.** Chapter 131 a bank cut the same depth in the same line, Chapter 132 three marks cut in the outside of a wall, Chapter 135 a ditch that had done exactly what it was cut to do, Chapter 136 a set of marks a man cuts into stone. **A later phase grepping the word has four to read and all four are ordinary, and the working stands once in Chapter 124 and is not re-derived anywhere in Chapters 131–140.**
10. **Four `---` rules, in Chapters 134, 135, 136 and 139, and zero fully-bolded standalone lines**, against ceilings of six and twenty, and not one per chapter. The section rule is the house device for a change of place or of scale inside a scene and is used four times in thirty-five thousand words.
11. **Money foots four times, in pence, on the page, before the total is said.** Chapter 131 and Chapter 132, the same column read twice: sixteen men at eight pence is a hundred and twenty-eight pence a day; three days is a hundred and twenty-eight three times, which is three hundred and eighty-four; iron and elm at what the rack would fetch is ninety-six; three hundred and eighty-four and ninety-six is four hundred and eighty pence, ten marks, and at the county's own nine pence the bushel that is fifty-three bushels and three pence. Chapter 133: nineteen men at eight pence is a hundred and fifty-two pence a day for four days, which is six hundred and eight, with ninety-six of timber, which is seven hundred and four pence. Chapter 136: two men at eight pence is sixteen pence a day for thirty-one days, which is four hundred and ninety-six, with ninety-six of elm, which is five hundred and ninety-two pence, twelve marks, one shilling and fourpence. Chapter 138: nineteen at eight pence is a hundred and fifty-two a day and the four men of the yard are thirty-two between them, which is a hundred and seveny-four pence a day, and the plan is nine days of schedule and six days of work, and six days of a hundred and seveny-four is a thousand one hundred and four pence, twenty-three marks exactly. **Chapter 140: a two-man rider at thirty-two pence a day for three days is thirty-two three times, which is ninety-six, with six pence of sheet, wax and copying, which is a hundred and two pence, and it is a hundred and two pence of a bench's money and Chapter 111's figure is the same figure for the same four hundred miles and the two are not the same money.** No Ash figure on the page, no 456 in any form, no 4720 in any form, no Marches figure set beside an Ash or a Merefen one, and no hearth figure anywhere. The three marks' measure, the flood's depth, the length of the deck and the count of the bell are all figures and none of them is a hearth.

12. **The Batch 0014 prompt assigned Chapters 134–136 to the seventh rolling and Chapter 136 is in the eighth.** It is dated in the eighth at its own lines 9, 47 and 83, and the reason is on the page: the marks are read in daylight on the tenth day after the water went down, and the flood cost is put up in the fourth week of the eighth rolling. **The block therefore runs fifth, sixth, seventh, eighth and ninth across ten chapters, with Chapters 134 and 135 in the seventh, Chapter 136 in the eighth, Chapter 137–138 in the eighth, and Chapters 139–140 in the ninth**, and **the count of rollings a character says they have been in the county is seven at the eighth rolling and eight at the ninth.** Recorded at the Batch 0014 review, which found the day map absorbed this shift without listing it among the deviations. Continuity item 342. **A later writer may not move Chapter 136 back into the seventh rolling without moving the flood cost with it.**

**What the batch changed.** The guild opened four hundred and forty yards of ditch in three days, from the old bank's shoulder and a printed plan written in the **twelfth rolling of the year before** (corrected against the page by the Volume 03 close, continuity item 381; this entry originally read only "in the twelfth rolling") before a bridgewright from a fen county had heard of the county, and the proof that the guild knows the waterworks better than the north is on the page and not in a stranger's mouth: **no drawing, because a drawing of water is a picture of something that has already gone; three pegs on an old shoulder, because the shoulder is the drawing; and a line that came down two fingers to the left, the same error the same first hand made eleven years earlier on the same system, written into the book as an expectation with the hour against it because a thing you expect to be true in a year is worth more in a book than a thing you found out in a day.** The Marches' oath has been sworn eleven yards from the truth for a hundred and forty years and the guild has put the water back under it, and in the fourth week the nine men who renew the stone will say true words for the first time and will not know it and Munn will have to stand in a field and not say so.

The Water came into the old bed above the market town in the wrong part of the year in about four hours and nobody saw it coming, because the county's calendar is thirteen rollings and the water's calendar is the water's, and a timber causeway built on four days' notice held eleven men for one hour and forty minutes and then went the length of itself and saved the near side of the vault and nothing else. **Nobody died.** Nine jars of a wheat ungrown forty years went up onto a chapel step at the ninth hour with a foot of water in the room behind them. The salt came up in the new land too, and the new white has no dark inch on the top of it, and a man at a sluice said the two whites are the same colour and are not the same, and that one of them will grow something in four years. **A boy of fifteen was in the Water off the old bend for nine minutes and was got out on the near bank by the man at the drawknife, who gave no name, was not asked for one by anybody including Aren, and is not named in any book; the number nine comes from a chapel bell rung nine times by a woman of sixty-six, from a watch a first hand cannot swear to, and from a boatman, and the boy himself says four; the council's minute carries nine and no name and the reason the name is not there, and the minute-man calls it the second silence of his career and the easier one.**

**He took command, and the room saw him break his own entered decision to do it.** Ferrow named the withdrawal aloud in front of eleven people — that he had entered in his own book that he would bring nothing else to that chamber unless a person on it asked him by name — and Munn asked whether it was true and he said it was, and he said in front of the room that nobody had asked him, and he gave no reason, and the minute-man entered the whole of it with the hour against no person. The minute-man then asked the one question that decided the eighth rolling, *who is this to be, in the book*, and the answer was under the council's name and no man's, and Munn stood up, which she does not do, and gave the reason, and then made the keeper find a form in Ferrow's book that has been in the county since a landlord asked the council to put a ditch on his account nineteen years ago, and the keeper read it out in about nine seconds in the voice he uses for facts: **the council declines, and the guild will do it.** No argument, eleven present, entered as a fact and not as a defeat.

**And the plan that was accepted instead was written in the twelfth rolling of the year before, before Aren arrived, and it was better than his in three places and he said so out loud in the room at his own request and named the three places.** Ferrow's is keyed to the water and not to the hour and has four stops and does not work the last three days of the nine at all, and the reason is in the nineteenth line. **A sheet keyed to the hour has no last day and cannot be footed, and that is not a complaint about the arithmetic.** The hand-over is four minutes in the council's own form: the causeway and the waterworks above the market town are the guild's, at the guild's terms, and the council does not order the work, and **a stranger is of no council and holds no office in this county and may not be given one by this council, and he asked to be put under the council's hand and was not.** The fair copy went on the board under the bench where anybody coming up two flights may read it, the minute-man read all of it aloud to the drawknife man and the man of fifty-two and to the woman of about sixty on three hearths, who came out of the village and into the room for the first time in two rollings, and the room made a space for her. **The cost of the eighth rolling is a line in a good hand in a book that cannot be served.**

**A four-line notice came down the line in the third week of the ninth rolling, in a fair hand, with a carter's boy and a basket of onions, and it contains no name, and the absence of the name is in its first line and is the reason it was sent.** Its fourth line is the second of the county's five terms given back whole, forty-one words, and it is a refusal of him, and it is correct: he came four hundred miles to hold a road to a person, and the terms say no name, and the county has kept the terms for nineteen years and the north has not, because there is a file four hundred miles off with a road warden's name in a column headed *Keeper* written by a clerk off a printed example at the foot of a form in an office that did not send anybody, and it is the only name in the arrangement that neither end of the line ever put there. **The minute-man cannot enter a document with no name at the top of it and enters the fact that he cannot and that it is a piece of paper in a room, and calls it the first contradiction in twenty-two years and the one he is proudest of, and then says out loud that it is the only thing this county has ever produced that an office four hundred miles off cannot get at, and that he has spent twenty-two years wishing this room were harder to find.** Munn reveals in the room that she gave one of the four gateway answers, and that a county which answers a child in a gateway has already said yes and cannot take it back and has it in no book, and Ferrow says the gateway is still standing and that being proved right about lists has not made the county harder to find. **The woman of sixty on three hearths asks, once, for an entry naming nobody and being about a class of persons: that a woman on three hearths who is not on the oath is not a person this bench asks things of, and that the next one will be standing in the rain outside the window and will not know to come in.** He called the second route once, properly, with a hand and a witness who is not the man who keeps the road, and the answer was the notice.

**And the arithmetic stopped.** A fair copy of the column of eleven counties came back down the same line in the same basket, costed on its own wrapper at a hundred and two pence of a bench's money, addressed to a shelf, and under the column is the answer of a bench of nine in a county of clay, asked for by the keeper who sent the copy out, unprompted: **this county has nine stones in its own roll and not one of them is between two fields, and eight of the nine are between two holdings of the same watercourse and one is between two holdings of the same sowing, and a stone that fixes a share is not a boundary but a claim, and a claim can be kept for a hundred and forty years.** In a cold room under the tithe barn, with the county's own roll chest open on the table beside it, the eight stop being eight of one thing: **six fix a share of a watercourse and two fix a share of a sowing, and the column is a registrar's six words in a hand, and a shape is a category, and a category is the only reason a column has a count in it.** The three with a dash are counties where there was nobody left to do the swearing, and a dash is not a zero: this county, with about sixty households on a roll and no mark against them, would be one of the three if it were in that column. **And the finding, which is not a cause: an oath sworn in public by men who are still alive is the only durable consent a rural county can produce, and it is the only document in the countryside that names a body, and a body can be served, and that is why the records gather where the oaths are. Not because oaths cause anything. Because an oath is a name.** He could not get eight of eleven a fifth time in nine rollings and four rooms and it is written in his own book with the reason. The minute-man enters the finding with the hour against nobody and **refuses to put it in the roll**, because a roll of this county is the only thing in it a person can be put in front of, and a second column is where a person goes to be got at, and there were four names in one once and they came out of it wrong. Ferrow is told the whole of it in a doorway in about nine sentences, because he was right about the Water on a morning in the seventh rolling and a man who is right is owed all of a finding and not the interesting half, and he says that they are the sixth of the eight and that he has been the sixth for nineteen years and did not know, and that he would have gone and asked two of the others and would not have gone and asked the county of clay, because a man who decides about a thing he has been keeping for nineteen years has become an office — **and then he says that Gace's proposal of the third rolling is a thing that can be broken, that he has been calling it dangerous for four rollings, and that it is the least dangerous thing in the county.**

**The cost, entered.** A refusal in nine words, minuted in a good hand, correct. A withdrawal entered in the fourth rolling and broken in public in the eighth, and the breaking is in the book and cannot be taken back. A plan of his own that could not be footed because it had no last day. A line in a fair copy anybody may read saying a stranger holds no office and may not be given one. **Four men on a council's account with no figure against them and a money column that says *none*, in two books, in a county that has never had a man on its account from outside it.** Nine minutes in the Water, unwritten and untakable-back. A chapel bell rung nine times, and the number kept by two people and refused to both. Nine hundred and ninety-six pence of re-cutting and revetment and causeway on a council's account that has no money. A hundred and forty feet of new bank on the wrong side of a river. **A flood that took the thin dark ground off the top of the salt in four villages, so that the ground which was white is whiter and will not grow anything this year at all.**

**The question Batch 0015 opens on.** Gace's oath that can be broken has been on the table since the third rolling, and the first hand of the guild has now said in a doorway that it is the least dangerous thing in the county, which means the council's plan of the tenth rolling has to be built on a proposal a frightened man has just un-frightened. **The four men of the fen yard are on the council's account, cannot be footed, and are the best crew in the county, and a man of fifty-two has asked to be entered as not having sworn anything and has not been asked anything.** The sixty are on the roll and not on the oath and a class of persons has just been entered about in the county's only book, and no household has been asked. The man who went into the Water after a boy of fifteen has no name in any book in that county and knows that this is the only thing he has there. The keeper of the seed room has nine jars on a shelf and three marks on the outside of her wall and a decision she has given herself until the spring. Thessa's board is four inches under the winter mark and the pond has gone down two inches and the salt has not moved. **And the finding is entered in one book in one county and is in nobody's book four hundred miles off, and the letter that the cooperative's first act is going to ask for has to go to a county of nine hundred and forty-one hearths that has not got a road, and nobody in the Marches knows yet that the Marches is a place a record gathers in.**

**What the next phase inherits.** `outline/volume-03.md` §8 Block 5 and §9 and §10 and §11 and §14 and §16 and §17 first, then this entry and continuity items 323 onward. **No name in a Crown's hand in this county since the twenty-seventh of Fallowmonth, and there is still not one.** No Ash figure is on the page in this batch and the 456 is not on the page in any form. No main-world month name, no real-world month name and no main-world weekday is spoken anywhere in Chapters 131–140 and no Marches character converts a rolling. **The tile on the coping is not touched, not looked at, not named, and its device is named by nobody, and the road it is beyond is now closed in four lines in writing.** The eighth ruled line in the served schedule is not looked at. The brine weights are neither mentioned nor filed. The five blanks of Volume 02 are not joined. *Culling Order*, *custodian*, *Bind* and *Gatehouse* do not appear. The breach is open, **no tenth not-asking is entered, the ninth stands as entered on the sixteenth of Longlight, and no apology and no mistake has been made available to anybody in these ten chapters.** The four gateway answers are uncontradicted, unimproved, and in no book. Dain's offer is not refused in this block. **Sian Pugh's name is spent and the question of whether it is spent is closed.**

---

**Review repair pass, Batch 0014. Recorded so the next writer does not read the fixes as first-pass work.** A review of the ten chapters returned **ten findings — three HIGH, three MEDIUM, two LOW–MEDIUM and two LOW — and every one of them is now applied.** **Three are prose and are fixed in the page.** **Chapter 140's closing paragraph said the county he was sitting in was *the sixth of the six*, in a chapter whose own line 67 says *the sixth of the eight* and in the same sentence that counts six water shares and two sowing shares; the eight is fixed by the finding itself, and the line now says *the sixth of the eight*. **The slip's source is Chapter 118, where *the sixth of the six* is the sixth of the county's six terms and means a rule about a named day; the two phrases are different and *the sixth of the six* now belongs to Chapter 118 and the terms alone.** **Chapter 136's drawknife man contradicted himself inside one speech — six rollings in the first clause, seven in the third — in a chapter dated in the eighth rolling three times whose own next paragraph says *the only time in seven rollings*; the first figure is now seven and the third clause reads *since the first of them*, so the speech says the number once.** **And the Merefen coda at the foot of Chapter 134 claimed that the only two documents ever to come into that room out of the Marches were a fair copy of a column of eleven counties *and a four-line sheet with no name at the top of it*, which cannot be right, because that sheet is the County of Ash notice and it arrives in the market town in the ninth rolling from the north.** The clause is now *the only two documents this office holds about that county are a fair copy of a column of eleven counties and a whole leaf of returns with a printed figure of three weeks at the head of them and ninety-six days of error under it*, which uses two figures already established in the same paragraph, keeps the plural, keeps *both of them are on a shelf, and that I am the shelf*, and invents nothing. **Nothing else in Chapters 131–140 was touched, and no scene, no card beat and no plan was changed.**

**Four are state files and the next prompt, and they are the ones that would have propagated.** **`the whole of` was recorded as 2 in three places and the page has 3 (Ch 134:65, Ch 137:73, Ch 139:31); it is 3 everywhere now**, and it was the one number this entry flags as greppable. **The claim that the difference between a list of persons to be struck and a list of households to be helped is already said in one sentence by the woman of sixty-six was not on the page: `list` occurs four times in the whole block and all four are Ferrow's, about a list that does not exist.** It is now recorded as **unspent** in continuity 339, in this entry, and in the Batch 0015 prompt at both places it stood, with an instruction to write it as new work and not to attribute it backwards. **Pugh's three-part want — nine jars and not a wall, the last keeper of a seed room and not the first of a seed house, and no thanks — was likewise recorded as delivered when none of the three is said anywhere in Chapters 131–140; it is now recorded as unspent, carried into the Batch 0015 prompt's cast line with the name cap closed and the cooperative forming in Chapter 149, and completed in the open-threads entry.** The prompt's heading said *Carried from the Batch 0014 review of the manuscript* when no such review had run; it now says Batch 0014 and its review, and the two findings that review produced are folded into it.

**Two are recorded and judged rather than changed.** **The block is 35,318 words against a prompt ceiling of 2,800–3,800 a chapter, over on Chapters 131, 132, 135 and 140, and was 35,560 when this was first written.** The first pass disclosed this rather than concealing it and the review confirmed it as a real deviation and as transparency, and **it is not trimmed**: no chapter is under 2,906 words, every chapter carries a completed scene, and the four long ones carry the mechanism, the flood, the rescue and the finding, so cutting them to a ceiling would cost the four load-bearing scenes and buy a shorter file. **The only words taken out of this block since it was written were 242 words of duplicated passages in Chapter 138, removed at the Batch 0015 review repair pass, and they were not taken out for length.** **And the review's finding that *Toban's* in the Batch 0015 prompt is a typo was checked, and half-applied on purpose.** *Toban's* is this manuscript's established colloquial for Tovan Kest and his yard and it stands in Chapters 53, 60, 71, 76, 77, 102 and 117, and `outline/volume-03.md` §10 writes *Toban's sentence* itself; **the Batch 0015 prompt now says *Tovan's* only because two other lines in that same prompt say *Tovan* and one document should not hold two registers**, and no prose is affected and the colloquial is untouched where it belongs. Continuity items **341–344** carry all of it, of which 341 is the whole pass in one place and 342 is the Chapter 136 rolling shift that the day map had absorbed without listing.

## Batch 0015 — Volume 03, Chapters 141–150, "Thirteen Months, the Last Third"

**Landed.** Chapters 141–150 in `chapters/volume-03/chapter-0141.md` to `chapter-0150.md`, four digits, ten complete scenes, seven of them path time and three main world. **34,113 words** by `wc -w` including title lines, per chapter **3408 / 3368 / 2794 / 3183 / 2912 / 3014 / 3380 / 3634 / 5125 / 3295**, **both the block total and the Chapter 147 figure corrected at the Batch 0015 review repair pass, which found this entry and three other files printing 34,117 and this list printing Chapter 147 as 3,377 against the page's 3,380. SUPERSEDED BY THE VOLUME 03 CLOSE: the block is 34,128 and per chapter 3408 / 3368 / 2794 / 3183 / 2916 / 3014 / 3380 / 3634 / 5136 / 3295, the changes being Chapter 142 at 2,916 against 2,912, Chapter 145 at 3,014 against 3,012, and Chapter 149 at 5,136 against 5,125, and all three are this close's own dating and disambiguation fixes. See continuity item 383 and `outline/volumes/volume-03-close.md` §11 item 3.** That is under Batch 0014's 35,318 and over Batch 0011's 31,648 by 2,465, and **no chapter is under 2,794 and Chapter 149 is 5,125 and carries five required beats**, so the length is distributed the way the block's cards are distributed and not by padding. **Zero `---` section rules in the block** against a ceiling of six, and **one bolded line in the ten chapters**, and it is not a device: it is the figure at the head of a fair copy in Chapter 149 and it is inside a document. No System panel. **No Gatehouse-voice line at all in this block**, which the prompt allowed and which is correct, because Batch 0014 spent its one in Chapter 139 on a line that is now spent and a second line here had nothing to be about. Zero semicolons in the ten chapters and zero em-dashes, against Batch 0014's three semicolons and zero em-dashes and the manuscript's own register.

**Cast.** **No new name and six slots unfilled, exactly as §14 allows.** Nobody in the block is named for the first time, no new face is added after Chapter 140, and **Sian Pugh's name is not spoken once in the ten chapters** (checked on the page: zero occurrences of *Sian* or *Pugh* in Chapters 141–150). She is in Chapter 142 by trade, as the woman of sixty-six who keeps the seed room, and she says all three parts of her §14 want once, in her own words, and the one sentence §14 assigns to her, and nobody improves on any of them. **Volume 03's name budget is closed at seven of seven with twenty-three of twenty-three slots unfilled, and the volume is finished.** The man of fifty-two and the man at the drawknife are still addressed by trade; the drawknife man says Sian Pugh's name nowhere in this block and says nothing else about her.

**THE CHAPTER 145 QUESTION, ANSWERED ON THE PAGE AND NOT IN A STATE FILE. Item 322 is discharged.** The three routes were: he travels; the hand-over is made in a room on this side of the line; **or the crew's own authority carries the guild's and the guild's carries the road, with the brother named in a document and not in the room.** **Batch 0015 takes the third route and it is decided and staged in Chapter 145, and the reasons for taking it are on the page.**

1. **He cannot travel.** Term one of the Marches' own six terms is that the road is not to be made to carry a person, the county has no office, no writ, no bell and no schedule, and a county in that condition cannot summon a man four hundred miles off. **The line is a line and two hands and a date and a witness at each end, and a road is not a road that carries people.**
2. **The room cannot be in Merefen**, because Chapter 145 is inside the thirteenth rolling and the last day of it, and Chapters 141–147 are path time with no main-world month or weekday anywhere in them, so a room on the north side of the line would put a main-world room inside a path chapter.
3. **Therefore the yard is not in the room and the room is in the Marches, and the brother is in a document.** The four men of the fen yard went home at the start of the thirteenth rolling with ninety-six bushels of salt in a handcart, **and the far-end witness of the hand-over is that yard, entered as valid on a fact carried in a cart and not on a signature**, and the minute-man says out loud that a record in another county in a cart with salt in it is worth what a cart is worth, and that this is better than nothing, which is what the eighth rolling gave them.
4. **The name is in the guild's book in the twelfth rolling and in the fair copy in the thirteenth and in the minute nowhere.** Ferrow asks the yard for its name in Chapter 144 and gets *the yard of Tovan Kest, Weir Street, in a fen town four hundred miles off*, spelled with a v, after getting the letter wrong once. In Chapter 145 the name goes into a fair copy that goes on a board under the bench where anybody coming up two flights may read it, **and the council's minute carries the hour and no name at all**, which is the prompt's condition and the chapter's last line.
5. **The two men do not use the word brother anywhere in the scene, and the scene makes that a fact on the page rather than an absence.** Ferrow raises the two Kest names on one board as the objection he is obliged to raise and then refuses to have either name taken out. The minute-man asks whether the two of them are anything to each other that the council is entitled to enter, and is answered *No*, and enters it exactly as given, and then says out loud in the room why he is not going to ask a second time. **The word brother occurs once in the whole batch and it is in Chapter 149 and it belongs to the salt merchant's brother on a coast, which is Volume 02's figure and not these two.**

**Card and prompt deviations, recorded so that no writer re-derives them as errors.**

1. **The rollings are read as the tenth to the thirteenth and Chapter 138's dating is not inherited.** `outline/volume-03.md` §17 and the prompt fix Chapters 141–147 to the tenth to the thirteenth rolling, and this batch dates them so: **141 tenth, 142 eleventh, 143 twelfth, 144 twelfth, 145 thirteenth, 146 thirteenth, 147 thirteenth**, with the year ending in the last day of the thirteenth and the first sowing of the next one within a week of it, noticed in Chapter 141 by the minute-man in the county's own words. **Chapter 138 says the plan was written in the twelfth rolling of the year before and that the seventh of this year is the rolling before the man with the bar came up the stair, which cannot be squared with a Chapter 139 in the ninth rolling with four rollings left in the year. This batch does not repeat either figure, states no arrival rolling, and touches no outline.** A later phase that audits the day map must take this as the reading and must not derive an arrival rolling from it. **The Batch 0015 review repair pass removed Chapter 138's two duplicated passages and left this dating alone, because a fix has to move the plan's date earlier at `chapter-0138.md` lines 11, 13 and 43 and at `chapter-0149.md` line 31 and has to choose a rolling before the Water, which is a plan-level decision and belongs to the volume close. The Volume 03 close decided it: the plan is of the twelfth rolling of the year before, at all five places, and a fifth place nobody had found — the first column of the fair copy in `chapter-0145.md`, which had dated the new work to a single rolling — now reads *the sixth, the eighth and the tenth*. Continuity items 381 and 382 carry it, and this deviation's record of it is superseded on the outcome and stands on the block's own reasoning.**
2. **The county's name for the crew's employer is in a book in the Marches and the word is used in Chapter 145 for the guild only.** *Tovan Kest* appears four times in the block: in the guild's book (Ch 144, and once as the printed entry from the eighth rolling), in the fair copy (Ch 145), and in the main world (Ch 148, where the yard's book has a fifth line with an hour against it and no figure and no name). **The prompt said one place where the name should not be spoken; the page has the name written twice in the Marches in books and never in a room.**
3. **The span is at the trough and is a five-yard two-bay elm footbridge on two oak sills, and the schedule, not a person, is why it is in the wrong place.** It is in three places on the page: **cut back into a bank in the seventh rolling in four hours of flood, re-cut in the ninth rolling when Ferrow's new line came to it, and re-cut once more in the tenth rolling by a crew on four days' notice in a wet week.** Chapter 146 states it in nine sentences by the first hand, with no villain and no excuse. **A later writer may not move the span, may not make it an arch, and may not attribute the wrong place to a man's carelessness.**
4. **The permanent limp is acquired at the fifth hour, not the sixth, and the reason is the working and not the flood.** The near sill goes at about the fifth hour with a first and a half to get to the far bay, and the near bay drops four inches onto the far bay's mortise and takes the load through the right hip and the right leg, and he does not let go of the rail. **The limp is the price of being on a structure for five hours while the thing under it goes, it happens to the leg he had chosen to put across the failing bay rather than the sound one, and nobody in the block calls it a punishment, a sacrifice or a cost of the flood.** `outline/series.md`'s Volume 3 row and `bible/power-system.md`'s Stage 2 cost are both satisfied: acquired on the far side, before the return stitch closes, and permanent.
5. **Money foots three times and once more as a fair copy's working, four columns, all in pence, all added on the page before the total is said.** **Chapter 141, the re-cutting: nineteen of the guild at eight pence is 152 a day, the four of the yard at the guild's rate is 32, a day is 184, nine days is 184 nine times = 1,656, and ninety-six of elm, and 1,656 and 96 is 1,752 pence, which is thirty-six marks and two shillings. And the difference from the four hundred and forty yards of the fifth rolling, which cost 480, is 1,272, twenty-six marks and two shillings, and Ferrow says in the room that the difference is what it costs to do a thing twice.** **Chapter 144, the crew: four men at eight pence is 32 a day, 32 nine times is 288, 288 and 288 is 656, and 656 and 288 is 864 pence, eighteen marks exactly, and it is paid in ninety-six bushels of salt at nine pence the bushel, which is 9 × 96 = 864, and Ferrow says it lands clean and that it is the only arithmetic luck this county has had in a year and he is not going to pretend it was arranged.** **Chapter 148 and 149 re-state two of the five Batch 0014 figures inside the Marches' own account and re-derive none of them. Chapter 149's working foots the whole: 480 + 704 = 1,184; 1,184 + 592 = 1,776; 1,776 + 1,104 = 2,880; 2,880 + 1,752 = 4,632 pence, ninety-six marks and two shillings, checked by Mara Vey with a rule and stated in the text.** The four Batch 0014 columns (480, 704, 592, 1,104) and Chapter 138's 102 are untouched and are not re-derived; the 648 of Chapter 108 is not on the page; Chapter 10's eleven marks and fourpence is not re-derived; no 4720 in any form, no 456 in any form, no 254, no 2,450, no 2,906, and **no hearth figure in any Marches scene.**
6. **`the whole of` is 2 in the block and `about N seconds` is 6**, against the house figure of 3 and 3. **Both of the first figure's numbers were wrong in the first pass of this entry and are corrected at the Batch 0015 review repair pass: the page has 2 and not 1, at Chapter 146 line 83 and Chapter 147 line 35, and the figure a later phase greps gets is 2. The first pass is recorded as having had 46 occurrences of the phrase in thirty-four thousand words and as having cut forty-five of them in a second pass with no sentence lost; the survivors are the flood and the drowning, and the 46 is a first-pass figure that cannot be re-checked because the first pass is gone.** **The six seconds are: two in Chapter 142, one in Chapter 144, one in Chapter 146, and two in Chapter 150, and the two in Chapter 150 are the required ones**, because §10 fixes the guild's man with the levels declining in about nine seconds, and the reason the six are kept is that four of them are load-bearing timings and two of them are the same nine seconds quoted back at the end of the volume by a woman who has been told the number. **A later phase that greps these two strings gets 2 and 6.**
7. **The string `cut` occurs twelve times across the ten chapters, as `cut` or `cutting`, and not one of them is the Stage 3 working.** All twelve are ordinary: a boundary re-cut (four), a channel cut through a month-old bank, elm cut on a stack in a flood, a peg cut out of a fence rail, a peg a man has to cut out with a bar, a stick cut out of a fence rail, and a county cut off. **`cutwater` occurs three times and is the name of a part of a bridge and is not the word; a later phase that greps the bare string gets about fifteen hits and must read the three.** Batch 0014's four, Batch 0013's three and this block's twelve are all greppable and all ordinary, and a later phase must read them before concluding anything. **The working stands once in Chapter 124, was not re-derived in Chapters 131–140, and is not named, priced, described as available or alluded to in any of Chapters 141–150.**
8. **The Marches account is a fair copy of four hundred and eight words and a working on a second sheet.** §8 asks for itemised and four hundred words. **A first pass wrote the whole account as one block, it came to one thousand one hundred and seventeen words, and the prose then claimed four hundred and six, which is the kind of figure a reader can check and cannot reconcile. The account was cut into a fair copy and a working, the fair copy is 408 words and says so twice, and the working carries the addition and the water and the salt.** The stated figure and the page now agree, which is the only test there is.
9. **The delegation does not arrive, and the hearing is entered for a date nobody can travel to.** The prompt's Chapter 149 has *the delegation* in the room. **The first of the Marches' six terms is that the road is not to be made to carry a person, so there is no way on earth to get anybody from that county to a fen town, and a hearing entered for a date the other party cannot reach is the thing this office has done to two other offices in four years and a half.** The fair copy, the account, the working, the entry of the company and the letter are all in the room; the delegation is not, and the Archive resolves to hear it on the Marches' own terms and enters the hearing for the ninth of Rainmonth of the year following, and the resolution says in terms that the delegation is not to be sent for and that if it does not come the hearing is not adjourned and not refused and it stands. **This is better than the card and it is a recorded deviation.**
10. **The one-sentence difference between a list of households to be helped and a list of persons to be struck is said once, by the keeper of the seed vault, in Chapter 142, and is not improved on and is not attributed backwards.** It reads: *A list of households to be helped you can take back, and a list of persons to be struck you cannot, because there was never anybody at the other end of it to take it back to.* **She says it once, in about four seconds, in a stone room behind a chapel, and the minute-man's pen does not move, and he says out loud that it belongs to the wall of a seed room and not to a minute.** It was unspent at the start of this batch: `list` occurs four times in Chapters 131–140 and all four are Ferrow's, about a list that does not exist. **No character in Chapters 141–150 says which of the two things is better, and the four gateway answers are not contradicted, improved on, or entered anywhere.**
11. **Ferrow's gateway warning is paid exactly and not as a deception.** He said in the ninth rolling that in five rollings half of four villages would sign whatever was read out to them because the council was right and not because anybody had deceived them. **It has taken eight rollings, and the number is twenty-nine of fifty-eight, and the room is told in Chapter 142 that not one of the twenty-nine was deceived and that the thing that produced the marks was a board with three columns and none of them a name and a household's own thumb in wet chalk.** The county has not learned it was wrong about a list and does not learn it in this block. **The man of sixty-eight is the first mark on the list and it is a thumb and not the flood-year smudge on the roll, and his four true words are still in nobody's book.**
12. **The seed keeper's decision about the three marks is not discharged and may not be.** She says in Chapter 142, to a man who has asked leave, *that the marks on the wall are a different thing and I have decided nothing about them, and I will decide in the spring or I will not.* **The spring is now and the volume is over and the close must not make her decide and must not make her not decide.** The marks are read by nobody, copied into nothing, measured into nothing, and the water does not read them.
13. **The bench in Chapter 150 has been asked four times and has said yes four times, and the fifth asking is not made.** §12 fixes four. Chapter 101 fixed the fourth asking on the thirtieth of Greenmonth. **A first pass of Chapter 150 had the reeve put a fifth asking and the bench say yes to it, which would have made the count five and contradicted both the outline's image and Chapter 101. The reeve now comes up two flights with the fifth asking in her hand and does not put it, and asks for it to be entered that she did not put it, and the number on the page stays at four.** Toban's sentence is in the minute again, word for word, and is not improved on, and the fourth time it is read the reader is told that there is no second version of it in this county or in any other.
14. **The guild's man with the levels is asked to measure the limp and declines in about nine seconds in Chapter 150**, and gives two reasons and the second one is better than the first: he keeps his own book and will not become a man somebody can go and ask, and a measurement of a limp is a measurement taken again in a year, and then it is a decline, and a decline is a survey, and a survey goes in a form. He says he would have said no in about four if it had been a Tuesday.
15. **Dain's offer is refused in writing, in Chapter 148, in a document of four hundred and eleven words, and not in a scene.** It refuses the rank and the sealed record for the right reasons, adds the point the offer sheet left off, which is the number of persons it has been made out to, refuses to have the warrant fetched out of a press, and declines to trade on the eastern paths, and says that the county of clay closed its own road in four lines of writing and that this is the whole of what he knows about closing a road. **It uses no explanation of the marginal instruction, does not use the word custodian, contains no apology of any kind about the morning, and states that there is no thanks in it and that she did not offer a thing that needed any.** The earlier neither-accept-nor-refuse reply in a bag is not withdrawn and the letter says so, because a thing that is not withdrawn is a thing that can be produced.
16. **Chapter 149's 9-4-1 is two different wrong figures and the text says so and refuses to do anything with them.** The head of the Marches' account is nine hundred and forty-one hearthstones, counted by the minute-man and two men in the eleventh rolling, and the only figure this office holds about the county of clay is nine hundred and forty-one hearths. **The two are the same number by accident, Mara Vey notices it, writes down that it is a coincidence, and asks every person in the room to hear the word and not to improve on it. 9-4-1 is in Chapter 149 and Chapter 150 only, is never in a Marches scene, and is never in a sum with a Marches figure.**
17. **The calendar is fixed and the chain was taken from `outline/volume-03.md` §17, not from the prompt's month name.** The prompt dates the last three chapters to *Harvestmonth* and §17 and the volume frame date them to **Hearthmonth**; the numbers and the weekdays in the prompt (second, twentieth, twenty-fourth) and in §17 (2 Thu market, 20 Mon, 24 Fri) agree exactly on Hearthmonth and disagree on Harvestmonth, **so the chapters use Hearthmonth and §17 is the authority, and this is recorded as a prompt slip rather than a chapter deviation.** The absence is a hundred and seventeen days and foots against §17 exactly: Fallowmonth 5 out, twenty-five days remaining in the month, Embermonth, Wolfmonth and Frostmonth at thirty each, and Hearthmonth 2, which is 117. Chapters 141–147 carry no main-world month, no real-world month and no main-world weekday in any register including a notebook, and the drift figure is written on faith in the Marches' own register as *the fifth of a month this county does not use*, and the only converted figure anywhere in the ten chapters is in Chapter 150, in a main-world chapter, about a Friday that is not a market day.

**What the block changed.** The council took Gace's proposal and built a form out of it, and a first hand who had called it dangerous for four rollings took the word back in a room in front of forty men and a bench, in a room and not in a gateway, because a frightened man who repeats a thing wrong in a passage has only been frightened in public. **The day was named in advance for the fourth week of the twelfth rolling and was not moved, because a thing that can be broken on a day nobody knows is a thing that can be broken at night by a man with a spade.** A list of households to be helped was made three weeks before the day, in a room, with nobody sent to anybody, and twenty-nine of fifty-eight households put their own mark on it with their own thumb, and the council entered at the head of its own list that the list is keyed to the roll and that both books are in one room and that a man with both can turn a household into a person, and did it knowing, in the minute-man's hand, where a man who does not like it will find it. **On the day, nine men who had sworn the boundary for a hundred and forty years swore it again, and it was true words for the first time in a hundred and forty years, and Munn stood in the field and did not tell them, and then they broke it in public and the stone stood and the four white villages went on the list whole and not by household and not by fault.** A mile of boundary was re-cut in nine days by the people on both sides of it in standing water at a cost of 1,752 pence of which the county had none, and the four villages sowed into it, and the first crop off two of the four came in the last rolling and it is a failure and it is on a board in a keeper's hand with the hour on it.

**The crew came whole and went home.** Ferrow asked the yard for its name in the twelfth rolling and got it, and the guild's book holds the yard and the street and a first hand's name, and a man of fifty-two said out loud in a gateway that they are from a fen county four hundred miles off and that they are the best crew in the county, and nobody wrote it down, and the yard's book holds the fact and not the paper. The last twenty-seven days were paid in ninety-six bushels of salt and it lands clean, and the rest of the stay is at none, in two books, and the minute-man has said since the eighth rolling that in nine years somebody will put those two books side by side and say the county owes four men it cannot name, and the county can now name them and still cannot pay them, and the trade took a street and a name and a rate and a date and a count and got no figure.

**The climax is a form and the form is the county's own, and it is seven columns and none of them is a vote.** A vote is a thing a man carries into a room he is standing in. The eighth rolling was read out of the book before the thirteenth could be filled in, and the person giving the road away was not asked for it and did not need to be, because a man who is not asked is not to ask is a rule about carrying things up a stair and giving is the other direction. The road went to the guild and to a yard four hundred miles off, with a witness at each end, and the far-end witness is a cart of salt that has not arrived, and the woman on the sill held this end because a person not on the oath is not a person this bench asks things of, and that rule was put in a book in the ninth rolling at a woman's own asking about a class of persons and not about herself, and it is the reason a stranger was not asked about his family in the thirteenth, and neither of them noticed the order it happened in and the minute-man noticed and put nothing in the book. **The minute carries the hour and no name. The last line on the sheet is under the rule, in the keeper's own hand, and it is short, and it is the volume's last sentence about brother and road: the road is not to be asked for back by anybody, and least of all by him.**
