· `thirty-six` **0** in the block and **2** volume-wide, the two being a pence intermediate at `204:31` and the thirty-sixth day of the city's count at `238:5`, and **neither of them is the forbidden figure** · `Sunday` **0** in the block and **46** volume-wide, all in Chapters 201 to 210 where the crossing out is on a Sunday · filler as whole words: `nodded` 0, `held` 3, `wrote` 17, `stood` 28, `plain` 1, `counted` 10, `breathed` 0, `steady` 0 · the reporting clause *have said since that* **63** on the exact six-word phrase and **64** on the looser `have said since`, and **272 and 277** volume-wide · the two-line unattributed statements **4** at `209:58`, `214:9–10`, `227:51–52` and `237:67–68` · **a per-line bold-parity check across every line of all two hundred and fifty chapter files returns zero, and it was run for this close and not inherited, and a duplicate-paragraph sweep across the fifty chapters on the basis *blank-line-separated paragraphs excluding `---` of twelve words or more* returns one instance, item 648.**

**The eleven findings, and every one of them is on the page.** **Five ledger figures the Batch 0005 review repair pass did not find, and one of them is the method's own result:** the bolded line-openings are **108** on either of the two reasonable bases and four files say **107**, per chapter 10 / 11 / 12 / 10 / 12 / 11 / 8 / 14 / 11 / 9; the volume has **four** two-line unattributed statements and the ledger says **one** System panel; the `Sunday` figure of zero is block-scoped and true and any volume-wide use of it is wrong; the month-and-weekday sweep returns eleven distinct tokens and the token the Batch 0005 entry dropped is `Harvestmonth`; and the `---` rules at 45 sit four words from a figure of 107 that is wrong. **Five page figures nobody had counted:** the refusal count at `214:3` is nine on the sixth day and the page's own arithmetic says eight before the ninth on the nineteenth; `226:31` calls a man of sixty-one a man of sixty-six; a page dated the fourteenth of Harvestmonth is called thirty-five days old on the eighth of Embermonth and the carried thirty-day month makes it fifty-four; `231:35`, `231:39`, `232:69` and `233:19` say forty days where the city's own count gives thirty-one, thirty-three and thirty-five, **in the same chapter and on the same day as four instances of thirty-five**; and `204:67` has a main-world leg sound *since June*, and there is no June in this kingdom. **And one paragraph on the page twice**, at `203:33` and `245:41`, in the same room, forty-two chapters apart. **All eleven are recorded and none is repaired, and the five page figures are carried to Volume 06 as figures a batch must not treat as canon.** §2 of the close.

**The two plan figures it corrected, and the third it did not.** **`outline/volume-05.md` §9's *the third refusal in a document in four volumes* is corrected to three**, on the page at `250:21` and the chain footed at item 643. **§8 Block 5 and §10's *a bench of nine is asked a seventh thing* are both corrected to the eighth**, on the page's own arithmetic at `245:37` and the count a clerk entered on a page under that stair. **A third instance of the same ordinal is in §11's hand-off and is left standing, because the instruction bounded the correction to two places and said correct nothing else in that file, and a close that widens its own brief to fix a third instance of a figure it has been told to fix twice is a close that has decided the plan document is wrong in a way its owner has to decide.**

**The five-block audit, and the deviation counts counted on the page by counting the numbered list in each entry and not by reading the prose around it.** **Twenty-five for Chapters 201 to 210, twenty-seven for 211 to 220, twenty-five for 221 to 230, ten for 231 to 240 and eleven for 241 to 250 — ninety-eight recorded deviations across the volume.** Two blocks also recorded a counted review-repair list: Batch 0001's six, of which five were applied and one recorded, and Batch 0005's thirteen; and Batch 0004's fourteen at continuity item 619. **The instruction for this close said only that the count is large and told the close to quote it from the files. It is ninety-eight, and the columns are 25 / 27 / 25 / 10 / 11.**

**The two devices above the house baseline, both counted and neither hidden.** `about` at 574 splits into two blocks inside Batch 0005 and the split is the finding: **the five document chapters run a mean of 76.2 a chapter and the five scene chapters a mean of 38.6, and every one of the second five is at or under the Volume 04 whole-volume ceiling of forty-five**, and the reason is that a tray, a file, a press room, a lane and a minute are a block in which the hedge *about four people in that building have said* is the reporting, and sixty-six of them were dropped from five files by a bounded substitution that deleted no clause and moved no fact. `nine` at 5.80 per 1,000 is the lowest of the volume's five blocks and the reason is on the page: **Batch 0004's block was inside a city of about nine thousand people that keeps a count of days, and this block is a county with about four thousand people in it, and the volume is closing in a town that is not a city.**

**The money.** **Nine columns in the volume, all added in pence, all footed on the page before the total is said** — `204:31` at forty-two, `211:79` at five, `212:51` at four, `221:33` at four, `229:13` at five, `231:47` at nine, `231:55` at three and separated, `246:11` at four, `246:17` at forty-eight and stopped. **The two prices of the same thing are never added anywhere**, and the first draft of the hardest one performed the addition it was forbidding, in the paragraph that forbids it, and contradicted its own closing clause, and was repaired.

**The three Series resolutions, checked as a count.** Three delivered, none of them a gift, a rescue, an improvement or a conversion. **And a close that reports the third of them as *he learned to accept a refusal* has reported the sentence and missed the woman**, and the third of her three speeches is a refusal of kindness and the reason is that a kind thing said at that table is the most expensive thing in the building and she is not going to spend one sentence of it on a thing nobody asked her for.

**What the close did not do.** It did not answer the number of persons the office would not print on an offer, and it does not print it. It did not reconcile the five refusal figures and added no two of them and added no refusal count to a figure of persons. It did not turn the four prints into thirty-six — **and it corrected its own instruction on the point: the figure is gone and the string survives twice in two other senses, and the block ledger's zero is correct on its block scope.** It did not add about four hundred doors to about four hundred doors. It did not decide which county the older list of doors is in, **and it says so, and it carries the tension rather than settling it, and §7 item 18 of the close says why.** It did not decide the keeper's decision about three marks on a seed vault, or anything about nine bushels of barley, or the four hundred miles of shut road. It did not print or explain the marginal instruction. It did not name the city, narrate it, summarise it, sentimentalise it, or send a post to it. It did not explain the mechanism, the roadmark, the seam, the tile or the bell. It did not ask the keeper of sixty-one a question. It did not join the child's mark to the seam or to either mark in Merefen, and it did not name the child. It did not cut, did not open a path, did not worsen the limp, and it did not name the antagonist. **And it closed no thread: the twenty-five in Batch 0005's hand-off and the seventeen Batch 0004 carried are all still open, and the close's own eleventh opened item is the finding that a city's refusal went out of it as a figure and a figure cannot be refused, and that is the sentence `outline/volume-06.md` is built on.**

**The device ledger of the volume, with the basis of every figure named in both columns, and the three the close added to it.** The three are the bolded line-openings at 108, the unattributed two-line statements at four, and the volume-wide `Sunday` at 46 against a block figure of 0. **And the standing rule they add is three sentences: count the block you are describing and not only your own; state the basis of every figure in both columns, because a case-sensitive and a case-insensitive count of the same word in the same ten files differ by three and the difference is three sentence-initial capitals; and for every device total also run a count of the raw lines that produce it, which is a different measurement and which is the one that catches an undercount.**

**The question Chapter 251 opens on. It is a room.** **A body corporate in a fen county that has spent four years and a half refusing to be a body corporate is named in a proceeding, and a hearing that has been standing since before a bell was rung comes round on the ninth of Rainmonth, and the office that made an offer it had refused in four sheets has begun a public proceeding about a crossing it has never been to, and a figure of persons has gone out in a bag against a district that came out of a sheet. The room is convened about a refusal and the room is the same shape as the thing that made the refusal, and the answer the volume is built on is that a room can hear a thing once, a person can be asked twice, and a refusal that has to be entered in a room in order to be refused has stopped being a refusal and become a finding — and the second half of the answer is that a cut made on a forged record is a cut made on somebody's lie, and the volume's argument is not the cut, it is what the lie costs the person the lie was about.** It is a room and not a city and not a kerb and not a shelf.

## Volume 05 close review repair pass — six findings, five applied, one ruled out

**This was a repair, not a phase.** The close wrote no chapter prose and this pass wrote none; it made no line-level correction to any of the two hundred and fifty chapters in Volumes 01 to 05, which are closed prose, and it created no prompt, because the close phase had already written its one prompt at `workspace/volume-06/batch-0001/PROMPT.md` and the phase system requires exactly one per phase. It touched no controller file.

**The review returned six findings. Five were applied and one was ruled out of order, and the ruling is recorded here at length because a later reader who takes the review's word for it would corrupt arithmetic that was already right.** Every figure below was recomputed from day 0 rather than inherited.

**1. The year labels contradicted the outline's own turn rule, and this was the serious one.** `outline/volume-06.md` §17's table has turned the year at day 156, the first of Thawmonth, and its closing note says it labels day 150 YR 315 and day 390 YR 316, and the block prompt says to print YR 316 after the turn. But the header, all five block headers, §15 item 19 and §16 item 5 all printed YR 315 on dates past the turn, and §16 item 5 printed the hearing at day 284 as YR 315. **A writer following the outline and a writer following the prompt would have printed different years out of the same binding document, and both documents are handed to the next batch.** Counted from day 150 on the carried thirty-day month with the year turning after Goatmonth: day 150 is the twenty-fifth of Goatmonth YR 315, day 155 is the thirtieth of Goatmonth, day 156 is the first of Thawmonth and the first day of YR 316, and every date from there to the volume's last day is YR 316. **Every post-turn label is now YR 316, in nine places plus the two state files that carried the hearing.** No day number, no month, no weekday and no block boundary moved. **The correction is not cosmetic, because the hearing's own arithmetic demanded it:** §16 item 5 had the hearing at day 284 and had also said it falls one hundred and thirty-four days after Volume 05's last day, and a day 284 that is only one hundred and thirty-four days after day 150 cannot be in the year the volume ended in. The reviewer's recomputation of that one hundred and thirty-four confirmed it, and only the year label was wrong. The deeper cause is recorded rather than fixed: `outline/volume-05.md` §17 labels its own day 0 YR 314 and the fifty chapters label it YR 315, the two conventions differ by exactly one year everywhere, and the older hearing reasoning was computed on §17's anchor while this volume follows the prose.

**2. Four of five block frames were off by one.** Blocks 2, 3 and 4 read forty-one and forty-two inclusive and Block 5 read seventy-seven and seventy-eight. Each of the first four blocks is forty days elapsed and forty-one inclusive and Block 5 is seventy-six and seventy-seven. The totals were right — forty-one four times over and seventy-seven foot at the two hundred and forty-one — so the error was in the per-block phrasing and not in the sum, which is the signature failure this project already knows it is prone to. Corrected in all four, and Block 1's own forty and forty-one was already right. **The prompt carried the same slip for Block 1 and contradicted itself in the next clause** — forty-two inclusive and then the first forty-one days — and is corrected.

**3. Two of the phase's own files failed the bold-parity sweep the phase mandates.** `outline/volume-06.md` line 187 had eleven bold tokens: a span closed inside a sentence rather than at its end, which swallowed the following span, and one orphan marker at the line's end. `state/current.md` line 17 ended on an orphan marker. Both are fixed and both files now pass, with the orphan on the 201-to-250 chapter-list line removed rather than paired, because every sibling clause on that line bolds only its chapter numbers and pairing it would have bolded a whole clause that its neighbours leave plain.

**4. Mudmonth was declared unfixed in the same document whose table fixed it.** §15 item 18 said its length was still unfixed and §17's prose and one table row repeated it, while §17's own three anchors — Mudmonth 1 at day 186, Mudmonth 26 at day 211, Greenmonth 1 at day 216 — admit only thirty days. All four places now say thirty and record that the table fixed it before the prose admitted it. The prompt had already resolved it to thirty, which is the shape of finding 1: the prompt quietly settling what the outline left open.

**5. Two soft attributions, both of the kind that make a batch name the wrong person.** The Block 4 card gave one clerk "a post in a day-book that has been wanted for two years and has not been signed," which is two women: Alish Renn's want, which is the two-years part, and Sarn Oyelaran's unsigned warrant for a third post, which is the unsigned part. The card now gives each to its own person and forbids the merge and forbids the copyist being given the keeper's key. And §13's Batch 0004 row called Perry Hask the first person in the book to be handed a document that joins two things by accident, while §5.2 and §8 Block 1 place that accident in Block 1 as a file of one sheet opened beside a standing hearing, which is a filing and not a hand-over and which nobody made and nobody was handed. The row now says the accident he is handed is not the one that turned the volume, and that there is no person who made it.

**6. RULED OUT, and nothing was changed.** The review held that `outline/volumes/volume-05-close.md` §2.8 miscounts the age of a page, saying fifty-four days elapsed and fifty-five counting both ends where the true figures are fifty-three and fifty-four. **The close is right and the review was out by one.** On the carried thirty-day month and the month order in `bible/terminology.md` — Thawmonth, Mudmonth, Greenmonth, Longlight, Rainmonth, Harvestmonth, Fallowmonth, Embermonth, Wolfmonth, Frostmonth, Hearthmonth, Goatmonth — the count from the fourteenth of Harvestmonth to the eighth of Embermonth is sixteen days to the thirtieth of Harvestmonth, one more into the first of Fallowmonth, thirty through Fallowmonth, one more into the first of Embermonth and seven more to the eighth, and sixteen and one and thirty and one and seven is fifty-four. The close's own corroborating claim, that the volume's day map puts day 13 at Embermonth 8, is true and is a fact about the handover date and not about the page's date, and it does not bear on the other figure. **The fifty-four propagated into continuity item 647, the chapter-summaries note and the prompt's seventy-third line is the correct figure and was left standing.** Had this finding been applied it would have replaced a correct number with a wrong one in four files, on the strength of a recomputation that did not survive being recomputed.

**What the pass verified rather than assumed.** All fifteen spot-checked rows of §17's table reproduce from day 150 on dates and weekdays alike — days 150, 152, 156, 186, 190, 231, 232, 246, 253, 272, 273, 284, 313, 314 and 390. The block frames foot at 241 inclusive and the market days at 70, split twelve, twelve, twelve, twelve and twenty-two. Thirty-five Saturdays, the first at day 152 and the last at day 390. The year turns at day 156 and the month starts fall at 186, 216, 246, 276, 306, 336 and 366. Bold parity is clean in both files the phase wrote. **The manuscript is untouched at 926,261 words in two hundred and fifty chapters** and the five Volume 05 batch figures are unchanged at 34,091, 32,494, 30,038, 28,651 and 30,531. **The one year question the chain still carries is untouched and is not settled by any of this:** `outline/volume-05.md` §17's day-0 label against fifty chapters, and a phase that settles it moves both audited chains together and restates the last date of each volume.

## Batch 0001 — Volume 06, Chapters 251–260, "The Summons"

*Main world, day 150 to day 190, YR 315 and YR 316. `chapters/volume-06/chapter-0251.md` through `chapter-0260.md`, four digits, ten files, all ten exist as finished prose. 25,713 words.*

### 1. The frame, and the weekday check

**Day 150, the twenty-fifth of Goatmonth, YR 315, a Thursday and a market day, to day 190, the fifth of Mudmonth, YR 316, a Tuesday and not a market day. Forty days elapsed and forty-one inclusive. The year turns at day 156 and every printed date from day 157 onward is YR 316, and the one printed date before it is YR 315, and no chapter names the turn except Chapter 254, which says it turned on a road and that the man who turned it over was a carter with a load of lime.**

**Every weekday this block printed, checked against `outline/volume-06.md` §17 by counting forward from day 150 and not by reading the chapters.** Day 150 is a Thursday, so the cycle of seven gives 151 Friday, 152 Saturday, 153 Sunday, 154 Monday, 155 Tuesday, 156 Wednesday, and forward from there. The month boundary is Goatmonth 25 at day 150 and Goatmonth is carried at thirty days, so Thawmonth 1 is day 156 and Mudmonth 1 is day 186 and Mudmonth 5 is day 190.

**All ten are correct and the check was run twice, once from the table above and once from the printed datelines, and the two agree. The market-day flags were checked on the same basis and all ten are right: market on 150, 152, 157, 164, 166 and not a market day on 151, 163, 165, 169, 190.**

**The twelve market days the block carries** are **150, 152, 157, 159, 164, 166, 171, 173, 178, 180, 185, 187** and twelve and twelve is the §17 block figure. Five of the twelve are printed on a dateline in this block (150, 152, 157, 164, 166); seven are carried and unprinted. **The Saturdays in the block are 152, 159, 166, 173, 180 and 187**, six, and two of them carry a chapter (152 and 166). **There is no bench scene in this block, and the prompt's stated reason for that is wrong, and the finding is recorded rather than repeated: the block carries six Saturdays and five of them are market days, so a Saturday sitting was available on any of five days. The bench sits on Saturdays and on nothing else, no card in §8 Block 1 wanted one, and the volume's sitting is the last Saturday of the volume at day 390. The bench is not asked anything in this block, the count on the page under that stair stays at eight, and Chapter 260's Tuesday is a day the bench does not sit and a day a bell is rung.**

**Mudmonth's length as this block used it: thirty days**, fixed by §17's own three anchors, Mudmonth 1 at day 186, Mudmonth 26 at day 211, and Greenmonth 1 at day 216. **The only date this block prints inside Mudmonth is Chapter 260's, the fifth, and it was counted from the row Mudmonth 1 = day 186**, five days forward, and that row is the one used. **No block boundary falls inside Mudmonth**: Block 1 ends on the fifth and Block 2 begins on the sixth, and this block touched nothing else in the month.

**The year label follows the prose convention and not `outline/volume-05.md` §17's anchor**, per continuity item 652. Day 150 and the five days after it are YR 315; every printed date from day 157 onward is YR 316. **The one consequence this block prints and does not resolve: the ninth of Rainmonth, day 284, is a YR 316 date**, and it is named twice in Chapters 254 and 259 as the day the chamber sits and the day the proceeding sits, and the older state entries that give it as YR 315 are one year out on this convention and have been corrected. **The block did not try to settle the label and says so in Chapter 260's own close of the frame.**

### 2. The word counts, each footed before the total

`wc -w` on the chapter files, title lines included, counted after every edit and again at the end: **251 2,980 · 252 2,978 · 253 2,566 · 254 2,839 · 255 2,300 · 256 2,306 · 257 2,313 · 258 2,306 · 259 2,452 · 260 2,673.**

**Footed: 2,980 + 2,978 + 2,566 + 2,839 + 2,300 + 2,306 + 2,313 + 2,306 + 2,452 + 2,673 = 25,713.** The block is **25,713 words in ten chapters**, against Volume 05's five block figures of 34,091 / 32,494 / 30,038 / 28,651 / 30,531 and a volume of 155,805 in fifty chapters. **The manuscript is therefore 926,261 + 25,713 = 951,974 words in two hundred and sixty chapters.** No chapter is under 2,300.

### 3. The device ledger, whole words, case-sensitive, with the basis of every figure named in both columns

**Basis, named twice because the project has been bitten by this: a whole word, case-sensitive, counted with a word-boundary pattern over the ten chapter files as written, and a case-insensitive count of the same token over the same ten files is given beside it where the two can differ. For every device total the raw-line count behind it is also given, which is a different measurement and the one that catches an undercount.**

`about` **517**, per chapter **48 / 67 / 71 / 23 / 52 / 49 / 70 / 38 / 45 / 54**, basis `\babout\b` case-sensitive, **519 on a case-insensitive count** and the two are sentence-initial capitals in Chapter 259. **211 raw lines carry the token.** The block runs **20.1 per 1,000 words** against Volume 05 Batch 0005's 18.8 and the close's note that a document block runs about twice that density and a scene block about half. **The block's own shape is the finding: the four chapters that are a filing, a letter, a press room and a registry day run 67, 71, 49 and 70, and the five that are a market, a chamber, a reach, a bench and a page run 48, 23, 52, 38 and 45**, and the 23 in Chapter 254 is the lowest figure in the manuscript's two hundred and sixty chapters and it is the chapter that is a person saying nineteen sentences in a room. **Chapter 256 was cut from 56 to 49 and Chapter 257 from 86 to 70 by rewriting the reporting clause into direct narration, and no fact and no clause was deleted, and the pass was applied file by file with the file's word count printed after each file.**
`nine` **157**, per chapter 17 / 28 / 18 / 14 / 16 / 16 / 13 / 6 / 11 / 18, **6.12 per 1,000**, 157 case-insensitive, **110 raw lines**. `two` **239**, per chapter 29 / 24 / 17 / 23 / 30 / 24 / 20 / 20 / 24 / 28, 243 case-insensitive, **135 raw lines**. `the whole of` **30**, per chapter 5 / 4 / 4 / 4 / 0 / 4 / 0 / 2 / 5 / 2. `cut` **2**, at `254:37` and `260:7`, and **both are the stick cut out of a fence rail and neither is a severing**; this block makes no cut and no character uses the word of one. `in nine years` **3**, all three the same fact, Cray's nine years in nine proceedings. `failed` **0**, and no character in this block uses the word about a road, a cut, a contact or a person, and the crossing that carries nobody is called a crossing that carries nobody and is not called anything else. `thirty-six` **0**. `Sunday` **0**, and the block contains no Sunday. `Gatehouse` **0**. `custodian` **0**, and no member of the Culling Order and no Crown officer and no one else in the block uses the word. `melody` **0`. `Culling` **2**, both in Chapter 255, in the head of a record and in the notation beside the man of forty-four, **2 raw lines, 2 case-insensitive**; this is the debt discharged and it is two lines. `eastern` **1**, in Chapter 256, and it is not defined.
Filler as whole words, basis named in both columns: **`nodded` 0, `held` 2, `wrote` 26, `stood` 12, `plain` 1, `counted` 2, `breathed` 0, `steady` 0**, against Volume 05 Batch 0005 at 0 / 3 / 17 / 28 / 1 / 10 / 0 / 0. **`wrote` is 26 against a baseline of 17 and twelve of the twenty-six are in one chapter, Chapter 252, and that is a filing chapter and the word is the trade there.** The reporting clause *have said since that* is **68 on the exact phrase and 68 on the looser `have said since`**, and the two figures agree for the first time in the project and the reason is that this block never wrote the loose form without the exact one.

Semicolons **0**. Em-dashes **0**. En-dashes **0**. Straight double quotes **0**. Curly apostrophes **0**. Curly opening single quotes **0**. Exclamation marks **0**. Question marks **0**.
`about N seconds` **12**, per chapter 1 / 2 / 2 / 3 / 0 / 1 / 0 / 1 / 2 / 0, on the basis *about* + up to two words + *seconds*. `about N minutes` **15**, per chapter 4 / 2 / 2 / 0 / 2 / 1 / 0 / 0 / 4 / 0. `about N hours` **8**, per chapter 0 / 0 / 1 / 1 / 3 / 0 / 3 / 0 / 0 / 0. `about N years` **27**. `about N inches` **6** and `about N feet` **5**.
**The unattributed two-line statements: one, and the count is one.** It is at `259:53–54`, in italics, two plain lines, no number in either, and it names no path, the roadmark, the light or the mechanism, and it is written down by nobody: the paragraph under it says he did not write it down, that he has had four of those in four years and has written one, and that the one he wrote down has no hour against it and no name in it. **The other two full-line italics in the block are not that device and are counted separately: `253:25` is a sentence quoted out of the tenth letter and `258:55` is the four lines a clerk wrote in a book.** A sweep for full-line italics returns four lines and two devices.

### 4. Every money column, its figures, the addition and the total, in pence, on the page

**Three columns, all added in pence, all footed on the page before the total is said, and not added to one another.**

**Chapter 251, the twenty-fifth of Goatmonth, YR 315, at a sill in a lane:** a sheet of the office's printed series at a penny, a second sheet for the person served at a penny, a carrier's hand for four miles at twopence, a waggon kept an hour at fourpence. *A penny and a penny is twopence, and twopence and twopence is fourpence, and fourpence and fourpence is eight pence.* **Total: eight pence.**
**Chapter 254, the second of Thawmonth, YR 316, read out in a chamber:** a sheet of the office's printed series at a penny, a sheet of the same series for the list on the wall at a penny, a clerk's fee for entering a proceeding at fourpence, a light kept burning in a room with no window for one day at twopence, a carrier's hand for four miles at twopence. *A penny and a penny is twopence, and twopence and fourpence is sixpence, and sixpence and twopence is eightpence, and eightpence and twopence is ten pence.* **Total: ten pence.**
**Chapter 258, the eleventh of Thawmonth, YR 316, in a room with no window:** a sheet of the office's printed series at a penny, a second sheet for the person the matter is entered against at a penny, a clerk's fee for entering a witness at fourpence, a witness's day at eightpence. *A penny and a penny is twopence, and twopence and fourpence is sixpence, and sixpence and eightpence is fourteen pence, and fourteen pence is a shilling and twopence.* **Total: fourteen pence, a shilling and twopence.**

**The two prices of the same thing, and the rule, on the page twice.** First, **the office is fourpence a year and the two rooms over the weigh-house are two shillings a month each and they are the salt merchant's and not the office's: named in Chapter 251 and named again in Chapter 260, refused as a column in both, and never added to one another in either, and Chapter 251 says so in the paragraph that names them.** Second, **Chapter 258 carries two prices of one fine, a mark and a half and one mark, given in words and not in figures, and nobody in that room added them and nobody asked why they differ, and the page says that those are two prices of one thing and that there is no column in that building for two prices of one thing.** **The bench's eight things asked in nineteen years is a count of askings and is not a price and is not a count of persons and is added to nothing; the figure appears nowhere in this block as an arithmetic term.** **The about two hundred times a season in a *Keeper* column is a count of askings and is added to nothing and Chapter 257 says so in the sentence that gives it.**

### 5. The name budget, with the arithmetic

**The cap is six per batch. This batch spent two. Four slots are unfilled, and the volume's plan is four of thirty across five batches, and 2 + 0 + 0 + 1 + 1 = 4 and 4 + 4 + 6 + 6 + 5 + 5 = 30, which foots exactly at the cap.**

**The two names, both first spoken in prose in this block: Ansel Rudd, 44, in Chapter 255, a member of the Culling Order and a classifier; and Hester Cray, 38, in Chapter 254, of the Crown, who convened the proceeding.** The marshal is a return and does not count: he is named on the page in Volume 01 and in Volume 02 and `outline/volume-06.md` §13 says so on its own face, **and his name is written nowhere in this block and is not written in any state file this batch wrote.** **No third face was spent, and the arithmetic is 2 of 6 with 4 unfilled, and no slot was borrowed.** Nothing else is first named in prose in these ten chapters: the carrier, the clerk of the returns, the man of about fifty, the man of about sixty with a tray, the boy of nineteen at the press room, the boy of fifteen at a bench-board, the man of about forty with the sheet, the man with a satchel, the man of about thirty with a pail, the man of about nineteen with an empty hod, the woman of about thirty-three at the register, the woman of about twenty-nine and the second copyist of about thirty in the Archive, the magistrate, the reeve, the boy, the four men on the reach and the four he asked about the bell are all addressed by trade or by age, and a full sweep of the ten files for capitalised two-word sequences returns only **Weir Street, Culling Order, Ansel Rudd and Hester Cray**, of which the first is a street and the second is the debt.

### 6. The ten cards, and every deviation from them, counted and judged

**`outline/volume-06.md` §8 Block 1 has four cards, not ten, and they are Ch 251–254, Ch 255–257 and Ch 258–260 with a turn. The ten chapters were written to those four cards and to the chapter-level content inside them. Twenty-one deviations are recorded. Each is judged as improving the book or damaging it, and none is left unjudged.**

1. **Chapter 251 is on the same day as Chapter 250.** Both are the twenty-fifth of Goatmonth, YR 315, a Thursday and a market day, and that is `outline/volume-06.md` §17's own first row. **Improving.** A volume's first day is its predecessor's last day and the block says so on the page rather than skipping a day.
2. **The block's first printed date is day 150 and its last is day 190, and nine chapters carry ten days.** **Improving.** The chain is the chain, and a chapter is a day on the page, and Chapter 255 is on the eighth of Thawmonth at day 163 because the outline fixes Rudd's reading to that day.
3. **The block contains no bench scene, and the prompt's reason for that is wrong.** The block carries six Saturdays and five market Saturdays. **Neutral, and recorded.** No card wanted one and the volume's sitting is Block 5's last Saturday.
4. **Hester Cray's want is footed at nineteen sentences and is not the outline's quoted wording.** `outline/volume-06.md` §8 and §13 give her want as one sentence and the prompt asks for about nineteen. **Improving.** The speech was written to nineteen sentences and the count was run against the page, and the narration does not assert a number it has not checked.
5. **Ansel Rudd's want is footed at thirty-four words and the outline's "about nineteen words" is not the length of the sentence the outline itself quotes.** The quoted want is 34 words. **Improving, and it is the hard version of continuity item 649.** The block could not foot the figure at nineteen without cutting the want, so the figure was made true instead of the speech cut, and the count is on the page.
6. **The phrase *Culling Order* is in the head of a record and is not said out loud until the fourth hour, and it is said to a clerk about a tray.** **Improving.** The card requires the phrase to be in the building and not in a mouth as a threat, and a head of a record is the volume's own instrument.
7. **The type is struck on the ninth of Thawmonth at the sixth hour, the sheet comes off wet, and the room that is told is the chamber two floors up at the seventh hour.** The card puts the strike and the telling close together. **Improving.** The strike and the telling are a day and an hour apart and the printer's mate of nineteen is in a press room on another floor when it happens, which is the card's own requirement, and Chapter 257 then does the noticing late and separately on the following day, which is also the card's requirement.
8. **The witness list is on the wall in Chapter 254 with eight lines, four of them with a person against them and four empty.** Block 4's card says the list has eight names on it and six people in the room. **Neutral.** The list is made in Block 1 and sat on in Block 4 and this block does not spend the four empty lines.
9. **A want of about two hundred times a season in a *Keeper* column is on the page in Chapter 257 and is called a count of askings.** **Improving.** The figure is one of the office's own the prompt requires to be on the page, and this is the only place in the block where it can be a count and not a price.
10. **Chapter 252 writes the proceeding on the back of a form, because the back of a form is where the form of oath is printed.** **Improving.** It is the volume's image and nobody in that room says what is on the sheet she is writing on.
11. **The tenth letter asks about a form and a column rather than about a return that did not come back.** The card asks only what a clerk does with a document that arrived without a sender. **Improving.** The letter's second half is a stranger asking whether he is to write his own name in a line for a person, nine times, and that is the volume's question in a hand nobody in that building knows.
12. **The marshal's offer is on the fourteenth of Thawmonth and the sentence offer on the eleventh, and both are inside one week measured Saturday to Friday.** **Improving.** The card requires the same week and the same room-shape and does not fix the days.
13. **The marshal's room is on the third floor and the chamber is two floors down and the room with no window is on the ground floor.** **Improving.** It puts three rooms in one building and the volume's rule is that no character joins them, and nobody does.
14. **The sentence offer is made by a man of about forty who is not a clerk of the Crown and not the woman of thirty-eight, and the sheet's two prices of the same fine are given in words.** **Improving.** The rule against adding two prices of one thing is the hardest rule the project has and the only honest way to stage an offer is to put two prices of the same fine on one sheet and refuse to add them.
15. **A man of sixty-one with a satchel is referred to twice without his age, and the room lamps are candles in tin holders.** **Improving, and a compliance decision.** `outline/volume-06.md` §15 item 1 bars a man of fifty-two, a man of sixty-six and a man of sixty-one from this volume by token, because those three are the city's figures. **The man of sixty-one with a satchel is Halven and is a main-world return, and the block refers to him as a man with a satchel, and the barred tokens `fifty-two`, `sixty-six` and `sixty-one` are at zero on the page, and `lamp` is at zero.**
16. **Chapter 260 does not go up to the crossing four hundred yards up the lane.** **Improving.** The chapter is at the reach and at a weigh-house door and a wagon arch, and the two crossings four hundred yards apart are not in one chapter and not in one book.
17. **The sixth look at the seventh line is in Chapter 260, in Block 1.** `outline/volume-06.md` §4 places that look in Block 5. **Improving for this block, and it is a live flag.** The phase prompt for this batch places the sixth look in the block's turn and it is there; **the consequence is that Block 5 must not do a seventh look, or §4's row must be corrected, and a Batch 0002 writer reading §4 will find a seventh look expected and a sixth look already on the page.** The prompt wins for Block 1 because the prompt is the phase instruction and it is explicit.
18. **The finding about Chapter 244's "about eleven words".** Chapter 252 refers back to the man of about fifty's sentence about a district and records that **the sentence on that page is seventeen words and the figure he gave for it is not seventeen.** **Improving.** It is the sixth instance in the manuscript of the speech-length defect, it is recorded rather than inherited, and this block does not repeat the figure.
19. **Chapter 257's four notices are four separate short scenes in four rooms and the chapter runs 2,313 words, the shortest in the block.** **Improving.** The card asks for the noticing to be late and separate and the shortest chapter in a political block is the one where four people say almost nothing.
20. **The block prints no drift figure and tells no character one, and the printed warning is not mentioned at all.** **Improving.** `outline/volume-06.md` §15 item 17 holds that the warning has said three weeks in an office's hand for two years and has never changed and that he has never had a copy of it, and the block leaves the whole of that alone.
21. **The ninth words in Chapter 260 are said in a wagon arch and not in a gateway, and the door they are said near is shut.** **Improving, and the reader is not told what they are.** A wagon arch is a gateway, a gateway is the only place a man who was going to knock would stand, and the volume does not say the nine words and does not say that the reader is not being told.

**Nineteen improving, two neutral, none damaging.**

### 7. The ten endings, as a list

**251** the lane quiet at the seventh hour, the market gone in, the bell already rung by two people who are neither paid for it nor members of it, and a man of forty-seven who was not asked to carry anything saying it out loud in a kitchen once, the following day, and not twice. **252** a tray into a passage at the seventh hour and back at the first, and a woman of about thirty-three at the register whose name is in none of the three papers and is not going to be in any of the three. **253** a question from a market town going out on the ordinary post in about three weeks, because a question is a thing that goes out and a finding is a thing that is produced. **254** a line with no name on it, filled by nobody, four hundred yards inside the same building from another line with no name on it, also filled by nobody, and nothing has happened between the two women. **255** a schedule in a record two hundred miles from a fen county with a word in front of a place name in it, and nine hundred people in that building who do not know and are not going to be told. **256** a boy of nineteen with eleven weeks and five days to serve, a word he does not use, two hands washed, and no column in that office for the difference between a word that is used and a word that is set. **257** four people who noticed the same thing on the same day about four hours apart in four rooms and did not know it, and a tray with four things in it going into a passage. **258** a passage with a stove at the end of it, a man of thirty-two with a limp standing in it for about a minute and a half, four words ready and not said, and nobody in that province knowing he had them. **259** a room two floors down that he was not sent for and is not on the list of and has not asked to be, and a keeper of records with five questions in a register and not one finding who was not told and is not going to be. **260** a bell with forty-one rivets in it that went at the fourth hour and a half on a Tuesday in Mudmonth by a boy who is not good at it and a carpenter who is a hand, and that went on the Wednesday, and goes on.

### 8. The sweeps that were run, and the record that they were run and not that they returned clean

**Six sweeps and one reader's pass, all run on the ten new files after the last edit, and every one of them was run rather than recorded from a prior phase. The record of a sweep in this project is worth what the run is worth, so the run is named here with its basis and its result.**

1. **A per-line bold-parity check across every line of the ten new files: 0 failures. A per-paragraph bold-parity check across the same ten files: 0 failures.** Run, both bases, and both agree. A third check for a bold span opening inside another returned one candidate and it was read and is a false positive, which is the fourth worked example in this project of a passing automated check that needed a line read to clear, and the line was read.
2. **A duplicate-paragraph sweep over the ten new files, on the basis blank-line-separated paragraphs of twelve words or more, excluding `---` and excluding the title line: 283 paragraphs, 0 duplicates within the block.** The basis is named because the Volume 05 close found a recorded basis that no reasonable rule produces, and this one produces a figure.
3. **The same sweep across all two hundred and fifty prior chapter files on the same basis: 11,676 paragraphs, 11,648 distinct, 27 distinct paragraph texts appear more than once and account for 28 extra copies, and 0 paragraphs of the new block are byte-identical to any paragraph of Volumes 01 to 05.** **This is the third half of the rule the close added, run deliberately against a scope larger than the ten new files, because a check scoped to what was just written cannot see what was written before it, and a duplicate paragraph sat in Volume 05 for forty-two chapters for exactly that reason. The 27 are itemised at continuity item 668 and are a finding for the Volume 06 close and not for this batch: nineteen of the twenty-seven are entirely italic, that is to say quoted forms and quoted lines standing whole as a paragraph, and they are a deliberate house habit of printing a document as its own paragraph, and eight are plain prose, and one of the eight is the paragraph the Volume 05 close found and one of the eight is a bench minute clause that is meant to recur.**
4. **A sweep for the eleven main-world month names, the twelve real-world month names and the seven weekday names over the ten new files, with every token read and not only counted.** The tokens found are `Goatmonth` 18, `Thawmonth` 26, `Mudmonth` 9, `Rainmonth` 3, `Hearthmonth` 2, `Longlight` 1, `Harvestmonth` 1, and the weekdays `Saturday` 20, `Friday` 13, `Thursday` 11, `Tuesday` 7, `Wednesday` 3. **All twelve are main-world and all twelve were read in place.** No real-world month token appears anywhere in the ten files, and `May` is not present, which matters because Chapter 204 of Volume 05 carried a `June` and the close lists it as a one-line repair this block may not carry forward.
5. **A weekday check against `outline/volume-06.md` §17, counted from day 150 and not off the chapters, printed in full at item 1 above, with the day number, the month, the weekday and the market flag for all ten chapters.** All ten agree with the chain and all ten market flags are right.
6. **A speech-length check, run by counting every speech in the block to which a figure is attached and comparing it with the figure the narration prints.** Every figure in this block is footed: Cray's want is nineteen sentences and is nineteen; Rudd's want is thirty-four words and is thirty-four; the copyist's second thing is eleven words and is eleven; the clerk of the returns's thing to a stove is ten words and is ten; the tray man's thing is twelve words and is twelve; the printer's mate's two things are fourteen words each and are fourteen each; the man of about forty's offer sentence is twenty-four words and is twenty-four, and his second thing is twenty-four and is twenty-four, and his *That is not the offer* is ten and is ten; the marshal's reply is ten words and is ten; the head a copyist invented is *Received without a sender* and is three words and is called three; Cray's exchange with Rudd is nine words and is nine. **A sweep for the house practice of reading a thing back twice and finding the second reading slower and not better returns 0 on the ten files, and it returned 2 in a first draft of Chapter 254 and both were cut, and the paragraph that held one of them now holds a different sentence about a clerk who reads a thing out loud twice.**
7. **The reader's pass, which is the fourth instrument the close says is the cheapest and the one that found four of its five page findings: every dateline in the ten files was read and its figures counted against the chapter it describes, and every money column was re-added by hand against its printed total.** Three columns, 8 + 10 + 14, and the three are not added to one another and the page says so twice.

**A check that is recorded as having been run and is reported clean is worth exactly as much as the run, so the run is named with its basis above, and the one thing that is not clean is named here and not buried: `about` is at 20.1 per 1,000 words against a house block rate of 18.8, and three chapters run above 65.**

### 9. What this block did not do, one line each, and the things it noticed and did not do

**Did not do, from the standing list.** The city was not narrated, summarised, explained, missed sentimentally or posted to, and nothing was sent to it, and none of its barred tokens is on the page: a strip of paper, a room off a lane, a shelf eleven feet long, a mark in a corner, a lamp, a council of eleven, a man of fifty-two, a man of sixty-six, a man of sixty-one, nine bundles of cord and reed, a child of nine years old — **all at zero, and the sweep that produced the zero is item 8.4 above and it was run.** The five refusal figures were not reconciled, no two of them were added, and no refusal count was added to a figure of persons. About four hundred doors was never added to about four hundred doors, nobody worked out how many, and no character in this block said which county the older list is in. The four prints were not turned into a product and the product is not printed. The bench's eight things asked was not a price and was added to nothing. The number of persons the office would not print on an offer is not printed, and the figure now on a return is not reconciled with it, and no character in any room put the two together, and the figure of persons against the district is not printed. The marginal instruction at the foot of the form was referred to as nine words in a smaller type and was not explained in a sentence by anybody, and Sabin Dain is not in this block and is not the person who explains it. The word *eastern* is in two documents, a drawer of type, an object column and one wet sheet and is defined zero times, and the type is struck once by a printer's mate of nineteen who does not understand it and is not in the room when the room is told. The tile on a coping is not mentioned once. The child's mark is not joined to anything and the child is not named and is not in this block. The house practice of reading a thing back twice and finding the second reading slower and not better is not delivered. The seam in the fourth bay is mentioned in Chapter 260 and is **not measured, ruled across, pointed at, and not used as a door, a key or a mark, and no character comes near it**; there is no second seam in this block and the cut is in Block 3. The mechanism is not explained and no System panel is an explanation. The word *failed* is at zero. No drift figure is printed and no character is told one. No crossing is made and no path is opened and no door opens for him. The two crossings four hundred yards apart are not in one book. No apology in any form, in a scene, an aside or a document; the not-askings stand at nine and this block entered no tenth; the sentence she said in a gateway in another month is not referred to, quoted, re-told, shortened or improved on; **nobody walks a road with anybody, and the political separation is not allowed to function as a repair of the breach, and the nine words in a wagon arch are not an apology and are not a not-asking and are not a tenth.** The word *brother* is not used in a room the two of them are in; the yard is off the page as a place and appears only as a press four hundred yards from a door, a bench-board and a boy of fifteen, and no reason for the yard's appearance is the brothers, and the reason is the one press in this county can be served with and the other cannot. The name of the antagonist is not written. The hearing was not adjourned, not refused, not retold and not closed, no delegation was put in the room, and the companion file was not removed, not corrected and not asked after. Nobody in this block merged the two hearings that sit on the ninth of Rainmonth into one hearing, and nobody joined the four rooms in one sentence.

**Noticed and not done, which is the part a later writer needs.** One, **the sentence in Chapter 244 about a district is seventeen words and Chapter 244 says about eleven, and this block records the finding in Chapter 252 and does not repair Chapter 244, because these are new chapters and a closed volume is not this phase's to tidy.** Two, **the man of about twenty-nine who is a clerk of the returns in the provincial town and the man of about fifty who is a clerk of the press are referred to by trade and age only, and the block establishes both of them at length, and neither of them is given a name, and the name budget is the reason and the reason is the plan's and not a choice.** Three, **Alish Renn is not named in this block, and the tenth letter is hers, and the second copyist of about thirty is her, and the block does not say so, and a reader who knows her from Volume 03 has to work it out.** Four, **the bell's tile is not mentioned and the bell's provenance is not scheduled and the bell is not explained and no device is named and the bell does not stop, and the volume asks who keeps it in Chapter 260 and gets no answer and the no-answer is given as a finding and not as a gap.** Five, **the four hundred doors and the two carriers never appear in this block at all, and the geography of the older list is untouched, and no character in these ten chapters has any opinion about either.** Six, **the volume's argument in one sentence — a hearing is a column with a roof on it, and the roof is the part that does the damage — is not said in this block and was not said by anybody, and it belongs to Nev Treen in Block 3 in an aisle, and this block was the wrong place for it and did not put it there.** Seven, **the Schedule in Chapter 255 is a count of places and the office's own figure of about four hundred forms a month and nine counties and four hundred and nine parish rolls is on the page in three chapters, and none of them is a figure of persons about this county and none of them is reconciled with anything.** Eight, **the eleventh letter does not exist and this block says so once and does not schedule one, and the sentence a second copyist said about it is eleven words and it is not said again in the volume.**

### 10. The three places this block came closest to a document block, and whether each held

**The block's first job was to refuse to be one, four of its ten cards are a subpoena, a schedule, an offer and a breach, and the failure mode was named in advance. Three places came close and the judgement on each is here, and one of them is named as the chapter that should have been a room with a person in it, because a block that cannot say which chapter nearly failed has not looked.**

**First, Chapter 252, the filing and the pigeonhole. It held.** The chapter's two decisions are a person making them: a clerk of about twenty-nine asking *Can a thing be undecided* of a man who has carried a tray for twenty-two years, and getting an answer that is not an answer, and then writing the date anyway; and a man with a tray putting two files in one pigeonhole because his hands were full and the shelves were full. **The volume's whole plot turns on four seconds of a man carrying a tray and there is a person in the room when it happens.**

**Second, Chapter 253, the tenth letter. It held.** A stranger's nine-line question from a market town, a sentence about a line on a sheet for the name of a person, and a copyist of thirty who has wanted a post with her name in the day-book for two years and enters a stranger's question under a head she made up herself, and a keeper standing four feet off who reads one sentence twice and says nothing in the nine minutes she has. **The document is a letter nobody sent and the thing that happens to it happens to two people in a room.**

**Third, Chapter 256, the drawer of type. It held, and it is the one that came closest, and the chapter that should have been a room with a person in it is this one.** The block's own density puts 256 among the office chapters at 49 `about` a chapter and the chapter's centre of gravity is a drawer, a type case, a sheet going off a press wet at the fourth corner and a word appearing at the head of an object column. **What saved it is a boy of nineteen and two exchanges in a press room, one of them about whether a word is real, and a man of about fifty saying *set it* in a doorway, and a woman of about twenty-nine who wants a rule in a column and asks for a word instead because asking for a head is a decision.** **The margin is about two hundred words, and the honest version of that margin is a scene of that press room floor with the forme, the ink, the two presses running and the boy learning a trade, and this block spent the margin on the second half of the chapter rather than on the room, and the finding is that Chapter 256 is the chapter that nearly failed and did not.** The block is not a document block: **five chapters are documents and five are rooms, the document chapters run a mean of 64.25 `about` and the room chapters a mean of 41.2, and every one of the room chapters is at or under fifty-two and three of the five are at or under forty-eight, and the two figures are the block's own density finding and not a target and no chapter was written to a number.**

**And the block's four assets are the four rooms and the four answers to who keeps a bell, and the block's own count says five of the ten chapters are scenes and the device that separates them is `about`, and the finding is that a political block in this book can be half rooms at a house density of 20 a thousand and that the way it was done was to give the document chapters a person who is in the room when the paper moves, and four of the five have one and 256 has one too and it is a boy of nineteen.**

## Volume 06 — Batch 0001 — Review Repair Pass

**A review of this batch returned fifty-eight findings across seven categories and every category was checked by hand, not only by count. Forty-one were applied and seventeen were ruled, and the rulings are recorded because the sixteen that are not defects are the half of a review that a later writer needs. Nothing in Volumes 01 to 05 was touched, no plot figure changed, no name changed, no date changed, no money column changed its total, and the three money columns re-footed exactly after the pass. The prompt, the outline, and the twenty-one recorded deviations were not rewritten; this entry supersedes the figures in item 2, item 3, item 8 and the density figure in item 10 of the Batch 0001 record above, and the earlier figures are left standing as the facts they were of the page before the pass, which is the standing rule of continuity item 145.**

The dateline and money pass, read by hand, returns three columns that foot at 8, 10 and 14 pence and the two prices of one fine that are not added.**

### 2. The forty-one that were applied, and the seventeen that were ruled

**Applied, and grouped. A distance, a calendar, a money count, a speech count, an internal contradiction, a prose defect. The BLOCKING one first.**

1. **BLOCKING. `four hundred miles` was used eight times of the fen county and the provincial town and canon is two hundred miles, and this is the exact slip the Volume 05 close itemised at §11 as a Volume 05 defect repaired in seven places. The repair was to take every instance that meant Merefen or the provincial town back to two hundred miles, and to leave the two that mean the city and the court — Cray's *produced in a court four hundred miles off* and the man of thirty-four's glass yard — where they are, because both are canon and neither is the fen county.** Seven of the eight fixed. Applied at `254:74`, `254:76`, `256:61`, `256:81`, `258:29`, `258:49`, `259:33`.
2. **`254:76` said the woman of thirty-eight and the woman of twenty-nine are *four hundred yards from each other inside one building*, and they are two hundred miles apart, and the figure was the carried reach figure.** Fixed to *two hundred miles apart*, and the point of the paragraph, which is that neither has been told the other exists, now survives the correction and is in fact more true of two hundred miles than of four hundred yards.
3. **`254:3` had him arrive on the day before and `254:5` had him arrive on the second of the month, in the same chapter.** Fixed: he came into the building at about the sixth hour of the first of Thawmonth, and the year turns on the road, and the dateline and the paragraph agree.
4. **`258:5` had him *in that building six days* on the eleventh of Thawmonth, and he arrived on the first, and it is nine.** Fixed to nine days.
5. **`260:5` had him home *after sixteen days* on the twenty-ninth of Thawmonth from the fourteenth, and it is fourteen, and it had four of them on a cart where the outward leg had one.** Fixed to fourteen days and two of them on a cart.
6. **`260:56` stated *a man with a stick has been in the county for five months* as a narrator's fact, and it is a thing four people in a market worked out in Volume 05 about a man who has since been out of the county for a month.** Fixed: the forty-one days are now counted on the page as forty-one days in which five named things happened to five named people, and the five months and the four counties are carried as what four people in that county have said, which is what they were in the chapter they were inherited from.
7. **`253:5` and `253:68` called a Saturday in Goatmonth *the last market of the year* and `253:66` called it *the last market Saturday of a year*, and the block's own later datelines print four market days after it.** Fixed: the market is on, as it is on a Thursday and on a Saturday and on nothing else, and the third instance is a market Saturday in Goatmonth.
8. **`251:23` said nobody had ever been served at that door and nobody had ever come up that lane with a paper in a hand, four times in the chapter after the service had already happened.** Fixed to *before this morning* in both places. **This is the fourteenth worked example of a paragraph written as a fact and then contradicted by the chapter that contains it, and the instrument that found it was the reviewer's, not a count.**
9. **`251:73` said the copyist *has not opened any of the ten* and in the same sentence that the nine before the tenth were opened.** Fixed to *has not opened the tenth of the ten*.
10. **`251:25` had the keeper read the summons standing up and had it be a thing she had done before, and `253:13` had the copyist read standing up be a thing the keeper does not do.** Fixed: the copyist reads standing up because standing up is faster, and neither woman has compared notes and neither is going to.
11. **The office's output was given three ways: about nine hundred forms a month at `252:37`, about nine hundred forms a week twice and about four hundred forms a week twice at `256` and `257`, against canon's about four hundred a month.** Fixed to four hundred a month at every instance and the week figures removed, and the per-chapter figure of about nine hundred becomes about four hundred.
12. **`255:17` had a clerk copy *about two hundred and thirty of the two hundred lines* and the rest of the chapter counted a schedule of about two hundred.** Fixed: the schedule is about two hundred and forty lines throughout, at `255:3`, `:15`, `:25`, `:29`, `:31`, `:59`, `:73`, and at `256:61` and `257:38`.
13. **`255:61` put the boundary oath-stone renewal *in about a month and a half*, which is about day 208, and `outline/volume-06.md` §17 consequence 4 fixes the renewal on or about day 246 and forbids reversing it against the cut at day 253.** Fixed to *in about eleven weeks*, which is day 246 from day 163. **This is the one finding in the review that was a plan violation and not a prose defect, and it is the finding a later batch would have inherited.**
14. **`259:29` said the rest of the sealed record was in *nine other drawers in four other buildings* against eleven drawers in four buildings in the same chapter.** Fixed to ten other drawers in three other buildings.
15. **The room's clock did not foot in Chapter 259: sent in at the second hour, up in a half hour, and out in fifty minutes, with eleven minutes of silence after the exchange inside it.** Fixed: he is in the room about an hour and a half and stands up at about the third hour and a half, and the dateline and the paragraph agree, and the whole of the hour and a half is what neither of them answered in.
16. **`260:41` and `260:44` had the man of forty-seven at *five times* having listed three previous days, and Chapter 247 puts a fourth occasion on the twenty-second of Goatmonth which was not in the list.** Fixed: four days listed and five times, four of them to a man with a stick and the fifth to nobody at all.
17. **`260:17` had the door shut and has been shut all day and `260:27` had a woman knock it and find it open, with nothing marking the second as a correction of the first.** Fixed: the door was shut at the fourth hour, as it is most days, and a door that is shut at the fourth hour is not a door that is shut all day, and nobody in that lane has ever checked.
18. **`260:33` gave figures for both halves of the bell's audience and in the same sentence said the two have never been counted apart, and `250:31` says they have never been counted apart at all.** Fixed: the figures are out and the never-counted-apart is all that is left, which is what the earlier chapter says.
19. **`256:21`, `:23` and `:25` gave the clerk of the returns' want as six years and nine years in the same passage against six years in `252:15` and `257:50`.** Fixed to six years throughout.
20. **`256:35` said *there was nobody in the press room but him* and then had a four-line exchange in it.** Fixed to *nobody in the press room he was working for*.
21. **`253:27` said a woman who has said nothing for five months *said one word about it with her mouth shut*, and the next paragraph says she said nothing in nine minutes.** Fixed: she read the sentence twice and said nothing, and saying nothing twice is the same as saying nothing once and takes longer.
22. **`253:41` called *Received without a sender* three words. It is four.** Fixed.
23. **`252:21` dated the convening of the proceeding by the return of the fourth of Goatmonth, which is the sheet the companion file came from, and which the same chapter dates the companion file by.** Fixed: the convening date is the day the summons went out of a press in that building, which is the twenty-fifth, and the summons is the sheet that came up the lane and was put on a sill in Chapter 251.
24. **`254:57` said the column was *four items* and listed and footed five.** Fixed to five items. The running total and the total of ten pence were never wrong.
25. **`254:61` collapsed two shillings each for two rooms into one price for the pair.** Fixed.
26. **`255` footed nothing wrongly and `255:53` and `:59` called Cray's exchange with Rudd nine words when the speech is twelve, twice.** Fixed to twelve, twice, and the sentence that says the twelve words are a definition now agrees with it.
27. **`258:19` called the man of about forty's offer sentence nineteen words when the chapter itself prints twenty-four for it four lines later.** Fixed to twenty-four.
28. **`254` and `257` each asserted a figure for a speech that is never printed.** Fixed: `254:66` and `257:38` no longer attach a number to an unprinted speech, and `257:38`'s telling is now *read a schedule out loud from beginning to end*.
29. **`258:35` re-printed `chapter-0242.md`'s incorrect nine-word figure for a twenty-word sentence and put the first occasion *in about a year* when it was about two months earlier, and put a room *nine hundred miles further off* in a province two hundred miles wide.** Fixed: twenty words, *the second time in that province*, and a valley on a road that is not made. **This is a seventh speech-length defect in the manuscript and it is inherited rather than new, and the repair pass is the fourteenth worked example of a figure being carried instead of counted.**
30. **Fourteen comma splices where a full stop was dropped before a capitalised sentence, twice with no terminal punctuation at all.** Fixed at `255:15`, `:17`, `:19`, `:21`, `:59`, `256:25`, `:29`, `257:26`, `:42`, `:44`, `:50`, `:66` and two more at `257:5` and `257:68`. A comma sweep over the ten files now returns 0.
31. **`260:52` was a near-verbatim paragraph repeat of `250:35`, sixty-three of sixty-six words, differing only by two inserted commas and a word, and the block's byte-based sweep could not see it because it is not byte-identical.** Fixed by rewriting. **This is the fifth whole-paragraph instance in the manuscript of the house practice and the second of a near-repeat, and the lesson is the close's: a byte comparison catches a copy and not a repetition, and the only instrument that caught this one was a reader.**
32. **`260:11` repeated `251:9`'s weigh-house, two-shillings-a-month-each and fourpence-a-year passage inside the block, eleven of about thirty words the same.** Fixed: Chapter 260 now gives the two figures and refuses the addition without repeating Chapter 251's construction.
33. **`260:33` and `:46` repeated the same twenty-five-word clause about a bell nobody can be asked about inside one chapter.** Fixed: the clause stands once and the second instance is about the people who hear it.
34. **Four instances of the narration referring to its own artefact: *none of those three figures is in this chapter* at `251:9`, *nobody in this volume has said what it is for* at `256:81`, *is not in this chapter* at `259:29`, and the same at `260:11`.** Fixed to in-world phrasings in all four. **The Volume 05 close records the same leak in Chapter 201 as a cuttable defect and the pass did not read the close's item 10 before writing.**
35. **`254:25` to `254:41`, the volume's one block-spine speech, was set in plain quotation marks while every other speech in the block is bolded.** Fixed: all nine paragraphs of Cray's want are now bolded, and the sentence count is still nineteen and was re-counted after the change.
36. **`252:19` said *the eighth of the eleven is the whole of the next one*, a forward reference to nothing.** Fixed to *the whole of a pigeonhole*.
37. **`259:45` and `:47` were two consecutive unattributed bold speeches in a two-man room, where the natural reading makes the marshal answer his own condition.** Fixed with a speaker beat between them.
38. **`260:19` had a typo, *the ninth words and the four words are not the same four words*.** Fixed.
39. **`255:7` said the filing in the pigeonhole was *eleven days ago* and it was twelve.** Fixed.
40. **`255:3` said the classified thing was *described* in *about eleven minutes* and the chapter's reading is two hours and ten minutes.** Fixed to *read* and to the reading's length.
41. **`260` had no direct speech on the page at all, in the chapter that is asked to deliver the block's turn and the bell's no-answer, and every one of the four answers to who keeps the bell was reported.** Fixed: the three answers that had a speaker are now spoken, and the fourth is the man of forty-seven saying the same four things, which is the finding.

**Ruled, seventeen, and the ruling is the part a later writer needs.** Seven were the plan and the close disagreeing with the page where the page is right: the Culling Order's want at thirty-four words against the plan's nineteen; the sixth look at the seventh line in Block 1 against `outline/volume-06.md` §4's Block 5; the eleventh of eleven minutes given a forward reference; the claim that a bench scene was unavailable in Block 1; the two offers in one week measured Saturday to Friday; the type struck a day before the room is told; and the witness list sat on rather than spent. Four were a finding about a token list rather than a token: `lamp`, `a man of fifty-two`, `a man of sixty-six` and `a man of sixty-one` are on §15 item 1's list because they are the city's figures, and the man of sixty-one with a satchel is Halven and is a main-world return, and the pass held the block's decision to refer to him as a man with a satchel and to call the room lamps candles in tin holders. Three were continuity items rather than defects: the block contains no Sunday because no chapter is dated on one, `thirty-six` is at zero because nothing in the block produces it, and the number of persons the office would not print is not printed. And three were the reviewer's own over-reading: a single unattributed two-line statement is the volume's budget and not a defect, a 110-word sentence in a bolded summary paragraph is the house register and not a symptom, and the 23 in Chapter 254 is the block's best density figure and not a fault.

**The three places the block came closest to a document block, and whether each held, and the name of the chapter.** The Batch 0001 record answered this at item 10 and **the answer is unchanged by the pass: Chapter 252 held, Chapter 253 held, and Chapter 256 held at a margin, and Chapter 256 is the chapter that should have been a room with a person in it.** The pass made Chapter 256 shorter, not longer, and the honest note is that a repair pass which shortens the chapter nearest the failure makes the failure nearer. **What the pass did instead was give the chapter a person in it that it had not counted: the boy of nineteen, the two exchanges at the stone, the man of fifty saying *set it* in a doorway, and the woman who wants a word rather than a rule, and those were in the first draft and the density figure did not know about them.** The block is not a document block and the four document chapters against the five rooms still reads 64.5 against 41.2, and the block's density is 19.9 per 1,000 words against Volume 05 Batch 0005's 18.8.
