## Volume 06 — Batch 0001 — Second Review Repair Pass

**A review of the first repair pass returned twenty-five findings, five of them blocking, and its central finding is that the first pass was a record of intentions and not a record of corrections: a double-digit number of the forty-one fixes it claimed were not on the page. This pass re-ran the first pass against the text instead of against its own record — searching for the strings the findings said they had removed and not the line numbers they cited, because line drift in these files is real and all five blocking findings were cited at a line that had moved — and applied twenty-six corrections across the ten chapters and four state files. The durable record with the disposition of every finding from both reviews is `reviews/volume-06/batch-0001.md`, and that file is the fix for the root cause, which is that a fifty-eight-finding review existed only as prose inside this file, written by the same commit that made the prose.**

**Nothing in the planned plot moved. No chapter was restarted, no card was broken, no name was spent, no date changed, no money column changed its total, no day number, month, weekday or market-day flag was touched, and no controller file was touched. The three money columns re-foot at 8, 10 and 14 pence. The good prose is the prose that was there: eleven of the twenty-six corrections are a single word, a single figure, a single full stop or a single line reference, and the two rewrites are one paragraph of Chapter 260 and one clause of Chapter 256, and both were rewritten around the facts rather than instead of them.**

### The five blocking findings

1. **The plan violation.** `outline/volume-06.md` §17 consequence 4 fixes the boundary oath-stone renewal on or about day 246 and forbids reversing it against the cut at day 253. Chapter 255 is day 163 and the page said *in about a month and a half*, which is about day 208, which puts the real document after the forged one that stands on it. **Now *in about twelve weeks*, eighty-three days, day 246.** The first pass had claimed *in about eleven weeks*, which is day 240 and a week short; the interval is now counted rather than rounded down.
2. **The office's output twice in one sentence.** `257:26` gave the four hundred forms a month *and* nine hundred of them a week in the same breath, against canon's about four hundred a month. **One figure now, and the week figure is gone.**
3. **A speech-length figure against the chapter's own printed speech.** `258:19` called the man of about forty's offer nineteen words; `258:21` prints twenty-four, and `258:25` gave the other speech as twenty-four and was right. **Now twenty-four.**
4. **A speech-length figure contradicted two clauses later in the same chapter.** `255:59` said the answer had come in nine words and then said the twelve words are a definition, against `255:53` and the twelve printed at `255:57`. **Now twelve.**
5. **A near-verbatim paragraph repeat across a volume boundary.** `260:62` was eighty-eight words of ninety identical to `250:35`, differing by *And he* for *He*, one comma and the `**` wrapper, and a byte-identical duplicate sweep is blind to exactly that. **Rewritten in its own words, keeping the number, the rule, the space, the widest of the four, the boy's not being asked and the man's not writing.** The first pass had claimed to have fixed a smaller instance of the same repeat at `260:52` and that one is indeed gone; the instance it did not find is the larger one.

### The twenty-one non-blocking findings, grouped

**Prose and punctuation, five.** Two comma splices the first pass claimed and had not, at `257:5` and `257:56`. A four-word figure against a printed five at `257:38`. A figure attached to a conversation that is never printed at `254:68`, which the first pass named at `254:66` and which had not moved because it had never been changed. And the same at `257:38` and again at `257:42`.

**The narrator referring to its own artefact, five.** `253:33` carried *the only thing in this chapter* and *the best piece of machinery in a volume about forms* in one sentence and now carries neither. `255:11` carried *not spoken in this chapter* and now says in that room. `256:81` carried *nobody in this volume* and now says nobody in that office. `259:29` carried *anybody in this block will see* and now says anybody in that building. A sweep across eleven patterns now returns 0.

**Internal contradictions, four.** `255:29` said nine of *the two hundred lines* of a schedule the chapter counts as two hundred and forty in six other places, and `255:31` divides that schedule into nine and two hundred and thirty-one; it is two hundred and forty throughout. `256:33` said the boy said the only two things he said and that both were about the eleven weeks and then that one was about the word; it is now two things about the word and one about the eleven weeks, which is what `256:37–43` and `256:51` print. `260:51` said the man of forty-seven had said the same four things four times and `260:54` then made the fifth of them *to nobody at all* when the chapter has him say them to the man with the stick four lines above; the fifth is now to a man with a stick, with the yard wall, the bench-board and nobody carried forward from `247:29`. And `260:5` came home in fourteen days with two of them on a cart for the leg `254:5` says took four days, three on a hired horse; the return is now four days and the same three and one, which puts his departure on the twenty-fifth of Thawmonth and satisfies every other date in the chapter.

**A figure that cannot be true of its referent, seven.** *About nine hundred people in that building* stood seven times across Chapters 252, 255, 257 and 258, in a building the same chapters put at four floors, about forty people, nine presses and nine people who know the drawer is not to be used. **Every one of the seven is now *in that town*, in a town of about four thousand people, which is what the parallel sentence in the lede of Chapter 251 has always said.** This one is the second review's own finding and the first review did not see it at all.

**A construction repeated inside the block, one.** `260:11` rebuilt Chapter 251's weigh-house, two-shillings-a-month, fourpence-a-year, never-added construction. The two figures and the refusal stand; the construction is now Chapter 260's own.

### Ruled, seven, and the ruling is the part a later writer needs

**`260:47`'s carter of thirty with a waggon of his own is canon and is not the carrier of about fifty in `251:31` and `253:5`.** The carter is established at `198:7`, `201:13`, `238:29` and `238:61` and the carrier at `188:7`, `199:33` and `200:41`, and they are two trades in one county with two waggons between them, and the finding that they were one man is refused. **This is the one blocking-adjacent finding in the second review that is wrong, and it is recorded because a repair pass that fixes everything it is given is not a repair pass, it is a rewrite.** `260:60`'s two men of twenty-six against `260:43`'s a man of about twenty-six was a real defect and was fixed at the deviant instance, because canon at `200:23`, `201:13` and `209:11` is *two men of about twenty-six*. `258:29`'s nine hundred miles to a valley is contradicted nowhere and the first pass's own remedy for that sentence is on the page. `251:13`'s nine words at the foot of a form is a printed form and not a speech. `252:59`'s seventeen words is the correct count of Chapter 244's sentence and is this block recording a Volume 05 defect. `257:18` and `257:22`'s nineteen words are the same nineteen words as the note face down on the second shelf at `257:20`. And the review's claim that `258:35` re-printed Chapter 242's incorrect figure was right about the defect and wrong about the repair: the page now carries no figure at all where it carried the wrong one, so the wrong figure stops travelling, and Chapter 242 stands as closed prose with its own defect recorded at item 687, exactly as Chapter 244's stands.

### The figures, re-counted after the last edit of this pass

**Word counts, `wc -w`, title lines included: 251 3,009 · 252 2,987 · 253 2,589 · 254 2,840 · 255 2,311 · 256 2,295 · 257 2,306 · 258 2,326 · 259 2,504 · 260 2,823. Footed: 25,990. The manuscript is 926,261 + 25,990 = 952,251 words in two hundred and sixty chapters.** The block is 258 words larger than it was when the writer finished it and 78 larger than it was after the first pass, and the whole of the gain is repair.

**Device ledger, whole words, case-sensitive, a word-boundary pattern over the ten files as written, case-insensitive beside the count, and a raw-line count behind every total.** `about` **514**, per chapter 48 / 68 / 72 / 23 / 52 / 47 / 69 / 37 / 45 / 53, **516 case-insensitive**, **19.8 per 1,000 words**, **212 raw lines on the case-sensitive basis and 213 case-insensitive**. `nine` **145**, per chapter 17 / 27 / 18 / 13 / 12 / 12 / 10 / 6 / 10 / 20, **103 raw lines**. `two` **252**, **256 case-insensitive**, **133 raw lines**. `the whole of` **29**. `cut` **2**, at `254:72` and `260:5`, and both are the stick cut out of a fence rail. `in nine years` **3**. `failed` **0**. `thirty-six` **0**. `Sunday` **0**. `Gatehouse` **0**. `custodian` **0**. `melody` **0**. `Culling` **2**, both in Chapter 255. `eastern` **1**, in Chapter 256, defined zero times. Filler: `nodded` 0, `held` 2, `wrote` 27, `stood` 12, `plain` 1, `counted` 2, `breathed` 0, `steady` 0. The reporting clause *have said since that* **70** and *have said since* **70**, and they still agree. `---` rules **41**. **Bolded line-openings 99** on the line basis and **99** on the paragraph basis. Semicolons, em-dashes, en-dashes, straight double quotes, curly apostrophes, curly opening single quotes **0 each**. `about N seconds` **12**, `about N minutes` **11**, `about N hours` **8**. **The unattributed two-line statement is one, at `259:55–56`, and the count is one**, and a sweep for full-line italics returns four lines of which two are it.

**Eight sweeps, all re-run after the last edit of this pass, and the record that they were run and not that they came back clean.** Per-line bold parity 0 and per-paragraph bold parity 0. Duplicate paragraphs of twelve words or more within the block 0, and 0 byte-identical to any paragraph of Volumes 01 to 05. **A near-repeat sweep at a 0.75 sequence ratio with a 0.55 word-set prefilter, run across the whole manuscript and not only the block, returns two pairs touching a Volume 06 chapter: `251:11` against `244` at 0.824, which is the volume's opening page restating the office's printed series for a reader meeting it, and which is left standing; and the `250:35` instance, which was 0.987 and is now below the threshold.** The comma-splice sweep on *comma, capital, lowercase* returns 0. The narrator-artefact sweep across eleven patterns returns 0. The month sweep returns twelve distinct tokens, all main-world, and no real-world month token anywhere in the ten files. The weekday check, counted forward from day 150 and not read off the chapters, returns all ten datelines correct with all ten market flags right. The speech-length check, run by finding every figure, following it to the speech and recounting, returns fifteen figures attached to a printed speech and every one true: eleven, nineteen sentences across nine paragraphs, twelve, twelve, thirty-four, fourteen, fourteen, five, ten, twelve, twenty-four, twenty-four, ten, seventy, ten. And the dateline and money pass, read by hand, returns three columns that foot at 8, 10 and 14 pence and the two prices of one fine that are not added.

**The density finding after this pass: 19.8 per 1,000 words against Volume 05 Batch 0005's 18.8, the four document chapters at 64.0 a chapter, the five room chapters at 41.0, Chapter 260's 53 in neither, and Chapter 254's 23 still the lowest figure in the manuscript in two hundred and sixty chapters.** The block's shape is unchanged by the repairs: a repair pass that fixes a comma does not make a chapter less like a document, and the second pass made Chapter 256 shorter and Chapter 260 longer and the honest note is the first pass's own, that a repair pass which lengthens the chapter nearest the failure makes the failure further away.

## Batch 0002 — Volume 06, Chapters 261–270, "The Classification"

*Main world, day 191 to day 231, YR 316. `chapters/volume-06/chapter-0261.md` through `chapter-0270.md`, four digits, ten files, all ten exist as finished prose. 23,184 words on first writing and 24,510 after the review repair pass. **Every figure in the numbered sections below was counted on the 23,184-word text and the repair re-counted all of them; where the two disagree the repair's figure is in continuity items 704 to 709 and in the repair section at the foot of this file.** The per-chapter figures are 261 2,630 · 262 2,286 · 263 2,300 · 264 2,502 · 265 2,572 · 266 2,645 · 267 2,493 · 268 2,586 · 269 2,191 · 270 2,305, footed at 24,510.*

### 1. The frame, and the weekday check

**Day 191, the sixth of Mudmonth, YR 316, a Wednesday, to day 231, the sixteenth of Greenmonth, YR 316, a Monday and not a market day. Forty days elapsed and forty-one inclusive. Every date in this block is a YR 316 date and no block boundary falls inside Mudmonth: Block 2 begins on the sixth and Block 3 begins on the seventeenth of Greenmonth.**

**Every weekday this block printed, checked against `outline/volume-06.md` §17 by counting forward from day 150 and not by reading the chapters.** Day 150 is a Thursday, so the cycle of seven gives 191 = 150 + 41 = Thursday plus 6 = **Wednesday**, and every date after it by the same cycle.

| Ch | Day | Date as printed | Weekday printed | Counted from day 150 | Market | Correct |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 261 | 191 | the sixth of Mudmonth, YR 316 | Wednesday | +41, Thu + 6 | not a market day | yes |
| 262 | 194 | the ninth of Mudmonth, YR 316 | Saturday | +44, Thu + 2 | market | yes |
| 263 | 199 | the fourteenth of Mudmonth, YR 316 | Thursday | +49, Thu + 0 | market | yes |
| 264 | 201 | the sixteenth of Mudmonth, YR 316 | Saturday | +51, Thu + 2 | market | yes |
| 265 | 210 | the twenty-fifth of Mudmonth, YR 316 | Monday | +60, Thu + 4 | not a market day | yes |
| 266 | 211 | the twenty-sixth of Mudmonth, YR 316 | Tuesday | +61, Thu + 5 | not a market day | yes |
| 267 | 212 | the twenty-seventh of Mudmonth, YR 316 | Wednesday | +62, Thu + 6 | not a market day | yes |
| 268 | 221 | the sixth of Greenmonth, YR 316 | Friday | +71, Thu + 1 | not a market day | yes |
| 269 | 228 | the thirteenth of Greenmonth, YR 316 | Friday | +78, Thu + 1 | not a market day | yes |
| 270 | 231 | the sixteenth of Greenmonth, YR 316 | Monday | +81, Thu + 4 | not a market day | yes |

**All ten are correct, and the check was run twice, once from §17 and once from the printed datelines, and the two agree.**

**A DISAGREEMENT BETWEEN THE PHASE PROMPT AND THE BINDING DOCUMENT, RECORDED AND THE DOCUMENT GOVERNS.** The prompt for this batch stated that day 191 is **a Friday**. `outline/volume-06.md` §17's own row for 191 says **Wed**, and the prompt's own list of the block's twelve market days — 192, 194, 199, 201, 206, 208, 213, 215, 220, 222, 227, 229 — only balances as Thursday-and-Saturday if 192 is the Thursday and 194 the Saturday, which puts 191 on the Wednesday. **The page is the Wednesday. The prompt's Friday is one day out and is recorded here rather than repeated, and no chapter carries it.** Every other date the prompt fixed — 231 a Monday, the twelve market days, the six Saturdays — agrees with §17 and with the page.

**The twelve market days the block carries** are **192, 194, 199, 201, 206, 208, 213, 215, 220, 222, 227 and 229**, and twelve and twelve is §17's block figure. Three of them are printed on a dateline in this block (194, 199, 201) and nine are carried and unprinted. **The Saturdays in the block are 194, 201, 208, 215, 222 and 229**, six, and all six are market days.

**There is no bench scene in this block, and the bench is not asked anything, and the count on the page under that stair stays at eight.** The block carries six Saturdays and all six are market mornings, so a Saturday sitting was available on any of six days. `outline/volume-06.md` §8 Block 2 has no card that wants one, and the volume's sitting is the last Saturday of the volume at day 390. The bench of nine is not in a room in these ten chapters at all.

**Mudmonth's length as this block used it: thirty days**, fixed by §17's own three anchors, Mudmonth 1 at day 186, Mudmonth 26 at day 211, and Greenmonth 1 at day 216. **The dates this block prints inside Mudmonth were counted from the row Mudmonth 1 = day 186**, and that row is the one used; Mudmonth 6 is 191, Mudmonth 9 is 194, Mudmonth 14 is 199, Mudmonth 16 is 201, Mudmonth 25 is 210, Mudmonth 26 is 211 and Mudmonth 27 is 212. Greenmonth 1 is day 216, so Greenmonth 6 is 221, Greenmonth 13 is 228 and Greenmonth 16 is 231.

**The year label follows the prose convention and not `outline/volume-05.md` §17's anchor**, per continuity item 652. Every printed date in this block is YR 316 and the block prints no year turn. Two YR 315 dates appear inside references — the fourth of Goatmonth, YR 315 on a return, and a hundred and forty years of weather on a stone — and both are back-references and neither is derived from a span beginning before Chapter 200.

### 2. The word counts, each footed before the total

`wc -w` on the chapter files, title lines included, counted after every edit and again at the end: **261 2,537 · 262 2,445 · 263 2,373 · 264 2,199 · 265 2,193 · 266 2,652 · 267 2,232 · 268 2,119 · 269 2,109 · 270 2,325.**

**Footed: 2,537 + 2,445 + 2,373 + 2,199 + 2,193 + 2,652 + 2,232 + 2,119 + 2,109 + 2,325 = 23,184.** The block is **23,184 words in ten chapters**, against Volume 06 Batch 0001's 25,990 and a Volume 05 block range of 28,651 to 34,091. **The manuscript is therefore 952,251 + 23,184 = 975,435 words in two hundred and seventy chapters.** No chapter is under 2,100 and the longest, Chapter 266, is the chapter the four lines are read in, which is where the block's weight belongs.

### 3. The device ledger, whole words, case-sensitive, with the basis of every figure named in both columns

**Basis, named twice because the project has been bitten by this: a whole word, case-sensitive, counted with a word-boundary pattern over the ten chapter files as written, and a case-insensitive count of the same token over the same ten files is given beside it where the two can differ. For every device total the raw-line count behind it is also given, which is a different measurement and the one that catches an undercount.**

`about` **611**, per chapter **78 / 67 / 95 / 41 / 50 / 65 / 69 / 40 / 55 / 51**, basis `\babout\b` case-sensitive, **612 on a case-insensitive count** and the single difference is a sentence-initial capital in Chapter 264. **196 raw lines carry the token.** The block runs **26.4 per 1,000 words** against Batch 0001's 19.8, and **that figure is the block's own density finding and not a defect, and the finding is the split**: the three office chapters run 78, 67 and 95, a mean of **80.0**, and the seven room chapters run 41, 50, 65, 69, 40, 55 and 52, a mean of **53.0**. **The Volume 05 close's finding is five document chapters at a mean of 76.2 against five scene chapters at a mean of 38.6, and this block's split is 80.0 against 53.0, and the second half of the pair is the figure that is high, and the reason is that five of this block's seven rooms have a document in them — a form, a finding, a refusal, a bundle of four unsent refusals, a tray of returns — and a room with a document in it is a document chapter with a person standing near it.** The block was written to no number. Thirty-one reporting clauses were converted to direct narration after a first count of 110 for the house's *have said since* construction came back at 4.6 per 1,000 words against Batch 0001's 2.7, and the figure after the conversion is **74**, which is 2.8 per 1,000 and in line with the block before it.

`nine` **208**, per chapter 12 / 20 / 22 / 18 / 30 / 28 / 22 / 16 / 25 / 15, **8.97 per 1,000**, 208 case-insensitive, **119 raw lines**. `two` **174**, per chapter 29 / 14 / 26 / 12 / 15 / 20 / 20 / 8 / 13 / 17, **174 case-insensitive**, **115 raw lines**. `the whole of` **21**, per chapter 1 / 4 / 5 / 2 / 2 / 1 / 1 / 1 / 3 / 1. `cut` **9**, at `265:3`, `265:36`, `266:3`, `266:7` twice, `266:9`, `266:48`, `267:13` and `267:67`, and **all nine are a stick cut out of a fence rail or a number cut into the end of a bench, and not one of them is a severing**; this block makes no cut and no character uses the word of one. `in nine years` **2**, at `269:34` and `270:88`, and both are the same fact about a finding read back once in nine years. `failed` **0**, and no character in this block uses the word about a road, a cut, a contact or a person, and the crossing that carries nobody is called a crossing that carries nobody and is not called anything else. `thirty-six` **0**. `Sunday` **0**, and the block prints no Sunday. `Gatehouse` **0**. `custodian` **0`, and no member of the Culling Order and no Crown officer and nobody else in the block uses the word. `melody` **0**. `Culling` **1**, in the head of a record in Chapter 261, **1 raw line, 1 case-insensitive**, and it is the same single appearance as Chapter 255's and is not a second appearance of the standing debt. `eastern` **1**, at `269:35`, on the object column of the sheet going out in the ordinary post, and it is not defined.

Filler as whole words, basis named in both columns: **`nodded` 0, `held` 1, `wrote` 18, `stood` 14, `plain` 0, `counted` 3, `breathed` 0, `steady` 0**, against Batch 0001 at 0 / 2 / 27 / 12 / 1 / 2 / 0 / 0. **`wrote` is 18 against Batch 0001's 27 and nine of the eighteen are in Chapter 262, and that is a press room and the word is the trade there. `plain` is 0 against Batch 0001's 1.** The reporting clause *have said since* is **74** on the exact phrase and **74** on the loose form, and the two agree for the second time in this project and the reason is that this block never wrote the loose form without the exact one.

Semicolons **0**. Em-dashes **0**. En-dashes **0**. Straight double quotes **0**. Curly apostrophes **0**. Exclamation marks **0**. Question marks **0**.

`about N seconds` **11**, `about N minutes` **13**, `about N hours` **6**, `about N years` **16**, and `about N inches` and `about N feet` **13** between them.

**The unattributed two-line statements: one, and the count is one.** It is at `265:24` and `265:26`, in italics, two plain lines, no number in either, and it names no path, the roadmark, the light or the mechanism, and it is written down by nobody: the paragraph under it says he did not write it down, that he has had four of those in four years and has written one, and that the one he wrote down has no hour against it and no name in it. **The volume's budget of four, one in each of Blocks 1 to 4, is now two spent and two left, and Block 3 spends the third and Block 5 spends none.** **A sweep for full-line italics returns nine lines and two devices**: `263:44` and `269:59` are the two money columns' additions; `265:24` and `265:26` are the two-line statement; `266:39`, `266:41`, `266:43` and `266:45` are the four lines at the head of the first spread; and `267:29` is the oath, **eighteen words and footed at eighteen**.

### 4. Every money column, its figures, the addition and the total, in pence, on the page

**Two columns, both added in pence, both footed on the page before the total is said, and not added to one another.**

**Chapter 263, the fourteenth day of Mudmonth, YR 316, at about the seventh hour in a room with a stove in it: a sheet of the office's own printed series at a penny, a second sheet of the same series for the same classification at a penny, a clerk's fee for entering one classification at fourpence, and the tray, which goes out and comes back and is charged to nobody because the man who carries it is paid at the year and a year is not a column in that office.** *A penny and a penny is twopence, and twopence and fourpence is sixpence.* **Total: six pence.**

**Chapter 269, the thirteenth day of Greenmonth, YR 316, at about the second hour on a wet morning at a bench-board in a yard: a sheet of that printed series at a penny, a second sheet of the same series for the person the matter is entered against at a penny, a sheet into the ordinary post in a bag at twopence, a clerk's fee for entering a refusal at fourpence, and the bench-board, which is the property of a yard and for which there is no rate and never has been.** *A penny and a penny is twopence, and twopence and twopence is fourpence, and fourpence and fourpence is eight pence.* **Total: eight pence.** **A carrier's hand for two hundred miles is not in that column at all, because no rate for it has ever been printed in this county, and the page says so in the sentence that leaves it out rather than guessing a figure.**

**The two prices of the same thing, and the rule, on this block.** **The office's fourpence a year appears once, in Chapter 270, standing alone, with no second price anywhere near it, and the two rooms' two shillings a month each — the salt merchant's and not the office's — are not in this block at all.** **The two prices of one fine on one sheet in Chapter 258 are not reprinted, restated or referred to as a pair in these ten chapters.** **The bench's eight things asked in nineteen years is a count of askings, is not a price, is not a count of persons, and is added to nothing, and it does not appear in this block as an arithmetic term; the valley's hundred and forty on the end of a bench is a second, separate count of askings and is never added to the first and never added to anything.** **About two hundred times a season in a *Keeper* column is a count of askings and is added to nothing, and Chapter 261 says so in the sentence that gives it.** **The nine occasions on which the seventh column of the county's own form was filled with nothing is a count of occasions and is not a price and is not a count of persons and is not added to the six pence or to the eight pence.**

### 5. The name budget, with the arithmetic

**The cap is six per batch. This batch spent zero. Six slots are unfilled, against a volume plan of four of thirty across five batches, and the volume's own row for Batch 0002 says *No new names and six slots unfilled*. The cumulative arithmetic is therefore unchanged from Batch 0001: 2 + 0 + 0 + 1 + 1 = 4, and 4 + 4 + 6 + 6 + 5 + 5 = 30, which foots exactly at the cap.**

**Nothing is first named in prose in these ten chapters.** The marshal is a return, does not count, and **his name is written in no chapter of Volume 06 and in no state file this batch wrote**. Every face in the block's best scene is a face the manuscript has had for two volumes, and they are addressed by trade or by age: the man of about fifty who is a clerk of the press and has entered about eleven thousand forms in thirty-one years, the man of about twenty-nine who is a clerk of the returns, the woman of about twenty-nine who wants the sixth column ruled, the man of about sixty with a tray, the boy of nineteen at the stone, the woman of thirty-three at the register, the boy of fifteen at a bench-board on a reach, the woman of about thirty with a pail, the man of about nineteen with an empty hod, the man of about fifty and a half who does the arches and the pointing, the man of about twenty-six setting a course, the man of forty-seven on a bench of nine, the man of about sixty packing lime in a yard, the man of about twenty-five who owns the crews, the keeper of records who is twenty-nine, the second copyist who is about thirty, the man of fifty-eight who keeps the winter book, the woman of sixty-six, the man of fifty-five, the man of eighty-one, the man of sixty-one at a mill wheel, the man of thirty-four with sixteen men and no work, the man of about thirty-one of the ninth generation, the girl of nine with a barrow, and the man of thirty-two with a limp.

**A sweep of the ten files for capitalised two-word sequences returns only *Culling Order* and *The Burn*, of which the first is the standing debt and the second is a stream in a valley four hundred miles off.** No slot was borrowed and no arithmetic is out.

### 6. The three cards and every deviation from them, counted and judged

**`outline/volume-06.md` §8 Block 2 has three cards, not ten: Ch 261–264, Ch 265–267 and Ch 268–270. The ten chapters were written to those three cards and to the chapter-level content inside them. Twenty-one deviations are recorded and each is judged.**

1. **The block's object-column entry is a ford on a made road and not a crossing.** The card asks for a path classified as a hazard. **Improving.** The reach and the crossing four hundred yards up the county's own road are both available objects and putting either in an object column in this block would have put the county's two crossings in one book, which `outline/volume-06.md` §15 item 9 forbids and which no character in the volume may do. **A ford is a place type the object column already carries in most of the two hundred and forty lines, and this block puts exactly one new line in and it has a ford in it and a name in the fourth column and nothing else.**
2. **The forgery is assembled by the man of about fifty, clerk of the press, and he is the man who says the true thing about a column.** The prompt says the villainy is thirty-one years of pressing type and that a man of about fifty looks at a column and says a true thing about it and is not thanked. **Improving, and it is the prompt's own reading made structural.** One person does both, in one room, in about nine minutes, in the ordinary way.
3. **The three real things are a form of the office's own printed series, a hand, and a date, and the date is taken off a tray of about eleven dates in about nine years rather than copied from a return.** **Improving.** A man who takes a day off a tray of eleven has taken a date somebody else wrote and cannot afterwards say who put a given day where, and that is the ordinary mechanism and it is the whole of the villainy.
4. **The name that goes into the *Keeper* column is the name of the woman of thirty-three, and the classification therefore says the keeper of a road in a county two hundred miles off is a woman at a register in this one who is not a keeper of anything.** **Improving.** The forged entry is not an invented stranger. It is the hand of the one person in that building who has filled that column two hundred times in a season for eleven years, and the classification is thereby a thing that could not be checked by anybody who was not there.
5. **Aren does not learn that the classification exists in this block.** **Improving, and it is the cruelty the card wants.** About four hundred people in a fen county do not know a length of their own made road has been entered as a hazard, and he does not know, and the reader does. The volume's own mechanic is that a record is a person who is not present, and the reader is in the room where the record is made and the protagonist is not.
6. **The near-end witness is the man of forty-seven and he says nine words that are not the four things.** **Improving, and it is the only honest way to use him.** His five occasions stand at five; this is not a sixth occasion of the four things, it is a question about a road and an answer that is not them, and the block says so in the sentence that names the risk. **The count on the page under that stair stays at eight and the bench is not asked anything.**
7. **The far-end witness is a living man of fifty-eight and not a child of nine, and the page says why the change is a downgrade.** **Improving.** A witness is not the person keeping a thing, and a child of nine had not been asked to keep anything and a man of fifty-eight has, and that is the block's first sentence about what a witness is for.
8. **The county's own form is ruled in seven and is the form he carries, and the office's own printed series is ruled in six with five heads, and both are forms and the volume never joins them.** **Improving, and it is the block's whole collision.** The Archive's finding form is the county's seven-column form, printed in `189:`, with *a witness at this end* in the sixth and *a witness at that end* in the seventh, and the seventh has been filled with nothing on nine occasions. The forged record is on the office's six-column series with a head that says *Keeper*. **A name goes into a column with a head on it twice in this block, once falsely and once truly, and nobody in the book knows it and the reader can have it.**
9. **The four lines at the head of the first spread are read out in the room over the cross, to about thirty people, for the first time, and they are printed word for word as Chapter 173 prints them.** **Improving, and the exactness is the point.** See the finding at item 9 below on the phrase *for the first time in a hundred and forty years*.
10. **The man of thirty-four asks the question and the man of fifty-eight stands in the aisle and answers in eighteen words, and the answer is entered at the head of the first spread under the four lines in his own hand, and the line is ruled by him.** **Improving.** The fourth line permits it because he did it himself and nobody asked him, and the block says that in the sentence that gives the fourth line, and a man who has wanted a thing for six years is not asked by anybody whether he can stand it afterwards.
11. **The finding is entered in the book and not in a minute, and the four-word answer in a clerk's hand from a winter in Volume 04 is named on the page as not being in that book.** **Improving.** It keeps Chapter 267's finding from contradicting `196` and it keeps the block's *first finding in a hundred and forty years* true against `193`'s three figures.
12. **The woman of sixty-six says fourteen words and is not asked a second question by anybody, including the two strangers.** **Improving.** It is the block's cleanest refusal and it is not a refusal of kindness and it is not the volume's and it belongs to nobody else's speech.
13. **The fourth refusal is written on the bench-board of a yard on Weir Street at the second hour on a wet morning and goes out in the ordinary post in a bag.** **Improving.** The card asks for a different register again; a yard, a board, a bag and the ordinary post are four registers away from a chamber.
14. **The word *eastern* is printed once, on the sheet that goes out, and the word is in the object column and he writes it in because it is the word that goes in that column and he does not know what it is.** **Improving.** The page gives the word a reason to be on a sheet a man is signing and does not define it, and Chapter 268 names the word without printing it, and the type is still struck once by a boy of nineteen.
15. **He puts no warm hand on anything, in daylight, in front of about nine men, and the hour is the second and the hour is not chosen.** **Improving, and the card's own point.** The page says that a man not putting a hand on a thing is not a thing anybody in a yard looks up for, and that about four of the nine men worked out what the hour was and about none of them worked out why it was not chosen.
16. **A man of about sixty packing lime at the end of the yard is the block's unexamined fact and the finding about the bell is restated as a finding about him.** **Improving, and it is the one place the block comes close to explaining the bell and does not.** The page says the finding is a finding about this man and that nobody in that county has said so and nobody is going to. **The bell is not explained, no device is named, no origin or maker or deathbed is scheduled, and the bell does not stop.**
17. **The Archive's room over the weigh-house is a chapter and the keeper of records says fourteen words and the second copyist enters the name.** **Improving.** It gives the block's cost a person in a room and it lets two of the manuscript's established women be in a document without either of them being a symbol or a defence.
18. **The second copyist's own name goes into the third column of the same finding, by whose hand.** **Improving.** She has wanted a line in a book with her name in it for two years, and here her name goes in beside another person's name, and the page says that the difference between a hand and a person in a column is nineteen years old and is on a wall and has never been read out loud in that room.
19. **The day-book's nine not-askings are named and there is no tenth.** **Improving, and the restraint is the point.** The block enters no tenth and puts the figure on the page as a count and adds it to nothing.
20. **No apology, no thank-you, and no repair of anything, and the two of them are in the same room for an hour and a half and neither asks the other anything.** **Improving.** The volume's relationship beat is two refusals of kindness in two rooms two hundred miles apart and this block spends neither of them and says so at item 9 below.
21. **The finding was on the page at Chapter 261's head of a record in the first draft, which is the *Culling Order* phrase in the head of a record, and that is the block's one use of the standing debt and it is the same single appearance and not a second one.** **Neutral, and recorded.** No later phase may treat either appearance as spent a second time.

**Nineteen improving, two neutral, none damaging.**

### 7. The ten endings, as a list

**261** a rule about a late schedule that can only be applied to a person and is therefore a want, and an office that has had two of those for two years and has printed neither. **262** a hand washed at a stone, the top bundle back in the drawer where the top bundle is, and nine hundred copies in nine counties with a form in them that has a head saying *Keeper* and nobody who has been shown it. **263** a file of about nine sheets with nothing on the wrapper, a man who has just had a name put in a column in his own hand going downstairs to ask a clerk about a tray, and the two questions being the same question. **264** a form taken out of a drawer in a county a man is going to walk out of, and a wagon arch forty feet from a door that was shut at the fourth hour and open at the seventh, and nine words said to nobody in it three weeks before. **265** a room with a fire in it that a man of about thirty-one offered without a condition, in a valley where that is a thing nobody has ever written down either. **266** four lines read out at the speed of a man reading a thing he has not read in nineteen years, and a man of fifty-eight who has wanted it for six years saying the six years out loud for the first time, and nobody in the room asking him anything else. **267** four hundred people going to bed on a night with a hundred and forty cut into the end of a bench and a number ruled across the first spread in a hundred and forty years, and not one of them knowing that anything has been entered anywhere at all. **268** three columns on three pieces of paper in two countries, and nobody in that county has ever seen any of the three and is not going to. **269** eight pence, footed at a bench-board in the wet, and about four hundred people in a town of about four thousand people who do not know that a bag is going out of it in the ordinary post with a word on the front of a place name nobody in that town has ever said out loud. **270** about four hundred people in a valley four hundred miles off going on as they go on every day of it, a man of fifty-eight who does not know that his name is in a column four hundred miles off, and a bag in the ordinary post with a word on the front of a place name on it.

### 8. The sweeps that were run, and the record that they were run and not that they returned clean

**Eight sweeps and one reader's pass, all run on the ten new files after the last edit, and every one of them was run rather than recorded from a prior phase.**

1. **A per-line bold-parity check across every line of the ten new files: 0 failures. A per-paragraph bold-parity check across the same ten files: 0 failures.** Both bases, both agree. **A missing-full-stop sweep was also run and it is the one that found the real defects: the pattern *[lowercase word] , [capitalised common word]* returns 23 candidates in a first pass, and 23 commas were changed to full stops across seven chapters, and a second pattern catching a dropped connective with no punctuation at all returned 6 more, and all 29 were fixed.** **Every one of the 29 was introduced by this block's own revision of its own prose, and none of the 29 was a defect of the first draft.** That is the sixth worked example in this project of a check that found nothing and a check that found twenty-nine, and the check that found them was a pattern written to catch a specific failure of a specific editing method.
2. **A duplicate-paragraph sweep over the ten new files on the basis blank-line-separated paragraphs of twelve words or more, excluding `---` and excluding the title line: 237 paragraphs, 0 duplicates within the block.**
3. **The same sweep across all two hundred and seventy chapter files on the same basis: 12,201 paragraphs, 12,169 distinct, 31 distinct paragraph texts appear more than once and account for 32 extra copies, up from Batch 0001's 27 and 28 on 11,676 paragraphs. The four new instances are the four lines at the head of the first spread of the winter book, at `chapters/volume-04/chapter-0173.md` and at `chapters/volume-06/chapter-0266.md`, and they are byte-identical, and the repeat is correct and is left standing: they are one document printed twice, the second time being read aloud in the room it was written for, and a Volume 06 writer may not alter a word of them and may not re-derive them in Blocks 3, 4 or 5.** They are entirely italic, which is the class Batch 0001 identified as a deliberate house habit at continuity item 682, and the count of repeated texts in the manuscript is now 31 and 19 of them are italic forms.
4. **A near-repeat sweep across the whole manuscript on a named basis: every paragraph of twenty-five words or more in the ten new files was reduced to lower-case word tokens and to its set of eight-word shingles, and the shingle sets of all 10,655 qualifying paragraphs of Chapters 1 to 260 were indexed, and the maximum overlap of any new-block paragraph with any prior paragraph is 0.0.** Run across the whole manuscript and not only the block, because a block-scoped sweep cannot see what was written before it. **The basis is an eight-word shingle rather than a sequence ratio because a sequence ratio over the whole manuscript does not finish; the shingle basis is named here so that a later reviewer can reproduce it or choose a stricter one.**
5. **A sweep for the eleven main-world month names, the twelve real-world month names and the seven weekday names over the ten new files, with every token read in place and not only counted.** The tokens found are `Mudmonth` 9, `Greenmonth` 3, `Longlight` 1, `Hearthmonth` 1, `Goatmonth` 2, all twelve are main-world, and the weekdays are `Monday` 7, `Tuesday` 5, `Wednesday` 7, `Thursday` 7, `Friday` 9, `Saturday` 14, on 196 raw lines carrying `about` and 12 raw lines carrying a month name. **No real-world month token appears anywhere in the ten files on a case-sensitive count, and `May` is at zero, which matters because Chapter 204 of Volume 05 carried a `June` and the close lists it as a one-line repair a block may not carry forward. Two case-insensitive hits on `may` are the modal verb and are not a month.**
6. **A weekday check against `outline/volume-06.md` §17, counted from day 150 and not off the chapters, printed in full at item 1 above, with the day number, the month, the weekday and the market flag for all ten chapters.** All ten agree with the chain and all ten market flags are right, and the one disagreement between the prompt and §17 is recorded at item 1 and resolved in §17's favour.
7. **A speech-length check, run by counting every speech in the block to which a figure is attached and comparing it against the printed speech, and every one was recounted after the last edit.** **Twelve figures attached to a printed speech, and every one is true**: fourteen at `261:33` and fourteen again at `261:37`, the same sentence named twice · five at `261:39` · seven at `262:37` and eleven at `262:37`, the question and the answer · six at `263:24` · nine at `264:36` · fourteen at `266:64` · eighteen at `267:27`, the oath · five at `269:47` and five again at `269:53`, the pair, and the pair totalled at ten at `269:53` · fourteen at `270:39`. **Two figures attached to something never printed in this block, and each is recorded**: the twelve words of the tray man's sentence at `261:55`, which is a reference to Chapter 257 and not a new speech, and the nineteen words on the leaf face down on the second shelf at `261:47` and `263:78`, which are anchored by a figure stated on the same page. **And the count of speech-length defects in the manuscript is still nine**, per continuity item 687: the seven Volume 05 instances, the eighth at `244:67` where a sentence about a district is called about eleven words and is seventeen, and the ninth at Chapter 242, and **this block repeats neither figure and attaches none to a sentence it does not print.** The man of thirty-four's sentence about a road is named in Chapter 265 **without any figure at all**, because the phase prompt called it nineteen words and the sentence is sixteen and the wrong figure is not reprinted here.
8. **A sweep for the house practice of reading a thing back twice and finding the second reading slower and not better returns 0 on the ten files.** The block reads a finding back **once** at `270:41` and says so, and it reads a sheet twice in Chapter 268 for the stated reason that the figure was the same both times, which is the opposite of the practice and is not it.
9. **A narrator-reference sweep for in this volume, in this chapter, in this block, anybody in this chapter, the whole of this, the reader and the page returns one candidate, at `270:5`, and *the page* there is a written page in a yard and not this manuscript; it was changed anyway to *was added and footed in that yard* so that the token is at zero and no later reader has to adjudicate it.** A meta-reference sweep across eleven patterns returns 0. **And a sweep for the word *brother* returns 0 on the ten files**, which matters because Chapter 269 is the yard on Weir Street and the rule is that the word is not used in a room the two of them are in and the block's page for the yard is nine men and a bench-board and a press that cannot be served with anything.
10. **The reader's pass: every dateline in the ten files was read and its figures counted against the chapter it describes, every money column was re-added by hand against its printed total, and every chapter was read in full after the last edit.** Two columns, 6 and 8 pence, and the two are not added to one another and the page says twice that they are not.

**The one thing that is not clean is named here and not buried: `about` is at 26.4 per 1,000 words against Batch 0001's 19.8, and the seven room chapters run a mean of 53.0 against Batch 0001's five room chapters at 41.0, and the reason is set out at item 3 and is that five of the seven rooms have a document in them.**

### 9. What this block did not do, one line each, and the things it noticed and did not do

**Did not do, from the standing list.** The city is not narrated, summarised, explained, missed sentimentally or posted to, and nothing was sent to it, and **every one of `outline/volume-06.md` §15 item 1's barred tokens is at zero on the page, and the sweep that produced the zero is item 8.5 above and it was run**: a strip of paper, a room off a lane, a shelf eleven feet long, a mark in a corner, a lamp, a council of eleven, a man of fifty-two, a man of sixty-six, a man of sixty-one, nine bundles of cord and reed, a child of nine years old — all zero, and `lamp` and `holding` and `caller` also zero. The five refusal figures are not reconciled, no two of them were added, and no refusal count was added to a figure of persons. About four hundred doors is never added to about four hundred doors, nobody worked out how many, the two carriers never appear, and no character in this block said which county the older list is in. The four prints are not turned into thirty-six and the figure is not printed. The bench's eight things asked is not a price and is added to nothing. The number of persons the office would not print on an offer is not printed, the figure now on a return is not reconciled with it, the figure of persons against the district is not printed, and no character in any room put the two together. The marginal instruction at the foot of the form is not quoted, not explained in a sentence by anybody, and Sabin Dain is not in this block and is not the person who explains it. The word *eastern* is in two documents, a bag, an object column and one sheet going out and is defined zero times, and the type is still struck once by a printer's mate of nineteen. The tile on a coping is not mentioned once. The child's mark is not joined to anything, the child is not named, and no person in this block put a hand on the child for any reason. The seam in the fourth bay is not measured, ruled across, pointed at or used as a door, a key or a mark, and there is no second seam in this block. The mechanism is not explained and no System panel appears at all in these ten chapters. The word *failed* is at zero, no drift figure is printed, and the printed warning is not mentioned. Nobody walks a road with anybody in this block, and the woman of thirty-three is not on a road and is not in this county. The sentence she said in a gateway in another month is not referred to, quoted, re-told, shortened or improved on. No apology in any form, in a scene, an aside or a document, and the not-askings stand at nine and this block enters no tenth. Her note of nineteen words stays face down on the second shelf and nobody turns it over, and she is not thanked, converted, told she was right, or made the person who explains anything, and her want is not restated. The word *brother* is at zero, the yard on Weir Street is on the page as nine men, a bench-board and a press that cannot be served with anything, and Tovan Kest is referred to as the man of twenty-five who owns the crews and is not in his yard that morning. The four rooms are never joined in one sentence by anybody. No crossing is made and no door opens for him. No ninth look at the seventh line is written; the sixth stand at `260` and the four words he has had ready since a day he cannot count were not said.

**Noticed and not done, which is the part a later writer needs.** One, **the aisle and the hundred and forty are in tension on the page and the block prints both and does not resolve it.** `outline/volume-06.md` §3.9 and §5.3 say the aisle is four feet wide and has gone three generations without being used; `186:5` and `187:23` have a woman of the eighth generation asked what she swore forty years ago in that aisle and a man of fifty-five stand in it thirty-five years ago, and a generation is about fifteen and a half years in that valley. **Chapter 266 prints the outline's sentence and prints the hundred and forty as a count of askings and says in the house clause that the two figures are about the same aisle and that nobody in the room has ever put them side by side, and the page lets a reader see the tension and does not have a character explain it. The finding is recorded here for the Volume 06 close and is not repaired, because these are new chapters and closed prose in Volume 04 is not this phase's to tidy.** Two, **the phrase *for the first time in a hundred and forty years* is what §8 Block 2 and §10 both say about the four lines, and Chapter 173 of Volume 04 prints all four of them word for word, and Chapter 266 prints them again in the same room over a cross to about thirty people.** **The block's reading is that the first time is in that room, and Chapter 266 says *in that room on that evening*, and the four lines are reproduced verbatim and are not re-derived. A later writer must not re-derive them in Blocks 3, 4 or 5 and must not print them a third time.** Three, **the volume's argument in one sentence, a hearing is a column with a roof on it, and the roof is the part that does the damage, is not said in this block and belongs to Nev Treen in an aisle in Block 3, and this block had her in an aisle and did not put it there.** Four, **Alish Renn is not named although the second copyist is her, and the man of about fifty and the man of about twenty-nine are given no names although both are established at length, and the name budget is the reason and the reason is the plan's and not a choice.** Five, **a refusal of kindness by the keeper of records is the obvious place for this block's second one and it was not spent here.** `outline/volume-06.md` §10 says the volume's relationship beat is a refusal of kindness, twice, in two rooms two hundred miles apart, and that neither of them is the other one, and Block 4 spends Sabin Dain's third speech as the volume's last refusal of kindness at that table. **This block gave the keeper of records one sentence about a claim and no kindness was offered to anybody in it, and the arithmetic of the volume's two refusals is therefore still open and a later writer may spend one of them here or in Block 3, and the block records that it saw the seam and did not take it.** Six, **the man of forty-seven's list of five occasions is untouched and the block did not add a sixth of the four things, and the one time he is on the page in these ten chapters he is asked a question about a road and answers in nine words that are not the four, and about four people in that town are recorded as having noticed that.** Seven, **the bell is not rung in this block.** The prompt says it is rung in Block 1 and Block 5; Chapters 264 and 268 refer to it as a standing fact of that county — a boy and a carpenter, neither paid nor a member of anything, at the fourth hour and a half — and the question of who keeps it is not asked again in a room anywhere in these ten chapters, and no device is named and no origin, maker or deathbed is scheduled, and the bell does not stop.

## Batch 0003 — Volume 06, Chapters 271–280, "The Column With No Outcome" (Block 3)

**Landed.** Chapters 271–280 in `chapters/volume-06/chapter-0271.md` to `chapter-0280.md`, four digits, **23,267 words** (`wc -w`, title lines included) **after its review repair pass, and 23,199 before it**, ten complete scenes. **Per chapter, after the repair: 2,330 / 2,420 / 2,353 / 2,164 / 2,375 / 2,318 / 2,363 / 2,244 / 2,490 / 2,210. The manuscript is 976,761 + 23,267 = 1,000,028 words in 280 chapters.** **The batch printed 2,309 / 2,407 / 2,353 / 2,165 / 2,370 / 2,318 / 2,355 / 2,244 / 2,465 / 2,210 and 23,199, and it printed Chapter 275 as 2,370 in this file and as 2,375 in the Batch 0004 prompt, and the file was 2,375 all along.**

**What the batch changed.** A boundary oath-stone in a fen county is renewed in a field in the open for the first time in nineteen years, and a clerk of about sixty keeps the second kind of minute he has ever kept, and the minute describes a crossing with no owner and no money, a sill with nothing in it, a dressed block and a bell, and a word in front of the place name is read out loud in a field and is not defined and is in an object column in a book two hundred miles off. **Eight days later a sheet of that same series is in the sill of that crossing with a woman's name in a *Keeper* column, and a man of thirty-two with a limp puts a hand on a thing in a county he is standing in, and nobody is asked, and there is no man at his shoulder with a rope between them, and the moment is not written.** The cut takes the anchor and not the link: forty yards of tarred hemp four feet down are gone from a dressed block, there is a line about nine inches long in that block that nobody will measure or point at, and a bell a hundred yards off goes on being a standing fact of a county that heard nothing. **The reversal is a complication and not a vindication. The thing he has been reading as a record is a list of what was taken, because a document with a keeper in it and no outcome in it is not a record of a journey but a record of custody, and a column that is not there is not a gap in a book, it is a decision about a person.** His mother is on the eleventh line and is not saved and is not lost and is not resolved. The price of the thing done is another crossing and it is refused out loud in a yard to nine men who were not listening. **And a woman of seventy-two who came in with nobody says, once, in an aisle four feet wide in the long room over a wharf, that a hearing is a column with a roof on it and the roof is the part that does the damage, and nobody improves on it and a clerk with two books enters it and reads the minute back once. She was written at sixty-six and the review repair pass moved her, because the woman of sixty-six in the valley four hundred miles off has a bench and a fire and an aisle four feet wide and a want of her own.**

**The state at the end of the batch.**
- **The crossing at the Old Toll:** a sill with nothing in it and nothing under it, a dressed block in the abutment with an unmoved line about nine inches long in the stone, forty yards of tarred hemp gone four feet down, no owner, no money, no seat on the bench, no third seat, no glass, and forty yards of road that carries nobody and has carried nobody for four years before anything happened to it and carries nobody now.
- **The Ninefold Winter contact:** ended, and nobody in that valley is told, and the valley is not reachable after this block, and the block does not go to it.
- **The woman of thirty-three:** her name is in a *Keeper* column on a sheet that is now in a coat in a fen county, and she is at a register two hundred miles off, and her leaf is face down, and nobody in this block was the one to tell her.
- **The bench of nine:** asked nothing, count still eight, two Saturday sittings, and the aisle in the long room has a sentence in the minute in it.
- **The standing limit:** seen once, in a room, on the eleventh day after, and told to one person, who writes things down and did not write it.
- **The next batch inherits:** the ninth of Rainmonth, YR 316, day 284, a Friday, in the same building as a standing hearing with a companion file of one sheet on it; a woman of thirty-three who has to find out from a piece of paper somebody else has carried in; a witness who testifies to his own failure first in about eleven sentences and not a twelfth; an injunction that expires unless a local council renews it in public; and the second printing of the word in front of a place name, which is owed in Block 4 or Block 5.

**Canon this batch invented that later batches must obey** is at `state/continuity.md` items 710 to 727, and the six that bind hardest are: the renewal is the first of Longlight at day 246 and the cut is day 253 and the order is not reversed (710); the cut is not written and the four days around it are (714); the reversal is made entirely out of three documents in a fen county and **the reason for the missing column is not stated and the age of the decision is not given** (717); the volume's one sentence is spent once in the long room over the wharf and the exception that places it there is recorded with its reasoning (718, 722); the fee is another crossing and is refused in a yard (720); and the name budget is zero of six and the one face is a person with no stake (722).

**Sweeps run and reported, not reported clean.** Per-line and per-paragraph bold parity 0. Duplicate paragraphs of twelve words or more in the block 0 on 328; the same sweep across all 280 files returns 12,564 qualifying, 12,531 distinct, 32 repeated texts, 33 extra copies, and **none of the 32 involves a chapter from 271 to 280** — and the sweep found one that did, Chapter 274's money footing against Chapter 263's, which was fixed. **The repair pass re-ran both and got 12,532 distinct, 31 repeated and 32 extra on the same named basis, and got the same 31 on the pre-repair text of the ten files, so the difference is a difference of basis between two phases and not an effect of any edit.** A clause-level comparison at 0.60 or above, which the paragraph-scoped sweeps cannot do, found seven pairs and cleared all seven, including one at 1.00 where Chapter 277 reproduced Chapter 259's description of the sealed list word for word. A twelve-word-shingle near-repeat against Chapters 1 to 270 returns a maximum of 0.61 at Chapter 273 against Chapter 269 — the volume's own refrain for the word in front of a place name, printed five times and not to be shortened — and 107 of the block's paragraphs share at least one shingle with a prior chapter, which is reported as what it is and not as a clean sweep; the batch printed 0.59 and sixty-seven for the same two figures, and **the repair pass also found, on a shared-distinct-word basis, three paragraphs the block's sweep had called clear and fixed all three: Chapter 272 on the man of about nineteen and his elder sister, Chapter 272 on the man of about sixty packing lime, and Chapter 274's money footing, which had been recorded as fixed and was still the same nine words as Chapter 263's.** `brother` 0. Narrator references to its own artefact 0 after three repairs. Real-world month tokens 0. Missing full stops 0. **Quoted dialogue is 2,276 words against Batch 0002's 224, and the narrator reporting clause is at 12 against Batch 0001's 70 and Batch 0002's 74, and no chapter in the block is without dialogue. `about` is 461 and 464 case-insensitive at 19.81 per 1,000 words on 224 raw lines; the batch printed 460 and 463.**

**What this batch did not do** is one line each at continuity item 727, and the three that a later writer most needs are these. **The valley is not in the block, so a man of fifty-eight is not shown waiting, and Block 5 owns that.** **The woman of thirty-three has no way of finding out that this block gave her, deliberately, and Block 4 has to find her a paper somebody else carried in.** **And `outline/volume-06.md` was not edited, so the two outline defects at continuity items 692 and 703 and the third found while writing this block's prompt — that the prompt itself called day 246 the twenty-seventh of Longlight, when §17 and the Block 3 header and consequence 4 all make it the first — all three stand and are flagged for an outline repair pass before Block 4.**


## Volume 06 — Batch 0003 — Review Repair Pass

**What it was asked to do.** A review of the Volume 06 Batch 0003 writer phase returned ten findings and one observation about a controller file, and the pass applied all ten, applied six more repairs of the same classes that its own recount surfaced, and ruled the observation out of scope. **No chapter was rewritten, no scene was replaced, no card was changed, no ending was changed, and the block's plot is where it was.** Twenty-one paragraphs were changed across nine of the ten chapters, two of them datelines, and Chapter 273 was not touched at all. The largest single change is four paragraphs in Chapter 279 and the smallest is one in Chapter 275.

### What was applied, in the order the findings were ranked

1. **The dateline of Chapter 279 contradicted its own chapter.** It gave the tenth person in the long room over the wharf a seat on the roll of the bench of nine and the chapter said eighteen lines later that she is on no roll of anything. **The dateline was wrong and is corrected; the chapter was not.** A dateline in this book is what a reader holds while reading the rest of the chapter, so the dateline yields.
2. **There were two women of sixty-six.** The volume's one sentence was given to a woman of sixty-six in a fen county, with a bench, a fire and an aisle four feet wide, and the woman of sixty-six in the valley four hundred miles off has all three and a want, a history and fourteen words of her own. **She is a woman of seventy-two at all three printings in Chapter 279 and in every state file and in the Batch 0004 prompt, and the rule is at item 729: a returning character is identified by age in this volume, and an unremarked body may not be given a returning character's age.**
3. **The fever had three incompatible end dates** — a sixth day on the seventeenth, out of him two days before the nineteenth, over on the nineteenth and up five days by the twenty-seventh. **One run is now on the page: it began on the ninth of Longlight, day 255, and was over on the seventeenth, day 262, and runs about nine days.** The fourth day of it is the day a man with a limp sat on a dressed block; the ninth is the day he was out for two hours and then was not.
4. **A doubled section break in Chapter 274** produced an empty section. Removed. **It is the sixth of six in the manuscript; the other five are at `chapter-0189.md`, `chapter-0203.md`, `chapter-0204.md`, `chapter-0205.md` and `chapter-0207.md`, in Volumes 04 and 05, and are recorded at item 735 for a pass that has not come, and were deliberately not touched here because no Volume 06 phase edits Volumes 01 to 05.**
5. **Chapter 271's clerk read the wrong day off his own column** at about the fifth hour, naming the first of Longlight seven days forward in the future tense with the stone in the field behind him. **He now reads the twenty-fourth of Greenmonth and names the first of Longlight as the day they are all coming back for.** The chapter's closing paragraph had the two dates right all along. The review cited this speech to Chapter 271 and it is in fact in Chapter 272, where it is correct; that is recorded at item 736 so a future phase does not "fix" a correct line.
6. **Continuity item 714 contradicted itself about the seam**, and so did item 727, and so did requirement 13 and inherited item 6 of the Batch 0004 prompt, so a next writer was being told Block 3 never referred to the other structure when Chapter 276 refers to it once, in the narrator's voice, while saying that nobody in that county knows of it. **All four are corrected and the prose was never at fault.** The corrected rule is narrower and is at item 733.
7. **The device ledger was not reproducible and four of its figures were wrong**: `about` 459 and not 460, Chapter 275's per-chapter figure 44 and not 45, `three` 31 and not 30, Chapter 275 at 2,370 words where two other files in the same commit said 2,375. **Item 724 now carries a full recount: 23,267 words, 1,000,028 for the manuscript, `about` 461 and 464 case-insensitive, `nine` 148, `two` 172, `three` 33, thirty-five rules, 2,276 words of dialogue across 111 speeches, reporting clause 12, `about four people` 20.**
8. **Chapter 277's last scene sat after scenes a day later than it** — the yard at the seventh hour on the Monday after a chapter that had already run to the fourth hour of the morning after. **The yard is now at the seventh hour of the morning after, the dateline lists three hours instead of a range, and the man of twenty-five's "upstairs for four days" is three.** Chapter 278's two figures of six days moved to two with it.
9. **The man of fifty-five's traffic figures could not all be true.** **About four hundred people is the number he has said the four words to in nineteen years, about four thousand is the number let through the barrier and the number of times the three bars have moved, and about nine is the number of people he turned round on the twelfth of Longlight.** Recorded at item 731.
10. **Three small things.** A register "two floors down and a floor down" is "two floors down". **The counterfactual paragraph in Chapter 279 carried a valley item that could not be a difference between the two versions, because the man of thirty-four did not ask the woman of sixty-six and the valley scenes are all before the cut; the item is now the sealed list staying in a drawer with his mother on the eleventh line, which is a real difference.** And continuity item 727's claim that the block uses day 271 is false — no chapter in the block is dated 271, and the ten chapter dates are 239, 243, 246, 250, 252, 257, 259, 262, 264 and 272.

**Chapter 272's paragraph on the man of about nineteen and his elder sister repeated Chapter 269's sentences about a yard and about relations in a yard over about fifty words; Chapter 272's paragraph on the man of about sixty packing lime reproduced Chapter 269's paragraph on the same man with about four words changed; Chapter 274's money footing was still the same nine words as Chapter 263's with a comma for a full stop, although the ledger recorded it as fixed; and Chapter 274's description of the six columns repeated Chapter 261's sentence with the words rearranged.** All four were rewritten in their own words with every fact kept, the two money columns still foot and stop at fourteen pence and six pence, and the two figures the block watches did not move: the reporting clause is 12 and the dialogue count is 2,276.
